mr president, as a member of the committee on the lisbon strategy and as the alde group coordinator in the committee on industry, research and energy, i have three wishes at the start of the new year. first, with regard to research and innovation: put pressure on developing the technical platforms to provide european industry with the opportunities that they can have with this. second, use the lisbon agenda to combine economic, social and environmental development and start to create a truly european green industrial policy, because that is one way of competing with the americans. third, with regard to energy: develop a new strategy on production, distribution and consumption of energy. in the negotiations with russia, we have to build on reciprocity, which is lacking. the rule of law, discussion and transparency are also lacking. finally, the baltic gas pipeline has to be judged according to swedish environmental rules. furthermore, it is totally unacceptable to have russian forces defending a pipeline through the territories of member states.
mr president, the biggest threat to european welfare societies is unemployment. we cannot fight unemployment with more regulations and more legislation, because the problem of unemployment today is not the lack of social regulations in europe or the lack of legislation. the problem is that we have a lack of employers. the problem is that we have a lack of competitiveness, investments and innovations. frau bundeskanzlerin, bitte entschuldigen sie mein deutsch. aber das problem in europa ist nicht nur, dass wir arbeitslosigkeit haben, sondern auch „unternehmens-“ und „unternehmerlosigkeit“. ich weiß, dass diese wörter in der deutschen sprache nicht existieren.... and not in swedish or english, either – and maybe that is the problem. i would urge you to launch an initiative for deregulation and better regulation. not all deregulation is better regulation, but we can achieve better regulation by deregulation. that is the way to fight unemployment, mr schulz, not by more regulation. i wish you all good luck.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur for a very good and comprehensive report. i especially like the way you bring up the security companies and the emerging lego nectar industry. if you, the people in the stands, were to read the eu arms export regulations, you would find that they are fantastic. these rules require respect for human rights and international agreements. weapons must not be exported to countries where social development is threatened. arms exports are only allowed to countries where stability prevails. but what is reality? the annual report on arms exports is hypocrisy. fourteen eu countries export arms to israel, 12 to indonesia, and the rest to the middle east. are there examples of countries enjoying peace, stability and security? five countries are exporting arms to saudi arabia. is this a country with human rights? saudi arabia, a country where women have not the slightest opportunity for political influence! criterion 8 of the code of conduct states that arms exports must not undermine the social and economic development of the recipient country. but half of africa is on this list of countries to which we export weapons! it is time we started applying this code of conduct to arms exports. i support amendments nos 3 and 4. lisbon and development cannot mean more arms production. we shall not have an arms export agency when it leads to these results!
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. more and more citizens are driving on foreign roads in europe. these are professional drivers, bus drivers, families on european holidays and border commuters. why would these citizens tolerate poor road safety in any of the countries they drive in? some 40 000 people die each year on eu roads. this is a tragedy that is constantly happening. families are affected. it's also a gigantic waste of resources. imagine the health care we could get if we halved the number of people dying on the roads! the problem is that in practice the countries of europe value road safety very differently and the differences between the member states with low road safety and those with high road safety continue to increase. but i do not think that the citizens of europe will accept this in the long run. we citizens will demand safe roads throughout europe. we will demand that the police stop drunk driving, regardless of nationality. we will demand speed limits throughout europe and that the major cities have public transport so that we can get around without cars. we will require the ability to travel safely by bicycle in urban areas. the european parliament must respond to these citizens' demands and put pressure on the european commission and the member states. but the member states are happy to act on a national basis. you talk about the principle of proximity, which unfortunately sometimes involves paralysis. road safety must be seen as a shared responsibility between the member states and the eu. we are now seeing a decline in the number of road deaths in europe, but unfortunately it is too slow. urgent action is now needed if we are to meet the target of halving the number of deaths by 2010. member states must ensure that existing legislation is complied with and that penalties are imposed, even if the rules of another eu country are breached. the main issues are safety belt legislation, speed limits and the banning of alcohol and drugs in traffic. this would directly reduce the number of road deaths drastically. it is important to involve the new member states in road safety work. the commission should promote so-called twinning projects between new and old member states and ensure that the new member states participate in commission expert groups. sharing good examples is vital. mr president, i would like to thank you. alcohol on the roads is a problem in the eu, causing up to 10 000 deaths a year. the number of checks varies between countries. we therefore need a common eu-wide alcohol limit of 0.5 per mille, with the possibility of lowering it. but we cannot have a limit of 0.0 per million, which the majority of the committee has decided on. such a limit is measurable and would therefore not be maintainable. that point must therefore be removed from the report. we also need to improve driver training and should therefore certify driving instructors in the eu. it is also important to continue the ecall system so that we can have quick calls to the accident scene. more countries need to make declarations of intent on this. the transport industry must also act, and here the car rental companies have a major role to play, as they buy new cars every year. if they only bought safe cars, it would improve the eu's transport fleet substantially. the transport industry has the greatest potential to influence the vehicles. if they design safe vehicles and make safety equipment standard, it will be cheaper for consumers and it will save lives. an example is alcohol locks that prevent drunk drivers from driving. these locks have dropped in price significantly since the market started to pick up. we should start to see the vehicle as a mobile workplace when employees are driving for work and then the occupational health and safety legislation should also apply in the vehicle. it would improve road safety. we must therefore take a holistic view of road safety in europe. it is about making it easier for road users to drive safely, but also about training drivers. all stakeholders must take responsibility, a shared responsibility, if we are to succeed in achieving our objectives. this is my last debate in the european parliament. i will leave office on 1 february and i would like to thank all my colleagues for the very good cooperation they have shown over the years and also with this report. i would also like to thank all the staff of the european parliament who have always made our work easier. i also thank all the others, none mentioned and none forgotten, with whom i have worked during my years in parliament.
for the gue/ngl group. thank you, madam president! my warmest congratulations on your new assignment as vice president! i'm very happy for you! the report in question rightly notes that there is still an imbalance between women and men in all areas of society. the evaluation of how the committees have so far lived up to parliament's decision that the gender perspective should always permeate our work shows, not surprisingly, that there is still a tremendous amount of work to be done before we can say that the gender perspective is truly integrated into our work. this is not news. many of us experience the lack of equality and equal treatment between men and women in our work on a daily basis. this is not unique to the european parliament. we must recognize that economic and political power, whether we are talking about parliamentary work or about the way society generally works, is still in practice the domain of one sex, that is, men. although we often talk about the importance of gender equality, it is apparently easier to talk about it than to put it into practice. we need different instruments to implement and work on gender equality issues. education and knowledge are such instruments. we need to clarify the power structures in order to have an impact. we therefore look forward to the work of the institute for gender equality, which can provide us with instruments such as gender-disaggregated statistics and knowledge dissemination, among others. another important tool in the field of gender equality is education. it is important that we allow officials to continue with training on gender mainstreaming. but it is equally important that members of the european parliament also receive training in gender equality. it is not that we, the elected representatives, have sufficient knowledge of gender equality and gender mainstreaming in any obvious way. but we can do something about that. we can vote in favour of a proposal in the report that gender equality training should be organised for all members for the next term. i would like to see this in the report. thank you, sir.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the committee on women's rights and anna záborská for an excellent initiative and report. but after this debate, i regret to say that the men who have participated have expressed dinosaur views. it's sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, sad, what is that much has already been said in this debate and i would like to devote my time to a few questions of principle. parliament will never have integrated committees, half-women committee chairs or speakers until there are half-women in the european parliament. i speak from experience. in sweden, it was only in 1994 that we got half of the women in politics and the parliament that we were able to make the big reforms. many women in european parliament committees have difficulty combining parliamentary work and family roles. as long as parliament does not offer parents the possibility of staying at home with parental insurance and receiving compensation and a substitute, we have a gender equality problem. it is a strength of the report that it both talks about and proposes measures at the level of officials and for us members of parliament, but in the next report i would like to see more targets and means for the employees. and i call for a debate on how and when we will have half the women in parliament.
in writing. mutual principles must be respected and the ability to carry passengers across national borders is important. however, it is up to the member states to decide whether to privatise their national rail services. privatized railways that are not integrated with the national railway network have often been poorly functioning and often resulted in increased costs for taxpayers (for example in the uk). i therefore support the council's common position, which is less far-reaching, and therefore vote against parliament's amendment.
in writing. passengers should have good rights, but this is too much regulation at eu level. the proposal is at a level which is far below the current swedish compensation law. the attempt by parliament to include strictly local or national transport in addition to international transport, where there is a logic in the eu's intervention, is unnecessary over-regulation. in addition, these rules risk putting downward pressure on swedish compensation conditions and could impair the ability of passengers to take a taxi or bus when the train is delayed.
in writing. the wallis report, a6 - 481/06, contains proposals on the choice of law in cases of invasion of privacy and protection of personality which provide that the case should be decided in the country where the damage occurred. we are voting against these proposals because they would imply restrictions on swedish freedom of expression and press legislation by allowing, for example, a swedish newspaper to be sued in other countries on the basis of laws different from those of the country where the newspaper is published.
in writing. the rome ii report which the european parliament voted for today contains two texts which are contrary to the swedish press freedom legislation. unfortunately, the amendments containing the texts in question could not be voted against because they were part of a larger block. i voted in favour of the report, but i reserve my vote against amendments nos 9 and 19, paragraph 7a.
in writing. the monitoring of arms exports shall be carried out by the respective member states through their national legislation and cooperation within the framework of the united nations. i am firmly opposed to the plans to establish a possible eu arms export control agency. the report is about creating a binding code of conduct for the export of war material within the eu framework. it is proposed that a new eu body be set up to control member states' exports. whatever the view on principle, there is reason to question whether this leads to a more restrictive policy, which is the aim. at the same time, the european defence agency is working to further coordinate and liberalise the export policies of the member states. i believe that the un, given its experience, knowledge and global reach, is best placed to drive global disarmament and ensure that the best possible code of conduct is adhered to.
in writing. i am voting against this report because i think it is based on a wrong reasoning about which political level is responsible for road safety. junilista believes that the member states have the primary political responsibility for the legislative measures to be taken to improve road safety. increased road safety can be achieved by member states adopting the strategies that have proven successful in different eu countries. the views of the european parliament are laudable, but they add very little to the picture. the report contains a number of proposals which do not take sufficient account of the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. these include the proposal to provide continuous training in road safety for children aged 3-18 through a specific eu action plan, to launch information campaigns against driver fatigue in the member states and to investigate the consequences of driver fatigue in traffic.
in writing. speed limits are a national concern, which depends on many factors and should therefore be determined at national level. as much as i would like to rule that the germans should not have their autobahn for environmental or safety reasons, it would be counterproductive to impose it on them from the outside. it would instead weaken the political forces in germany which are fighting for the same thing. a level of 0.5 per thousand is too high and still not acceptable. the requirement for traffic lights is as stupid to decide at eu level as forcing the uk to introduce right-hand traffic. if you save 5,000 lives, it should be easy to push through on a national level. i would, however, vote in favour of calling on the member states to prevent dangerous driving behaviour by lorries, because that would respect the rights of national parliaments. it is also an environmental benefit not to allow trucks to compete with trains by using poor safety.
mr president, we have two tasks to fulfil: one is to reduce co2 emissions and the other is to ensure that we can meet the changes that follow climate change. they both require that we have the resources for investment, that we have the opportunities for growth and the opportunities to take care of the environment. limits and goals are important in this, but it is just as important to take action that is compatible with economic growth and resources for investments in order to safeguard what we must do. sometimes we can look at the more practical and realistic actions. i shall mention only two. long-truck combinations are one of the more substantial ways of reducing co2 emissions regarding road transport. commissioner, president-in-office: you can do something about that as a practical action that will have a substantial result. the second task concerns energy policy. phasing out nuclear power in the way and at a pace that is being planned in today’s europe is a secure way of getting increased co2 emissions. if we succeed in launching more renewable energy sources, then the phasing-out of nuclear power will reduce the gains and maybe we will get into minus with increased co2 emissions. i should like the german presidency to take the lead in a new policy. you should do that not in order to solve the greenhouse effect, as some say, but in order not to make things worse. we must at least keep the same share of energy coming from nuclear power that we have today in order not to increase co2 emissions. we need high-tech, not low-tech; we need more trade, not less trade; we need more investments, not less investments and we need more growth, not less growth. the challenge is to combine those things, and i look forward to your reply on my two certain tasks, however politically uncomfortable it might be for you.
mr president, first of all i would like to agree with mrs doyle that the problem that we are facing is not environmental. it affects all sectors of society and we have to take a systematic and comprehensive approach. to mr reul i would like to say that i think we have to realise that parallel to climate change, we are also affected by the depletion of fisheries, huge and rapid deforestation, and water scarcity in many parts of the world. we have to face the fact that the lifestyles that we have developed in a world with 8 to 9 billion people goes beyond the carrying capacity of the globe, and that is why we have to take a systematic approach that brings us back onto a normal, sustainable path. the reason i asked to speak is primarily that i am very disappointed with one aspect of the energy and climate package from the commission. you talk, commissioner, in that package, about an energy partnership with africa. that is good. but why not the same for china and india? i know there are technology agreements, but there is very little money to back them up. in the coming five to ten years, emissions from these countries because of expanding energy systems will totally overwhelm us unless we help them to do the right things now. some people say ‘let china pay’. but then we disregard the historic responsibility we have for all the emissions in the past and we also disregard the fact that the chinese need skilled people; they need technology to make it happen; they need to invest in clean technology. so my question to you and to the minister is, why do you not think big? why do you not establish a partnership with china and india and beef it up with financial resources so that we can really do this together aggressively? otherwise i think what we are doing here will be rather marginal in the years to come. we have to have this dual approach, and i feel the omission of this in your energy package is conspicuous. for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we are now debating a report on an issue which affects both the member states of the eu and the rich world in general, but in different ways. there is no doubt that overweight and obesity are serious problems that have consequences not only for those directly affected but also for society as a whole. the question we should ask ourselves, however, is whether this is really a problem to be dealt with at eu level. i don't think it was. why should the eu decide where schools should be located in the member states? does this house not think that the people are aware that fruit and vegetables are a healthy diet, or that exercise is important for health? in paragraph 46 of the report, concern is expressed about the decline in the consumption of fruit and vegetables in the eu, due to high prices for these products. this in itself is a sobering thought when one considers the enormous subsidies that eu farmers receive through the cap. will the next step be to demand an increase in agricultural subsidies? obesity and related diseases are an issue for each member state to address, taking into account the specific circumstances of each country. let us instead address the issues that are appropriate to this forum.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs věra flasarová, for a very important report on discrimination against women and girls in the field of education. there are no legal restrictions in eu member states that discriminate against girls' and women's access to education. but as in so many other contexts of gender equality, there are other structures than the purely formal rules that apply in reality. this is why, despite formal rights, the proportion of girls and women is still underrepresented in terms of continuing education or pursuing an academic career. it is therefore structures, attitudes and old gender stereotypes that set the framework for the education of women and girls. the rapporteur is therefore right to focus on the need for a strategy which must start early and involve training teachers and school staff in this area. increased awareness, knowledge and education is the way to build a just society for all, regardless of gender, class and ethnicity.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the ries report and the fight against obesity go deep into issues which i think are the responsibility of the member states. the report also raises issues that politicians should not be concerned with at all. in my view, there is no individual perspective at all. to say that obesity is a chronic disease is not only wrong, it is to take responsibility for people who are overweight or, worse, to take away from so many overweight people the hope that they can change their own situation. obesity may be a symptom of a chronic disease, but to claim otherwise is to create the stigma that the report claims to be trying to eliminate. but the most serious thing, in my view, is something quite different, namely that the media are being steered in a direction which is currently opportune. it is unacceptable and politicians should not do that. i am surprised that so many of my swedish colleagues voted in favour of this report. we moderates voted against, of course.

member of the commission. mr president, honourable members, both the state of preparation of postal markets for full opening and the financing of the universal service in an open market have been analysed with great care and attention by the commission. the commission’s analysis, which was based on several years of studies and continuous and transparent discussions with all stakeholders, is presented in considerable detail in the commission’s preparatory documents. the proposal for a directive and its supporting documents are now under discussion in both the council and the european parliament. it is up to you and the member states in the council to make your own evaluation. the commission took into account the swedish experience to which the honourable member refers, as well as all other market and regulatory developments in the postal sector. it is not a question of attributing a particular value judgement to any or all of these developments or to suggest that the swedish model, or indeed another model, should be copied elsewhere. it is rather a question of showing that various solutions exist. these solutions can be implemented according to the specificities of each national market in order to conduct the necessary postal reform while guaranteeing the provision of an efficient universal service. it is worth noting that in sweden the opening of the market was completed, guaranteeing the provision of the universal service, without the need for additional financing. the swedish government recently presented a detailed study on the opening of the swedish postal market, and its findings do not seem to confirm the fears expressed by the honourable member. in conclusion, and as i said earlier, the commission believes that completing postal reform is vital for securing further improvements in efficiency and quality of service and for ensuring the long-term viability of the postal sector and the business opportunities and employment potential it generates.
member of the commission. according to my information, the situation in sweden has meant that performance is better now than it was, and that has been due to competition. furthermore, the average price of mail (of which mail sent by consumers only represents a small part), including mail by business to consumers, has sharply decreased. in the proposal we have put forward, there is a large menu of options allowed to member states as to how they can deal with the universal service obligations. it will be noted that i made no change at all to the universal service obligation and have allowed the member states a large menu of options as to how they go about financing it. our package should come as no surprise to anybody who has been following this debate, because this debate and the opening-up of the markets have been ongoing for 15 years, and this is the final step in that particular process.
member of the commission. i am pleased that ms kauppi has spoken on this subject, because finland is a country which is very sparsely populated, and the universal service obligation has been fulfilled there, and so too in sweden. these markets have seen the benefits of open competition, and, it is fair to say, so too have the other markets that have opened up their postal area. it is our intention to stick to the time frame of 2009, which is expressed in the 2002 directive on opening up markets, which further said that, by the end of 2006, the commission would come forward with its proposals confirming, or not, the opening of the market in 2009 and whatever other further steps are necessary, and that is what we have done. but the matter is now in the hands of the co-legislators at parliament and at the council of ministers.
i'm from northern sweden. the liberalisation in sweden has been quite good in terms of big cities, but the state is allowed to step in and cover other places. for example, in the north of sweden, in pajala, where i live, we have a 270 km route that covers 200 households. if we have a common directive, can the commissioner guarantee that these households will continue to receive their mail?
in addition, there is the 2006 un human rights council resolution no. 2, which early emphasizes the right of indigenous peoples to sanctuaries. in this case, the entire mountain is the sanctuary, especially the water in the mountain. large amounts of water are used to transport the coal in a pipeline, which means that both the coal and the water in the sanctuary are extracted in a way that deprives the indians of their original rights. i don't know if we have any coal from this mountain in europe today. at this time, an investigation is underway into the possibility of an extended breach.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. producers in other parts of the world have succeeded in producing wines that appeal to european consumers, while being cheaper than european wine. the rapporteur believes that this must be countered by pumping more money into the agricultural policy, while at the same time implementing various types of campaigns. there is no doubt that excellent wines are produced in europe. the question of principle is whether we should allow poorer countries to be excluded in favour of european wine production. the new world is certainly made up of economic giants like the united states and australia, but also of newly industrialized countries like south africa, chile and argentina. in these countries, wines are often produced in poor, underprivileged areas. the social safety nets that exist in the eu countries do not exist in the same way in the above countries. if wine production in these areas is curbed, the consequences for the population will be severe. it is important to have a global perspective, and therefore a public health perspective, when discussing this issue. the protectionist features of this report are unfavourable in the long term for both eu and new world wine producers. after all, wine is an alcoholic beverage. the eu should therefore not sponsor campaigns which encourage more wine drinking.
mr president, i would like to thank you. on december 18, 2001, ahmed agiza, the father of five children, is waiting for the bus in karlstad, sweden. he had taken a swedish course. the next day, he's in a cold, solitary cell in cairo, egypt. he has been tortured and humiliated, both during the abduction in sweden and in egypt. this was over five years ago. ahmed agiza is still in captivity in egypt. ahmed agiza could just as easily have been mohammed al zari, abu omar, khaled el masri or any of the other prisoners imprisoned and tortured by the cia, often in cooperation with the member states. in this report, we document 1,245 cases of illegal cia flights, illegal transfers and a host of torture and abuse. this is a report calling for judicial investigations in those countries which have not opened such investigations, such as my own country, sweden, and for compensation and justice for those affected. the report also calls for the closure of the guantanamo prison camp. i would like to conclude by saying that human rights are universal and inviolable. the report shows that they must be recovered every day.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the rapporteur, mr fava, has delivered a very slanted and, in my view, bad report, but in this debate on the cia flights we are for once entering an area where the eu should have a say. human rights must be respected in the eu member states. the fight against terrorism must not lead us to give up that respect and to become like those we are to fight. it is true that the united states under president bush has begun to break the basic rules of civilized society. cia flights and secret prisons are just part of this frightening development. it is appalling that eu countries, including my own country, sweden, have been involved. these member states excuse themselves by saying that they have diplomatic guarantees that those who are extradited will not be tortured, that their guilt will be tested in a fair trial and that they will not be sentenced to death. all experience shows that such guarantees are worthless. torture and unfair trials are standard in many countries. shame on those who continue to contribute to this!
mr president, i would like to thank you. the cia committee has not been a court of law. the report is not an attack on the united states or anti-american. anyone who says that is wrong, including charles tannock. too many colleagues seem prepared to close their eyes and ears to the abuses that have occurred. too many coincidences, indications and pure evidence tell us that there have been repeated violations of fundamental human rights in the fight against terrorism and this on european soil. you who deny this should prove otherwise. in my home country, sweden, we know what has happened. two egyptians have been deported from the country with the help of the cia and the swedish government. the criticism of sweden is entirely correct and should not be watered down as inger segelström proposes. let us learn for the future and strengthen the eu's capacity to act for human dignity on the basis of articles 6 and 7 of the treaty. terrorism must be fought, but it must be fought by the rule of law.
mr president, i would like to thank you. amnesty international has called the cia's method of "torture on the job". that's exactly what this is about. we have documented flights, kidnappings, abductions and expulsions carried out in a completely unacceptable manner. as far as sweden is concerned, this concerns the expulsion of the two egyptians following a government decision on asylum and what happened at bromma airport during the execution of the expulsion. the cia then went too far in violating the common rules that sweden has supported both nationally, in the eu and in the un. i am glad that we have agreed that diplomatic guarantees cannot be used as a guarantee for expulsions to third countries or that a foreign power can execute executive orders without the full responsibility of the national police, even if it is suspected of terrorism. victims shall receive redress and compensation. what concerns me is whether the ppe-de group will follow the line taken by the cia committee and vote against the report. in my own country it is particularly important that the swedish conservative members vote in favour of the report, so that we agree that this must not happen again and that terrorism must be fought, but not by violating human rights.
in writing. we abstained in the vote on the report on the alleged use of european countries by the cia for the transport and illegal detention of prisoners. it is clear that the fight against terrorism has involved violations of human rights, which are also stated in the report, and which we moderates condemn. however, by mixing facts with assumptions and unconfirmed rumours, the report risks undermining rather than reinforcing legitimate criticism. we opposed the creation of the special committee because we believe that criminal offences should be investigated by the judiciary. the responsibility for investigations has been primarily national. for example, the swedish constitutional committee has examined - and condemned - the swedish government's actions. the issues that the report deals with are primarily those which fall within the competence of the member states and not the eu. the governments of the member states are not responsible to the european parliament in these matters but to their national parliaments and only to them, unless european legal rules have been violated. if there has been a breach by member states of commitments at european level, it is the commission's responsibility to investigate and bring such breaches to justice.
in writing. many regrettable incidents have been recorded in the fight against terrorism. however, these issues have already been addressed by the un human rights council and the council of europe. the swedish government has taken up this criticism. there is therefore no need to discuss this issue in the european parliament. i therefore voted in favour of many of the proposals, but abstained on the vote on the report as such.
in writing. since the junilists believe that the eu should not have a common fisheries policy, we have voted against this report. we also ask for references to previous explanations of vote on fisheries policy.
in writing. the proposal contains a number of good suggestions to prevent overfishing. that is why i voted in favour of this proposal for pragmatic reasons. the eu's fisheries policy has generally discouraged the long-term sustainable exploitation of marine resources by subsidising the purchase of efficient boats by fishermen. fisheries policy also harms the people of poor countries by buying fishing rights in their waters from weak governments. the eu should therefore return fisheries policy to the member states and only monitor that member states do not overfish species that move in the territorial waters of several countries.
in writing. the junilist believes that the eu's common agricultural policy should be abolished. we are also opposed to the new aid under the name of rural development. we wonder why the eu thinks that the member states are not capable of developing their rural areas themselves. we are faced with two options in this vote - the commission proposal or the proposal from the european parliament's committee on agriculture. in this situation we have chosen to vote for the commission's proposal, because it is better than the agriculture committee's proposal, which is totally unreasonable. the report's explanatory statement contains a number of views, including criticism of the abandonment or renationalisation of the cap, which junilista considers to be a good thing, and that it is contrary to the european parliament's right to participate, a view which junilista does not share. in addition, the explanatory memorandum states that the health check of the long-term budget should only form the basis for proposals for the financing period after 2013. that view is certainly not shared by junilistan. we believe that the mid-term review should lead to cuts in the agricultural/rural development budget already in the current 2007-2013 budget period. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the definition of the term 'competition'.
in writing. we voted in favour of amendments nos 3 and 8 because we think it would be good if the eu member states could agree unanimously on taxes that help to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. on the other hand, we do not support any decisions on taxes with majority voting at eu level, because we swedish social democrats believe that decisions on taxes should be taken at national level.
in writing. the greenhouse effect is one of the great problems of our time. it is therefore important that major efforts are made to reduce co2 emissions. one of the most important and best ways of eliminating co2 emissions is to replace energy production by burning coal and oil with nuclear power. the iaea has stated that nuclear power is a necessary component for reducing carbon dioxide emissions. it is regrettable that this resolution chooses to ignore this by insisting that emissions must not be reduced by nuclear energy. that is why i am voting against this resolution.
in writing. we supported the resolution on the lisbon strategy. we believe that the lisbon strategy should continue to be based on the mutual support of social, economic and environmental policies. it is important to continue the work on the strategy by emphasising a holistic approach, which means well-functioning and open markets, education, an active gender equality welfare policy and a progressive environmental policy, which together contribute to growth and job creation. we did not, however, support the demand that obstacles to job creation, including those for relatively low-wage jobs, must be removed. this can be interpreted as the opening of a low-wage market. we are absolutely against it. on the contrary, we want to prevent such a development. this can be done by giving a greater role to collective agreements and cooperation between the social partners. we also oppose legislation that encourages the use of 'clean energy' and 'carbon neutral energy technologies', as this could be interpreted as supporting the expansion of nuclear power. we would rather see increased efforts to develop renewable energy, to promote sustainable development.
in writing. the proposal contains several good suggestions on the importance of increased competition and improved conditions for entrepreneurship and innovation. the lisbon process must be seen as primarily a national matter, however, and it is unfortunate that parliament is going into a number of areas which are for the individual member states to decide. the report, for example, looks at how member states should organise their social security systems and how they should ensure that all those leaving school are offered jobs or measures aimed at employability. that is why i am voting against this resolution.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the report i have drawn up on the employment guidelines is extremely short and was adopted by the employment committee by a unanimous vote. it is really a question of how we are going to deal with the next year's employment guidelines and integrated guidelines. the guidelines we have this year remain the same, but next year we will have a more thorough review. then we expect, as commissioner almunia said, that we will be able to spend a little more time on this. then we need more time in parliament too. i really want to emphasize this. there is a difference between the employment guidelines and the economic guidelines. the employment guidelines cannot be adopted by the council until parliament has given its opinion. this requires good cooperation between the institutions for next year. we've made it easy for this year. we did that last year too, when there was no major review. next year, there's a big review. then we expect a cooperation that will last for several months. in fact, we have already had a discussion in the committee on employment between the coordinators and myself about appointing a rapporteur now to prepare the work for next year's change. i would like to mention something about the situation, even if it is only mentioned in the explanatory statement. europe is doing much better. employment trends are more positive than they were a few years ago. unemployment is falling. we're growing at a decent rate. however, we must not forget that there are still shortcomings on which we must concentrate. it concerns young people who leave school early, young people who go into unemployment immediately after their education. if they are excluded from the labour market for a long time, we know that it will be extremely difficult to get them back into it. the differences between men and women are still large in the labour market. this is the case for people who have come to us from other parts of the world and who still have problems entering the labour market because of our deficiencies in integration policy. this applies to people with disabilities who are also excluded from the labour market. i was at the informal ministerial meeting with the labour ministers. another aspect was also raised, namely that we want better jobs. it is true that we have more jobs, but we also want better jobs. what does 'better jobs' mean? well, we mean jobs that pay a living wage, jobs that provide further development and the training that is so necessary to get ahead in the job market. it is also about a good working environment and influence, so that you yourself and your trade union organisation have influence. the development we have now with more jobs does not always mean better jobs. we know this, and there are discussions on this in several countries, including germany, which i visited recently. i also know from my own country that some of the new jobs are so-called atypical jobs which do not always provide a living wage and do not always provide opportunities for further training. the impact is also quite minimal. so we must also concentrate on creating not just more but also high-quality jobs. europe can never compete on the world market by creating low-wage and non-earning jobs, but only by creating high-quality jobs that give influence, further training and so on. we therefore intend to return to this next year when we make the more thorough review of employment trends, which concerns more and better jobs and groups which today still have difficulty entering the labour market.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i am responsible for a case on cod fishing in the baltic sea in the environment committee. in my work on this subject i have been able to see that, despite the reduction in fishing quotas, some of the cod stocks are close to the biologically critical limit. the threats to cod are many, but the biggest threat is, after all, the illegal fishing that is taking place. i have sought to obtain the commission's assessment of the extent of the baltic sea's illegal fishing for the purpose of my report. however, the information has been classified. it took me months to get this information. but yesterday i came across the report, and suddenly i understand the secrecy. the baltic sea piracy was worse than we all imagined. i can only regret that sweden is one of the worst offenders. however, the commission notes that poland is the worst-affected country. when inspectors checked the fisheries in poland, they found that reported catches increased by 48 percent. this means that up to a third of polish fisheries turn out to be illegal. the second worst offender was sweden. the catch there increased by a whopping 21 percent when it was controlled. this is completely incomprehensible, since swedish fishermen do not even meet their quotas. the conclusion i draw from the report i read is that we must find a new strategy. we cannot put our hopes in endless negotiations on reductions in fishing quotas. the environmental movement should put its demands for a fisheries moratorium down somewhere. all of us who care for fisheries in the baltic sea, for example, should instead join forces in the fight to stop poaching. i would therefore like to make four demands to the commission. firstly, instead of hiding and protecting the criminals, you should expose them by publicising and naming the countries which do not stop the poaching. secondly, that you ensure that electronic logbooks are required as soon as possible for all vessels fishing in the baltic sea, regardless of their size. thirdly, that you bring poland to justice for breaches of the fisheries regulations, and fourthly, that you immediately launch an inquiry into the introduction of individual transferable fishing quotas which give individual professional fishermen a vested interest in protecting fish stocks. i therefore ask here today whether the commission is prepared to do so.
the rapporteur. no, i'm not voting for the motion to deport. oomen-ruijten should know that the economic guidelines cannot be adopted by the council until parliament has given its opinion. that's the actual order. the commission, and in particular the council, must take parliament's views into account. this is the case with the employment guidelines. this is a major problem with the economic guidelines, since there is no such framework in that area.
in writing. we swedish social democrats have voted against the report on the common organization of the market in wine. the report is characterised by a high degree of protectionism which can be detrimental to economic development both in the eu and in developing countries. the report also contains a number of recommendations to stimulate the wine sector by means of aid for the production and marketing of wine. we cannot support this, both in terms of public health and the impact on the eu budget. a number of amendments have been tabled by the xenophobic group its, which deletes certain passages from the report. we social democrats have voted against the its amendment. we think it is unacceptable to support this group. we consider that we have distanced ourselves from the report by voting against it in its entirety.
in writing. the present report raises a very important and topical question, but the approach is totally wrong. the european parliament is once again trying to advance its positions, this time with the argument of the increase in terrorism and its victims. the report is full of writings which represent small but nevertheless worrying steps towards a european union state with a common foreign and security policy. it is also interfering in the internal affairs of states, in some cases in violation of the constitutions of the member states. we also note that they have the audacity to refer to a constitution which was rejected in european referendums. the most serious of these calls include the abolition of the unanimity rule on such an important issue as police and judicial cooperation in criminal matters. this is completely unacceptable. we have therefore voted against the report, including most of the amendments tabled, in accordance with our mandate from our electorate.
in writing. this report does not contain any analysis of what may need to be harmonised at eu level. it's based on what should be shown. the aim is to achieve almost total control of economic policy at eu level. it is claimed without a shred of evidence that emu has strengthened europe's economy. experience suggests rather that the single currency has been directly detrimental from the point of view of economic stabilisation and has had little positive structural effect. the rapporteur calls for more money from the community budget for the lisbon strategy. this is unreasonable. what is required is that the member states actually do what they should do in their own self-evident interests. there is no point in trying to get other countries to finance them from common budgetary resources. the report shows no understanding of the value of the fact that the member states are still free from top-down control and can therefore seek new ways of conducting economic policy. at the same time, the rapporteur points out that some countries are doing much better than others in different areas. the fact that he can point to these differences and draw conclusions about what is clearly best practice is precisely because we have not implemented the extreme top-down management and uniformity that he advocates. the report points to a very dangerous path for economic policy in europe. that is why we voted no.
in writing. the report is old-fashioned and out of date with its focus on traditional growth policies. it has a very unbalanced appraisal of the euro and a very unbalanced applause of the single market. at the same time, it is to some extent self-critical and recognises the growing wage gap, greater poverty and the advantages of the scandinavian model. the report calls for continued forced liberalisation of the energy market, but combines this with calls for investment and tax policies that favour renewable energy. the report thus contains both positive and negative elements. that is why i am abstaining in the final vote.
in writing. junilista believes that adaptation to rising energy prices is best done at national level. that is why we are sceptical of this 'opinion' resolution by the european parliament. for example, in the draft report, paragraph 20, it is considered that there is no eu energy regulator. bureaucratization and top management are, as always, the only way forward in parliament. we believe that the member states should be responsible for such supervision themselves, as long as there are no crossborder risks of the nuclear type. in that case, however, the authority responsible for supervision should be the un agency, the iaea. eu countries have to agree on certain energy targets. how these are achieved will be left to the individual countries. the report contains both positive and negative comments. but as it is a report outside the legislative procedure and of little importance, we have chosen to vote against it in the final vote, as we believe that it does not make any difference to or from the debate in the member states on these issues.
for the pse group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr hasse ferreira, for his good work and for his success in securing a large majority in the committee for his proposal. social services of general interest were excluded from the services directive. there was a reason for that. they have a very specific character, different from the commercial services, and have therefore been excluded. the special nature of these programmes means that they are often publicly funded, in whole or in part. they do not target groups, primarily on the basis of ability to pay, but some services target everyone, such as childcare in my country, sweden. some are for people with special needs, the elderly with special care needs or the disabled with the special needs they have. the aim is to ensure that people have as equal a life as possible. how they are organised differs across europe. often it is a local organization. they are democratically run, often close to the citizens, and are also often carried out by the public by public employees, but they can also be cooperative and sometimes also private companies. it is up to the local authorities to decide how they want their activities to be carried out. social services are important for europe. they are important for several reasons, of course, first and foremost for the citizens, for all those citizens who take advantage of public services. they contribute to both equality and gender equality. it is important for the economy, as rapkay said, without the social services all over europe the rest of the economy would not work, and it is important not least for employment, lots of people are employed in the social services, not least a large group of women. as regards the importance of these questions for europe, we can discuss what we need to do at european level. i share the view of rapkay and most of my group that we need some kind of sectoral directive, precisely to distinguish the specific nature of these services and to separate them from the commercial services. it is not to take them back from the local level or to go against the principle of subsidiarity, but to protect these services on the basis of the activities they have today, the diversity of our member states.
member of the commission. ladies and gentlemen, thank you for a discussion that has been extraordinarily profound, extraordinarily interesting. let me summarise very briefly the points which i consider to be of appropriate special importance in the debate. it is quite clear that social services of public interest are, according to the prevailing view, the object or subject of the discussion sui generis. they are not ordinary services, either because they guarantee rights, or because they are directed at people who are and can be very vulnerable and very weak in terms of defending their own position and their own rights. it is also clear that the principle of subsidiarity has been strongly emphasised and that proposals for different types of solutions have been received from many parties and that there is a certain uncertainty in the legal concept of these services, in particular in relation to the principles applicable in the internal market. ladies and gentlemen, i think all these issues are so important that they need to be given continued attention. the commission intends to take the following steps:present a european strategy on social services of general interest to be adopted in the autumn of this year. this communication will set out the further steps the commission intends to take. the commission will assess the legal feasibility and the appropriate political opportunity to present a legal instrument in this area. the communication will also be accompanied by a first biennial report on social services of general interest. this report will aim to provide an overview of the situation of social services of general interest in europe, to describe the trends observed in the modernisation of these services, to highlight the socio-economic importance of the sector and to present the legislative developments over this period. ladies and gentlemen, i share your view that social services in the public interest are a solid structure in our social model, that future developments in them will be very rapid, that they will present us with new challenges and that action is needed. thank you. thank you.
vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. in fact, corporate social responsibility (csr) is on the rise in europe. the commission's communication last year has triggered a wide wave of activity and new ideas. csr can make a major contribution to sustainable development and increase europe's innovation potential and competitiveness. csr is therefore closely linked to our strategy for growth and jobs. the aim of this strategy is not only to create more jobs but above all to create better jobs. it is not about short-term successes, it is not about seemingly favourable quarterly results, but about our companies sustainably adapting to changing global competitive conditions. the social environment is an important factor in competition, but it is not the responsibility of policy alone. companies have their own responsibilities which go beyond mere compliance with the law. they have a responsibility for the social climate, for the environment, for equality, for education, for innovation and for structural change. in short, they have a responsibility for the people who work in the companies and for the location where they are located. the commission welcomes the knowledgeable and competent report by mr howitt, which substantially supports and makes an important contribution to the commission's objectives of strengthening csr in europe. i strongly agree that the debate should be about delivering concrete results, that we should fully involve the stakeholders and interested groups in the multi-stakeholder approach and that we should integrate the whole process even more fully into the lisbon strategy. the commission sees, as you know, limits to the possibilities of regulation in this area. we believe that corporate culture cannot be legislated and standardized from above. this is why the commission has not decided to propose a regulation on csr or to standardise csr at european level. if something has to be regulated somewhere, if we have to insist on the fact that the companies do something out of our political responsibility, then we must have the courage to make the appropriate legal regulations, and we must do so concretely and in the area in question. but corporate social responsibility is by definition something that goes beyond the legal obligations of companies. it's more than what we're prescribing in regulations. we want to support and encourage companies to use the many internationally recognised instruments available. the commission also supports csr in a global context. an example of this is the eu-africa business forum, set up by my colleague louis michel, where business representatives from two continents discussed social responsibility in november 2006. the commission continues to discuss with developing countries progress in implementing the basic provisions of the international labour organisation. in europe, we are encouraging the further development of such instruments within the different sectors as well as cross-sectoral initiatives. my colleague špidla and i meet more and more entrepreneurs who are committed to making europe a pioneer in csr and who see it as a clear advantage for their business in the market. the european csr alliance has developed impressive model initiatives in its short existence. forward-looking managers and investors have long known that a company with a good csr record is usually a successful one. you can even read it in the stock prices of these companies. the commission is calling on european companies to make csr a european brand and to put together a competition to find the best ideas. we believe that a european company must be one which recognises its social responsibility and acts accordingly. the commission and parliament are in full agreement on this.
member of the commission. mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, my colleague günter verheugen has characterised the general framework of corporate social responsibility. i think it is appropriate for me to stress that these are voluntary undertakings which clearly go beyond the legislation and this of course does not in any way restrict the right of any legislator, any body with the right of legislative initiative, to propose such proposals as it deems necessary. since the general framework has been characterised clearly enough, let me mention some specific areas where we support the enterprise initiative, which are part of the strategy of march 2006. first, through recruitment and personnel management policies, enterprises play a leading role in vocational integration and promote social inclusion. secondly, 2007 was declared the european year of equal opportunities for all. we have neither the right nor the means to deprive ourselves of our diverse talents, and we support business initiatives to promote equal opportunities. the third important element of the intergenerational pact that an ageing europe urgently needs is the inclusion and retention of older people in employment. the other priorities identified in the march communication are equally important, whether it is health, investing in lifelong learning, education innovation, science or global scope. the text you will be discussing addresses these issues. to ensure that csr continues into the 21st century, a dialogue with society as a whole is needed, which is why we convened the multilateral forum on csr in december. we also decided to convene a european-level discussion body between the plenary sessions of the forum, bringing together representatives of civil society, business and academia. the forum meeting allowed us to evaluate the results of the initiatives that have taken place since 2004. as regards the commission's initiatives, we believe that csr is increasingly being integrated into all our policies, in particular into actions related to the european union's external policy. topics of common interest such as education, research and working conditions in the subcontracting chain have also been identified for the future, which is of course of great importance, especially in promoting sound social conditions in areas outside europe for subcontractors who supply european companies. we have a lot of work to do, but we're on the right track. our discussions and exchanges with stakeholders show that csr is an evolving process. it is important to maintain the current conditions and not to slacken in our efforts.
draftsman of the opinion of the committee on industry, research and energy. – mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur and congratulate him on his report. i think he has managed to present a report that can secure widespread support in this chamber. i think it is important to make some remarks about corporate social responsibilities. first of all, the main task of companies is to deliver the services and products they are established for and to be run at a profit, because otherwise they will not survive. if they cannot fulfil these main tasks, they will fail in their fundamental social responsibility as employers and as producers of services and goods. i think it is important to underline this when we discuss how to define corporate social responsibilities. our point of view – because we have discussed this question in the itre committee as well as within our group – is that it should be a matter of voluntary responsibility. it has to be performed on its own merits and as a part of the responsibility that the individual company has in society. companies have a social role in society. they must be good employers, delivering services and products of a high quality, because otherwise they will lose credibility and they will lose out in the market. but there are also grounds for saying that they must behave well in their own terms, because good and strong companies who stand up for fundamental values, whether in europe or anywhere else in the world, are contributing to a better world, but also to a better economy and to better products and services. there should be voluntary responsibility, because otherwise legislation would be needed and that is another matter. if we try to streamline too much, we will lose the variety and the dynamism that each company can deliver by making the most of its opportunities to be a good employer, a good company and a good service and product provider.
