for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. thank you to sartori for a great job. the commission's communication on the roadmap addresses various priority areas for gender equality, including the promotion of equal participation of women and men in decision-making. the commission therefore considers it to be important. but the commission's interest in helping to promote women's participation in decision-making itself is beyond criticism, i dare say. this is because the president of the commission appointed an advisory group on the important issues of energy and climate change as recently as 6 march this year. the group consists of 11 persons, one of whom is a woman. so much for priorities. unfortunately, as long as the eu institutions continue to act in this way, their credibility is at risk. all roadmaps, resolutions, statements and so on will then be mere paper products. gender equality and women's rights must be the subject of concrete action, we are many women who have grown tired of the beautiful words about equal rights. we don't need words, we need action. i have long argued that we would make progress on gender equality if we had a commissioner who was solely responsible for gender equality issues and also made sure that decisions and resolutions were implemented. i still think it would be a real step forward. (applause) i would like to thank you.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is a pleasure to adopt this report on a roadmap for a europe of equals. the report addresses many important areas such as equal pay for equal work, violence against women by men, fatherlessness, etc. issues where gender equality really needs to be concretised and strengthened. here all the member states have a great responsibility to ensure that the document is not simply put away and forgotten, but that we are really working towards a more equal europe. but i would also like to address all men in particular, because without the participation of men in this important work, we will never have equality in europe. last thursday was international women's day. the swedish newspapers wrote about equality from top to bottom, and it probably looked the same all over europe. what upsets me is that this issue is less frequently raised during the rest of the year. just take this with the gender pay gap, where the pay gap is still 15 percent in favour of men in europe. this is despite the fact that the treaty of rome already provided for the right to equal pay. it is remarkable that we have not progressed on this issue in 50 years. i have high hopes for the commission's communication on this important issue and for the gender equality plan for europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank the committee on the environment for putting this excellent question to the commission, which gives us the opportunity to hear the commission's reaction to important questions. it also gives us in the committee on the internal market the opportunity to put forward what we think is important. health is a matter for the member states and the principle of subsidiarity applies. however, this is not the same as citizens not having the right to healthcare in other member states. we cannot claim to defend the right of patients to safe and high quality care if we do not allow those who need it, for whatever reason, to seek care in another member state. we must do our utmost to preserve these rights, despite the various dimensions of which everyone here is well aware, namely that there are problems, but also positive dimensions, particularly on the information side. it is also clear or at least should be clear that the jurisdiction of the court of justice cannot be weakened by secondary legislation. the practice exists, and the treaty gives any citizen who so wishes the right to provide services in another member state, notwithstanding the full compliance with the laws of the respective country. the commission, in my view, should withdraw any legislation whereby member states and governments seek to restrict existing primary rights of patients or service providers. it is important that the proposal the commission is putting forward is a step forward and not a step backwards and we have high hopes for commissioner kyprianou in that respect.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? i would like to begin by expressing my satisfaction, as so many others have, that climate/energy policy is now high on the commission's agenda. i too thought the outcome of the summit was satisfactory. i also think that the focus on growth and employment is positive. let me remind you of the major revision of the economic and employment guidelines which will come next year. it will be important, and we in parliament have stressed on several occasions the importance of parliament being properly consulted on this occasion and given adequate time to submit its proposals. now i'm going to go over what hannes swoboda was up to. i think it was jacques delors who once said that nobody loves the single market. it must therefore be supplemented and balanced by a proper social dimension. the commission talks a lot about the social dimension but delivers very little. there are few new proposals in the social field. let me take the positive side, however, it is positive that the commission is now tackling the issue of discrimination also outside the labour market, against disabled people and other groups. it is good that social services are being brought up and that efforts are being made to clarify their specific nature. we had a discussion about it yesterday because of a report and i personally hope that it would be in the form of a sectoral directive. otherwise, it is a matter of surveys of the social situation, surveys of the way in which labour law operates, surveys everywhere, but few concrete proposals. let me mention two proposals where i think initiatives could be taken, to be a little more concrete. old jobs are gone, new jobs are coming. the instruments we have for information and consultation, such as the european works councils and the directive on information and consultation of employees, are not working satisfactorily. employees lose their jobs without being properly informed and without being involved in the process. it is time for the commission to take the initiative to review the existing directives so that they work in accordance with the intentions. the second area concerns the working environment. europe has had a great deal of success in the field of health and safety at work over the years, with some 50 directives in existence. there are far fewer deaths and injuries in the workplace today. at the same time, new occupational health and safety problems are emerging in the labour market due to the changing labour market. these include stress, burnout, workplace bullying and other emerging workplace problems. the commission's initiatives are thereby absent. my advice to the commission is not just to talk about the social dimension, but also to deliver, for example in the area of information and consultation and the field of health and safety at work. (applause) i would like to thank you.
in writing. interest subsidies to offset the interest costs incurred by member states in purchasing agricultural products produced in the eu are one of many aspects of eu agriculture that need to be radically changed. in the name of consistency, however, it is acceptable that some interest compensation is given to the new member states in order to avoid creating mass unemployment in the poorer parts of europe. the report concerns the extension of a scheme in place since 2004 which compensates the new member states whose interest rates are substantially above the eu average. the cost of an extension is approximately eur 10 million per year. the financing has been proposed to be through savings in other budget lines and thus does not lead to additional budgetary increases at the aggregate level. moreover, the commission considers this extension as part of a package which at the same time proposes to eliminate the subsidies for maize cultivation, which would mean a tough saving of some eur 35 million in 2008 (and the total saving for 2008-2014 amounts to eur 617,8 million according to the commission). in the longer term, a 'no' vote not only risks affecting the new member states but could also jeopardise the commission's proposal for savings of just over 600 million. i have therefore decided to vote in favour of this report.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the roadmap for gender equality because we take it for granted that equality between men and women should prevail. but we also believe that the responsibility for most actions to achieve gender equality rests with individuals. as the measures requiring legislation are usually national competence, we have voted against a large number of individual proposals in the report. we do not believe, for example, that the eu should decide on childcare, parental leave or abortion legislation in the member states.
in writing. gender equality and the vulnerability of women in both the public and private spheres is one of the most important issues of our time. however, we have voted against the report because we have confidence in the proper management of this important matter by the respective national parliaments of the member states. we do not believe that the eu should express itself on this type of issue in lengthy reports, which are rather political programmes, and we are opposed in principle to the general trend of the eu institutions seeking to gain influence and competence in an increasing number of areas. the eu's role in this context is to ensure that no country that is or becomes a member violates the values of gender equality.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. mr president of the council! commissioner, what is it? as has been said, we have ambitious targets from the barcelona summit for the development of childcare. some countries have already achieved the targets, others are a long way off, which is not satisfactory. why, then, is child care important? it is important for families to be able to reconcile family and work life. it is important for gender equality, since women have traditionally been the ones who have had to take care of children and thus have not entered the labour market. it is important for children to have access to good quality childcare as an important part of their development towards school and the adult world, etc. it is also good for employment and growth, because if we look at the countries that have come a long way in terms of childcare development, they also have high employment rates, high employment rates for women and therefore also higher growth. we see the demographic trends. we need women in the labour market - we need everyone in the labour market. the method we have is best practice. many of the decisions are taken in the member states, at local and regional level, but i must say that it is a satisfactory thing that the debate has now spread. i am following the debate in germany, for example. in germany, this debate is very much alive in a way that it has not been before. it is important that this debate takes place in our member states and that we see the need for childcare so that this development can begin. i would like to thank the german presidency for inviting me, as chairman of the committee on employment, to the informal meeting in may to discuss these issues. i am happy to accept this meeting. we hope that we will speed up the development of childcare, for the sake of children, for the sake of families but also for the sake of employment.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the most important basis for equality between women and men is the right and opportunity for women to be self-sufficient, i.e. the right to work but also the opportunity to have good and quality childcare. without the support and responsibility of society to ensure that all children have access to good childcare at a reasonable cost to the parents, women are forced to rely on others for their livelihood. but i want to emphasize, as the presidency did, the rights of the child and the child's perspective in this context. it is very important for children to be able to socialize with other children, to meet other adults than their parents, to have access to the pedagogy necessary for the child's development. children who have the benefit of being in childcare with other children and adults with educational training get a better start on their development and increase their social skills. so good childcare is not only important for gender equality and economic growth, but it is just as important for the children. i think it is time for the eu institutions, and above all the member states, to reduce the level of commitment to children's rights that has been applied to goods in the internal market. otherwise we won't even reach the barcelona goals.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for a very good and important document. many of my colleagues have said that the functioning of the internal market requires well-informed and knowledgeable consumers, a well-functioning market and good competition. this is, as we all know, a matter where national interests are to some extent at odds with the interests of the eu and of consumers. my question to the commissioner is how do you see the possibilities of getting a greater response to a common consumer policy where there is also the possibility of further action within eu and ec legislation today compared to the past. we know that it has been difficult to get the member states to agree to a wider eu commitment on these issues.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. greenhouse gas emissions are a concern. they appear to be leading to rapid changes in the earth's climate. what can the eu do about this on its own? it's a difficult balance. by taking the lead, we increase our ability to put pressure on china, india, the united states and russia, and europe can gain an environmental technological edge. but we must not go too fast and thereby undermine our competitiveness. the council agreed on a fair balance in this matter. a rare success in an eu where the vast majority of decisions are undermining freedom, weakening democracy, undermining national sovereignty and bureaucratising our lives. but at the same time, it should be noted that this success shatters the official argument for the draft constitution, which is that individual member states must be able to be overruled in eu decision-making and that democracy must yield to efficiency. otherwise, they say, the eu will be impotent. this is not true, as it seems, and neither was it true of the services directive. the hidden argument for the draft constitution is that the creation of an eu state requires a power platform for a new european elite. political power must be taken away from the nation states which are the origin and foundation of european democracy. the council has once again shown that it is possible to take major decisions even when all the member states have vetoes. thank you for that. the court of justice has also held that the principle of proportionality is not applicable to the application of the principle of proportionality.
in writing. the report is seen as a further step towards full harmonisation of community legislation. the creation of an authority to monitor and record migration flows to and from the eu is, in our view, completely unnecessary and would place an additional burden on the eu budget. the rapporteur has defined legal concepts such as 'citizenship' and 'illegal immigrants' without respect for existing national legislation. in fact, there is already accurate and reliable expert analysis and data on migration, not just within the eu but across the world, that the un represents. citizenship, refugee status and immigration are other concepts that are well defined in the un charter of human rights. the junilist does not want to contribute to the building of a surveillance society in which the free movement of people can be affected by decisions taken at supranational level and therefore votes against the report as a whole.
in writing. a directive is not the best way to regulate complex statistical collection, but it is better to manage it through agreements between responsible authorities with the necessary competence. i therefore abstain from voting on this report.
in writing. common rules for air transport are necessary and desirable, not least from a safety point of view. there are therefore good grounds for discussing how the current cooperation in the field of aviation should continue and develop. as usual, however, the european parliament is trying to bring forward other proposals aimed at extending the eu's powers in areas which should be reserved to the national states. however, we believe that the present report proposes that the european aviation safety agency (easa) should be given an overly broad mandate. we trust that the competent national authorities are able to ensure that pilots have satisfactory professional competence and adequate language skills. we also do not share the view that the eu countries should have a common representative in the un body for global aviation affairs (icao). we have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. we supported the report. social services have a special character and are different from commercial services. the way in which these services are financed and delivered is a matter for the member states themselves, but it is important that they are of high quality and accessible to all citizens. in order to protect social services from the rules of the internal market, a sectoral directive is desirable, distinguishing between social and commercial services with a clear definition. we are, however, confronted with the concept of 'women's work', which is a word unfamiliar to us. we assume that the rapporteur is referring to the fact that many women work in the social services sector and that it is important to promote their participation in the labour market, while at the same time combating precarious employment conditions. as regards 'part-time work', we consider full-time to be a right and part-time to be an opportunity. we also want to clarify that 'paid volunteering' does not exist in the public sector, but can exist in a complementary social economy. unpaid homework is not, however, part of the social services. we interpret the concept of 'public-private partnership' as meaning that in the social services sector there are different services that are publicly funded and performed by different actors, such as public companies, non-profit organisations, cooperatives and private companies.
in writing. the junilist accepts that the eu represents the member states in trade policy matters. this includes the current aviation agreement with the united states, which aims to allow eu airlines to fly freely to any place in the united states. we are critical of the european parliament's views on the ownership structures of american airlines. this is a matter for the competent american authorities. we do, however, share the general views expressed in the resolution. the aviation agreement is also good for the internal market, an area which we want to protect and develop. we have therefore voted in favour of the final vote.
in writing. nuclear weapons pose a global threat to the world's population. the developments we are seeing now, with more countries acquiring nuclear weapons technology, are very disturbing. every sovereign state has the right to defend itself and its citizens, but in principle we believe that nuclear weapons are not justified. history has shown the devastation these weapons can cause. the present resolution contains both good and bad phrases. for example, we believe that all references to the european security strategy should be deleted. in fact, this issue is a prime example of why we should not have a common foreign and security policy (cfsp) in the eu. several member states have substantial nuclear arsenals, while others cede their territory to non-european nuclear powers. in a future eu with a common security policy in place, non-nuclear-weapon member states could be inadvertently drawn into conflicts with such weapons. furthermore, the world is bigger than the eu and a global problem must be solved at a global level. the un has the knowledge and experience and is the competent institution to stop the proliferation of nuclear weapons in the world. we believe that the underlying meaning of the resolution is to strengthen the cfsp and parliament's influence on these issues. that is why we are voting no.
mr speaker, we are discussing two issues here tonight, and they should not be mixed together. one is the problem of unemployment, which should be dealt with by member states with the necessary support from the union and the different social funds – as was pointed out earlier by the commissioner – but not by more political involvement in the european aircraft industry. the other is the future of the european aircraft industry. if there is to be a future, that future must be based upon commercial decisions and the preconditions of the market, not on political discussions in parliaments or in governments. it is the involvement of too many governments, too many parliaments and too much politics that is creating problems and hindering the decision-making that would make the best of the opportunities open to the european aircraft industry. the task of airbus must be to produce and deliver the best aeroplanes in the world, not to deliver political promises from whatever country or from whatever government it is. i think there is only way to ensure that we can contribute to that. of course, we shall have the best possible support for research and science, but we shall also ensure that we can have a functioning market – and a functioning transatlantic market because that is crucial. it is important to ensure that the responsibility of the aircraft industry and of airbus, as we are discussing here, is kept within the company and in the management of the company, because otherwise all the decision-making will be split, complicated and bureaucratic and will lead to new failures. i think we should go for new success and give the company better opportunities by letting it be independent and not involved in political decision-making.
the questioner asks for age limits on gambling. there is a pg label partly funded by the eu today. in my country sweden, all computer games are age-marked with a cooperation between the industry and between the states: +3 years, +7 years, +12 years, +16 years, +18 years. furthermore, all computer games have content descriptions about discrimination, about drugs, about the scary and the ugly, about foul language, about sex and nudity and violence. there is a very good system in place today and, as i said, the eu is involved and finances it. i would like both the council and parliament to take note of this, so that we can discuss it when we come to the report on children which the commission is presenting at the moment.
member of the commission. mr president, i do not think i have ever been asked to reply, in any parliament in which i have been president-in-office, to an opening question or a speech with which i have agreed more than the speech i have just listened to. in terms of its description of the spirit, purpose and context of this exercise, i must say i thought the honourable member captured in every respect what we are doing most accurately and well. the only stage at which, i have to say, i somewhat part company is when he asks me why our services – dg trade – are applying new rules before the conclusion of the review. i have absolutely no idea what instances or issues he is referring to, and i would be pleased to hear because i do not know of any. on 29 may last year, i informed the european parliament of the necessity to review our trade defence instruments. i am pleased to be able to be back here and to update you on this process. we are now coming to the end of the consultation that we launched in december. that consultation solicited opinion from member states, business, ngos, individuals and, of course, the european parliament. what it did not do in any way was question the importance of trade defence instruments. tdi is necessary to combat unfair trade in an international economy that has no international equivalent to the competition rules we take for granted in our own domestic economies. tdi, in my view, is the flip side of an open economy. it is the guarantee that others will not abuse that openness by trading unfairly. the green paper asks if we could use tdi better, if our tools have adapted to a changing global economy and whether our rules could be clearer and operate more transparently. i think the justification for such an exercise is pretty obvious. the last review of our trade defence instruments was in 1996, and a lot has changed in the ways eu companies operate and as regards the role of global supply chains in our economy. many more eu companies now produce goods wholly or partially outside the eu for import into the eu. these changes challenge traditional understanding of what constitutes eu production and the eu’s economic interests. they make a definition of european workers’ interests harder to write, as cases are more complex. because these interests overall are those in which trade defence is rooted, that is good reason to assess the way we work and the way those rules operate. but the green paper launched a consultation; it contains no recommendations for reform and is not intended to. it puts forward a set of questions. i have repeatedly emphasised that this is an open process and i have no preconceived ideas. there was a question asked of me about the intentions behind the six categories of question in the consultation paper. they are designed simply to put the various issues in context. some are linked to the impact of globalisation on our trade defence system. others, especially those related to transparency, were raised by stakeholders and experts with whom i had informal discussions in july last year. so there are no intentions beyond the desire for intelligent debate and to rebuild the consensus and solidarity that has underpinned tdi and has come under strain in some recent cases. i fully echo the honourable member’s initial observation: we need to replace national selfishness with european solidarity, and that is what i hope to rebuild through the process of this review. i do not come to you today with substantive proposals because that is not my role at this stage. right now, we are listening. the scope of any proposed changes will depend on what we hear. somebody asked how this review fits with our attempts to reform anti-dumping rules in the wto. the honourable member reflected that question. actually, it is a rather good question. it is vital to push through the wto to ensure others match the kind of standards we apply to ourselves. we are doing that and we will keep doing that in the dda negotiations. but eu legislation on tdi already goes beyond wto requirements in many ways. the most obvious examples are the compulsory lesser duty rule and the community interest test which we apply in all investigations. these are rules we introduced because they make the system work better in the wider eu interest. of course we will push others to adopt similar rules, but reform at the international level is difficult and some of our key partners are, frankly, stubborn. so long as our refinements do not put us at a competitive disadvantage, so long as they reflect the eu’s economic interests, why should we not pursue further reform? that point relates to the question about the eu’s general posture on anti-dumping. are we protectionist or do we follow a ‘response and defence’ approach? in my view, protectionism is the shielding of a domestic industry from foreign competition, from fair competition – tough competition, yes, but fair nonetheless. that is not the intention of eu tdi policy and i will, of course, remain vigilant on that. we are not going to see our trade defence instruments turned into measures to protect eu industry from fair, legitimate competition. a protectionist does not recognise the difference between tough competition and unfair competition. we do. our system does. that is the difference between protection and protectionism. the eu process is complaint driven. we act only when eu industry can provide sufficient evidence that they are threatened by unfair trade, but we defend european production only against unfair trade, and we are bound by law to ensure that any trade defence measure is truly in the wider european economic interest. we are prudent and we are restrained but, above all, we are objective and dispassionate. a number of questions have gone right to this issue of making sure that tdi is effective and serves europe’s growth and competitiveness agenda. as you know, this review is part of the global europe framework policy that i launched last year, which is explicitly intended to put eu trade policy at the service of this growth and job strategy. beyond saying that, i think tdi can and should be part of our wider strategy for ensuring that eu companies compete on a level playing field internationally. i think it is for stakeholders to suggest how well the system is working to that end, and that is the purpose of the review. the question about the effectiveness of our measures is a good one. the possibility of review of trade defence measures always exists and measures cannot be extended without clear evidence that they are functioning as intended. the commission also undertakes internal analysis to ensure the effectiveness of its work. dg trade has recently started to analyse the impact of trade defence measures on certain companies and sectors. a credible tdi system has to be based on this sort of analysis. a question was asked about public information and about the results of the consultation process. as you know, the commission has very clear rules on the transparency of decision-making. some of you may have attended the seminar on the green paper that took place only yesterday in brussels. this event was open to the public and relayed on the website. unless a contributor requests anonymity, we are publishing all responses to the green paper on the website of dg trade. the whole process has been totally transparent. finally, on cooperation and dialogue with parliament: you know that i have constantly appeared before you on all important trade policy matters, and i will continue to do so. parliament has a very important role in the tdi reflection process. your report will be central to the ongoing review process, and i will study its recommendations very closely. i thank you for hearing me again today and i look forward to being back here again soon to discuss any and every issue of trade policy, on which i delight in remaining answerable to this house.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: trade defence instruments - the name is quite revealing - a tool to protect against trade. but protecting against trade is both expensive and foolish, and that is why few economists tend to support the use of this instrument. i myself have spoken here several times about how consumers are forced to pay a sky-high price for a limited producer interest. the fact is, however, that we will continue to have some form of trade policy safeguard. until we have a common international competition law, i think all countries will feel the need to be able to protect themselves against what they perceive as unfair trade. i therefore believe that we must now design the instrument so that it becomes legitimate and is perceived as such by all producers, importers, consumers and all member states. we must move away from the predictable conflicts of north against south and producers against importers and consumers, because these conflicts in themselves undermine confidence in the instrument and, by extension, in the eu's trade policy. if we are to have any consensus on these trade defence instruments, we need to emulate competition law more. for example, everyone i meet is calling for more transparency, more predictability and, above all, less political interference in this matter. i understand them, because it is frankly not serious that the member states are only given a few days to evaluate thousands of pages before decisions on anti-dumping duties. the fact that we have an army of consultants in brussels running around chasing rumors of commission proposals for new safeguard measures is not serious either, nor is the political adjustment country, where the excise duties can be exchanged for exemptions to the working time directive, which in turn can be exchanged for duties on norwegian salmon. all of this shows that we need a fundamental overhaul. besides, the world has changed. as global tariffs become tighter and lower, more of our trading partners are using instruments to stop imports of goods in the traditional way, and europe is the largest player in the world market, so we need to show leadership. i would therefore like to end by asking you how we can ensure that this reform does not become, in fact, a reform which, as frédéric bastiat would say, 'throws stones into our ports just because others happen to have rocky coasts'.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. free trade is not good if it is unfair, and trade protection measures are an attempt to make it fairer. i think the commission is putting too much emphasis on multinational companies and i am concerned about a redefinition of the community interest. i would like to clarify that if an eu-based transnational company is involved in social dumping or environmental dumping, either itself or through subsidiaries and subcontractors, then they cannot be counted as a community interest merely because they are registered in the eu. they must be punished for such a practice. the green paper also contains many interesting issues which we will discuss, such as greater openness and greater influence for small businesses and ngos: however, one important dimension is missing: the green paper is not green enough. i am missing the whole dimension of environmental dumping, let me remind you of paragraph 11 of the muscardini report from october: the european parliament calls on the commission to consider whether a comprehensive review of the rules on the use of trade defence measures in the wto would be appropriate. this is intended to include non-compliance with global environmental and social agreements and agreements as a form of dumping or subsidization. countries with weak environmental legislation or lacking the environmental taxes of competitors must be considered as subsidising or dumping production costs just like your traditional dumping. this will become a growing problem as global ambition increases. then there will be no free zones for environmental destruction that undermine global environmental work. we must, for example, introduce a kyoto tariff for countries which do not comply with the kyoto agreement. then we'll have others dragged before the wto and maybe we win, maybe we lose. many people think the wto is powerful, but one thing is certain: the climate is totally disregarded by the wto and will change regardless of what the wto thinks if we do not incorporate climate action into trade policy. a final comment: if we are to have solidarity and we have a 1% threshold, when will malta be able to apply these safeguard measures, when will they reach more than 1% of internal trade?
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. these reports call for the developing countries to deregulate, to give european companies power in public procurement, to protect european and north american patents, to do everything possible to protect the investments of european large companies. one of the reports even calls for the establishment of a free trade area, and i quote 'without excluding any sector'. suck on that wording. no, this is not the way we're going. the more deregulation, the better it may be for big business, but the worse it is for those who are to be protected by the laws being removed, i.e. workers, the environment and local small businesses. two examples: it's good for monsanto if they can patent crops in south america, but bad for farmers and the environment, and good for european healthcare companies if the healthcare sector is exposed to competition, but bad for those who can't afford to pay for healthcare. there is an alternative: fair trade instead of unfettered free trade, cooperation and security instead of competition and market madness. that is what the people of europe and latin america need. i shall now conclude with the position of the united left group, namely that we abstain.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it's a matter of protocol. yesterday, when i was going to speak about the report on 'trade defence instruments', i wanted to quote the muscardini report from october and then i discovered that an important piece was missing, and i do not know what we are going to do now. it is from october 2006, but there is a serious error in the swedish version because the whole following text is missing in paragraph 11 and i wonder how we can correct it. the following text is missing from the chinese version: with a view to including non-compliance with global social and environmental agreements or international covenants as forms of dumping or subsidy. if the whole dimension of environmental and social dumping is missing from the swedish version, the swedish people will have a wrong picture of what parliament wants.
in writing. the report points in the right direction but contains a couple of bad proposals, including in paragraph 2, which goes against our mandate from our electors. it is important that all eu decisions be taken with respect for fundamental human rights. the report criticises the previous agreements concluded by the commission with the us authorities on the transfer of passenger data. the rapporteur points out the illegality of the secret prisoner transfers carried out by the cia on eu territory and stresses the importance of ensuring that similar human rights violations are not repeated. the report clearly shows that a european constitution is not necessary for the protection of human rights in the member states.
in writing. the report contains a lot of talk about the eu being constantly evolving and some positive writing about the constitution and more power for the new agency. this is not conducive to democracy and development. but these are standard texts which are constantly appearing in the reports of the constitutional committee. the thrill of this is the requirement that the committee on human rights be able to stop eu legislation that threatens them. this is extremely important as ever-new terrorist laws increasingly threaten the very society they are meant to protect. that is why i am voting in favour, despite the shortcomings.
in writing. the junilists believe that freer trade is good for world prosperity. the idea of free trade is that the more people involved, the better the outcome for the parties involved. this own-initiative report contains many meritorious elements which could lead to increased prosperity. unfortunately, as so often happens in this congregation, irrelevant and negative writings are added. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the context of the directive. unfortunately, the aspects just mentioned overshadow the desirable that could be achieved. i therefore voted against the report in today's vote.
in writing. free trade is the most important means of prosperity in the world and the more countries that participate in such free trade, the more prosperity increases in the countries that participate. bringing large parts of south and central america into a free trade area with the eu is therefore a major step towards prosperity and poverty eradication. this own-initiative report therefore contains many worthwhile proposals which, if implemented, could lead to increased prosperity. as is so often the case in this house, it unfortunately also contains a great deal of writing, the aim of which is to promote the development of the european union into a federal superpower and to increase the power of parliament at the expense of the member states. the european parliament has been asked to make a statement on the situation in the middle east, and it has been asked to make a statement on the situation in the middle east. despite these strong objections, i believe that it is better to conclude these regional association agreements than to stop them.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, last year i went to see the seal hunt in canada on behalf of the animal welfare intergroup. the salesmen tried to force me to stop seeing what was going on. they ran our car off the road, they sat on our helicopters so we couldn't fly, they threatened to kill us if we left our hotel to try and document the seal hunt. despite these methods, we were able to obtain photographs and we saw the incredible ferocity of the seal hunt. they knew they had something to hide. the sellers are skinned alive, they are clubbed and left to suffocate on their own blood, they are shot and drowned and shot but suffer extra hardship, because a second mercy shot costs two dollars because the fur then drops in value. it is not for conservation reasons that we should ban this hunting, it is for ethical reasons. i'd still like to say, however, that the population has declined since the last time there was a mass hunt. and now, faced with climate change, the greenland seal needs ice cover to feed its young. many babies are aborted in the water today. as the world's largest market, the eu supports this hunt and parliament has made it clear that we do not want this on our market. as bowis said, many countries have imposed national bans. when it came to cat and dog fur, the commission said: the internal market is not working, we are imposing an eu ban. the same logic applies here. does the commission want studies? there are many studies showing the problems mentioned here in the house, not least the bird and veterinary report, but these must not be a delay in necessary action. so if you want that time, we can give you that time, but then we want a temporary injunction in place until you're done, and you can find the legal basis on which to build it. i thank you for choosing the efsa animal welfare committee to carry out the study. it has been approved and has become independent and representative of the will of the countries. i would point out that in the countries that are hunting seals - canada, norway, russia and namibia - this is in no way of vital economic interest. we see from whaling that when it was abolished and replaced by whale watching it was more profitable, so we can also help the commercial hunters themselves to have a better economy if they can stop this cruel management.
a writer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. despite many problems, the situation has improved since the fall of the military dictatorship in 1999. it is therefore remarkable that a law is suddenly being drafted which is detrimental to human rights. already, homosexuality is outlawed and punishable by anything from fines to 14 years in prison, and we know that there are state-level sharia laws that give 100 lashes for an unmarried person and even the death penalty for a married person. now they want to ban not only gay weddings, but also to be a witness or to help with the planning of the wedding, with up to five years in prison. can anyone here explain to me how it could be life-threateningly subversive to witness a declaration of love between two people? i can't see how that would be a criminal offense. since the law also prohibits any form of support for homosexuals to organize, lawyers and human rights activists are also at risk, making it impossible to debate the rights of homosexuals. one of the things that is actually happening today is that there is, for example, enough tolerance for homosexuality that the largest newspaper, this day, has to have lesbian personals. this law has nothing to do with the spread of aids, as some argue the vast majority of those infected are heterosexual and even if it had such reasons they would be foolish, because fixed relationships reduce the spread of infection and in such cases should encourage and facilitate marriage between homosexuals. nigeria needs more love and solidarity and it is to kick off a retreat against dictatorship and oppression if these types of laws are passed.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the question of introducing a european tax has been raised, as there are apparently those who believe that the eu has far too little money. the aim is to solve this by allowing the eu to levy a tax directly from the pockets of its citizens. in paragraph 6 of the report, criticism is made of the requirement that all member states must agree on such matters. obviously, countries that are reluctant to do so can be overrun. this is a regrettable position, not least from a democratic point of view. the junilists strongly oppose the eu's share of national taxes. the report is intended to take another step towards an eu state with taxation rights, a common foreign minister, a common military and a common currency - a terrible idea! we have tabled an amendment in which we emphasize the inviolable right of the member states to decide for themselves in the field of taxation. we believe that all member states must agree to the introduction of some form of eu taxation. this is in line with the opinion of citizens in several eu countries. we europeans must listen to the wishes of our clients, that is to say, the views of the citizens, and act accordingly. i hope that we, ladies and gentlemen, will say a clear and unequivocal no to this report when we vote tomorrow.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the financing of the eu budget has become a mess. only a few experts understand how the system works. but one thing we do know, it's short-term and it's unfair. there are therefore strong reasons to reform it towards fairness, transparency and long-term sustainability. alain lamassoure has produced an important report with which we swedish social democrats agree in all essential respects. we also want to find a simple, straightforward and fairer financing system, like a gnp-based system without rebates. we do not want to give the eu taxing rights or to undermine the sovereignty of the member states in tax matters. for me, the specific character of the eu is precisely its ability to combine fundamental national sovereignty with the ability to combine power in certain areas in order to solve cross-border social problems. to create a genuine eu tax would be to anticipate events. if we are ever to go down that road, the conviction that it is beneficial must come from below, from the citizens and the member states. that's not where we are today. i am glad that we in the socialist group have come a long way towards a more common vision which closely follows the line of lamassoure. we have therefore a broad consensus in parliament and this could be very important for the future.
in writing. we have voted against certain amendments concerning reduced percentages for gmo labelling of organic products. although we believe that organic products should be gmo-free, we are concerned that different limits for organic and other foods will be a disadvantage for organic production. we believe that gmos should be treated with caution and that measures should be taken to reduce the risk of accidental contamination, but we do not want to create an unnecessarily heavy burden of proof which could lead to a reduction in organic farming.
in writing. organic production is very important from both an environmental and a health point of view. however, we have voted against the report because we believe that agricultural products should be sold on a free market without interference at eu level. we are convinced that the free market forces, with conscious european consumers at the forefront, will be the ones to cope with the urgent need to make the transition to organic and long-term sustainable agriculture. we also believe that this development will be accelerated and that its impact will be greater if the labelling of organic products is left to the national parliaments.
in writing. we support the un's efforts to bring peace to kosovo and the work of matti ahtisaari. we voted against the statement that sovereignty for kosovo is the best way to achieve stability and a political solution for kosovo. we believe that it is not the right report to bring this up and that it is foolish to lock in such a formulation at the moment, even though it is a desirable objective in the future.
in writing. the junilists believe that the issue of kosovo must be resolved through the un, which is also about to happen. if the un then asks the eu to play a major role in this matter, we will be in favour of it. this report contains some good words, but also many bad ones. we are opposed, among other things, to the european parliament's wish to have a supervisory role in the question of establishing the status of kosovo. this is not a matter for the eu. the european parliament also called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's external relations are not jeopardised by the threat of a nuclear war. it may be desirable, but it is the serbian people who choose their government and this must be respected in the name of democracy, whatever the outcome. furthermore, the european parliament shall not have any say in the position of member states in the council of ministers, or in the way member states act in the un security council. we have voted against the report as a whole in today's vote because we felt that there were too many bad elements in the report.
in writing. we think the report is good in general. however, we have voted against all the proposals for a european tax. we also chose to support co-financing under the union's agricultural policy.
in writing. i abstained on the lamassoure report on the future of the eu's own resources. i agree that the eu's revenue and expenditure system needs to be reformed and made more transparent, but this report goes too far. i insist that the eu be financed through membership dues and do not want to see a move towards a european tax.
in writing. we supported the report on the future of professional football in europe. it is essentially a good report, which also addresses the problem of the need for smaller football clubs to have better financial conditions for youth activities. the report also mentions the possibility of national teams using players free of charge. other important aspects of the report relate to the fight against the exploitation of young players, gang violence, racism, doping and corruption. we believe that the eu should work with the governing bodies of football at national and european level (especially uefa) to solve these problems. however, we do not see today the need for any new legal instruments to solve the problems, with the exception of a possible directive on gambling agents. we also do not see the need for member states to change their social and tax legislation because of the differences between countries that create problems in the cross-border transfer of footballers. furthermore, we interpret the term 'forced prostitution' in the report as covering all prostitution, since all prostitution is done under some form of coercion.
in writing. this report should not have been discussed in the european parliament at all. this is a matter for the national football associations and their partner organisations and for the respective national parliaments. we also object to the fact that the draft report refers, among other things, to the draft eu constitution, which has already been rejected in two european referendums, to the drafting of a legal framework at eu level for football and to the consideration of the introduction of an eu legal status for sports clubs. the junilist therefore votes against this report.
in writing. we believe that the common agricultural policy should be abolished. it is unreasonable to introduce new member states into an outdated system and to accustom them to the rules and subsidies of that system. we are increasing financial aid to the new member states of the union, but this must be channelled to the less favoured regions and focused on education, infrastructure and judicial institutions.
mr president, we are against the motion for a resolution and whether we should link it to the brussels part-session, because we take the view that important issues should be dealt with in committee. we have committees in order to make the decisions of parliament strong, important and reliable and therefore we stick to that. an important issue should be dealt with in committee. that is why we have committees in this parliament.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is a parallel proposal dealing with serious environmental offences, clearly defined offences which cause serious harm to people and cause death. despite the clear aim, the proposal is controversial, as the eu is going into criminal law. we have here a bill that aims to protect commercial interests without risk to consumers and the environment. however, this definition is much broader and creates legal uncertainty. the proposal, in its present form, is not about fighting organised crime. on the contrary, this law is itself organized crime. it is a violation of human rights and freedoms and an attack on natural communication between consumers and businesses. take the betamax example. mp3 players can now be used for copying. so you're supposed to prove that these are not criminal before you sell them? when sold, will consumers face technical limitations and barriers to using the products, music and films they have purchased? the proposal is an unreasonable attack on consumer rights which must be rejected in its entirety.
commissioner, what is it? mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission's annual policy strategy shows that we are facing major and partly new challenges. i am thinking of the climate issue, which requires action in the fields of research, the environment and energy. i am thinking of research and innovation, where it is a question of creating a europe which, according to the lisbon spirit, should be as attractive to researchers as the united states, and offer equal opportunities to translate research results into jobs and production. the implementation of the cip innovation programme and a world-class european institute of technology are key issues. i think about the balkans. we have an applicant country, croatia, which wants to join soon and still has great security needs in bosnia. we have brand new countries, including montenegro and perhaps kosovo, which will require major eu action. i am thinking of democracy and communication, which require wider popular cooperation between individuals, parties and movements. with the european elections, the debate on a new treaty and more, 2008 must be a breakthrough year for the resources of the eu's new communication strategy. the picture of what the eu does and wants must be clearer, not least in the member states. these challenges require the eu to be able to adopt political decisions, allocate budgetary resources and carry out activities that will enable us to move our positions forward. but, honestly, we've got a long way to go. the union's budget is still rigid. it is difficult to change priorities and, above all, to implement new priorities, even if the principle of redeploying staff to new political priorities is gaining ground. in the long term, perhaps already at the mid-term review, we need to have greater opportunities to invest vigorously in new areas and to reduce correspondingly in others. in today's world, the demands for change are increasing for everyone, for companies, individuals, regions and the eu as a whole. so seven years, which our long-term budget spans, is oceans of time. we need better links between legislative work and the budget. i think the eit, the european institute of technology, is an example of this. the institute is a great idea, but it becomes problematic if other research efforts have to be reduced. i mean, the rules are still often both bureaucratic and difficult to manage. the layout of the forms and the level of detail in the audit cannot be characterised as modern management, nor can the administration. many people were enthusiastic about commissioner kinnock's ambitions for a flatter organisation, better staffing policies and greater equality between women and men. now we must continue to reform in his spirit. the commission's annual policy strategy identifies the right areas, but now it is time to go on the offensive and implement them. we must then be more daring and perhaps parliament will have to play that role.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. a core element of the democratic process is the demand for accountability. the chamber's past record in this area is appalling. despite repeated reports of fraudulent behavior, irregularities, incompetence and waste, the house grants year after year a discharge with near-north korean majorities. last year, we had an exception. parliament had paid too much rent for the building we are in now. it became impossible to sweep the problem under the carpet. instead, the pathetic situation arose that the leaders of the big groups acted like "public mourners". they assured me that their refusal to grant an exemption was really just a pretense. that is how the majority of the house is managing its democratic mission. last year i was rapporteur and found it impossible to propose a discharge to the committee of the regions. a culture of corruption seemed to permeate its secretariat. although three separate investigations by the committee of the regions were still ongoing, the chamber granted a discharge. the olaf report has now been submitted. the case has been brought to the attention of national authorities. there are therefore now even stronger reasons for not granting the committee of the regions discharge this year. in addition, the rapporteur, mr herczog, has shown that neither the european maritime safety agency nor the european food safety authority should be granted a discharge. so say no to this!
mr president, i would like to thank you. between 1998 and 2005, according to official statistics, more than 215 000 people were infected with hiv in the eu. in europe, nearly 650 000 people were infected. many of them were under 25 years of age. in a way, this makes me very tired and sad. it also upsets me greatly because, as mr kyprianou said earlier, this is happening unnecessarily. these people could have avoided being infected. that is why i am very pleased that we are having this debate today. i am very pleased with the report we are voting on and with the fact that the commission and the council have indicated their willingness to give priority to these issues. the lack of knowledge about the transmission of hiv and aids is accessible. this only shows that we need to invest even more in information and openness about sexuality in general. we have to have the courage to talk about gender equality and sexual self-determination. the fact that it is above all the most vulnerable groups in society that are affected by hiv and the spread of infection shows that we need to work in new ways to really reach all groups in society. the fact that vulnerable groups are affected also poses very serious challenges to our work to combat the stigmatisation of people living with hiv. we have to prioritize this very, very hard. i am also very pleased that the report highlights the support for the global fund to fight aids, tuberculosis and malaria. it is a very important work which i hope we can support more. i am also very pleased that the report and several amendments point to the importance of combatting tuberculosis and hiv in parallel. i am grateful for the work we have pledged to do together to counter this completely unnecessary spread of infection.
in writing. the junilist believes that greater democratic, judicial and budgetary control of europol is desirable. however, since the europol convention is an intergovernmental cooperation, it is the national parliaments and not the european parliament which are to exercise this control. that is why we have voted against the report.
in writing. we have voted against this report. the junilist believes that a comprehensive reform of the eu sugar market is necessary to liberalise the market as soon as possible and to allow non-eu sugar producing countries to compete on fair terms. it is therefore unreasonable to drag new member states into a doomed system and to accustom them to the rules of the system. as romania and bulgaria have a functioning sugar market, they should be allowed to maintain it and not be included in the eu common organisation of the sugar market. instead, that common organisation should be abolished.
in writing. we oppose the present common agricultural policy and we oppose changes of little consequence in principle to the existing system. we are calling for a comprehensive review and reconsideration of the whole of the cap, and it is difficult to single out individual areas such as the matter now before the vote. we believe that the quota system for potato starch should not be extended at all, but that the eu must abolish all agricultural quota systems. as in may 2005, the federalist majority in parliament continues to advocate the extension of the quota system. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament.

in writing. we swedish social democrats have voted in favour of report a6-0094/2007 on the discharge for the european parliament for 2005. the vote must be seen as a partial success, although some of the amendments on increased transparency of the pension conditions of members of the european parliament which we supported in the vote were rejected. we swedish social democrats have chosen not to be part of the european parliament's pension scheme. we believe the system is immoral in design and have therefore chosen to remain outside it. however, we do not think it necessary to vote against the whole report because these amendments were rejected, since pensions only concern a very small part of parliament's activities.
in writing. we swedish social democrats chose to support amendment no 1 on charging the council for the cost of the european parliament's activities in strasbourg, because the council denies parliament the right to decide its own seat. the best solution, however, would be for parliament itself to decide, and for parliament to choose to operate solely in brussels.

in writing. we voted in favour of amendments nos 43 and 44 and against the report in the final vote, as we consider that there is insufficient evidence of a legal basis under the first pillar of common criminal law in the field of intellectual property. the commission's extensive interpretation of the ecj judgment in case c-176/03 has been challenged and cannot, without further investigation, be extended in this way to the field of intellectual property.
in writing. the report also calls for the eu to be able to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action to combat terrorism. in cases where member states have nevertheless chosen to cooperate in this area (e.g. certain crossborder crimes), the decisions are taken by the council and the legal basis is therefore to be found in the third and intergovernmental pillar of the eu (judicial and internal affairs) and not in the first pillar, which is the supranational ec law. as long as we do not have a constitutional treaty on fundamental rights at eu level, we cannot have a common european criminal law either.
in writing. the junilists have repeatedly pointed out that criminal justice should not be a matter for the eu. this has been largely confirmed by the judgment of the court of justice of the european communities of 13 september 2005 in case c-176/03 commission v council. the rapporteur considers instead that the initiative to impose sanctions at eu level is 'fully consistent with the commission's broad interpretation of the ecj judgment'. the report is not legally defensible. we are all about freedom of speech and the right to exchange information. it is clear that the commission and many meps are bowing to the special interests of the powerful music and film industries. they do so without regard for either the clear interpretation of the ec court of justice of the eu's powers or the legal certainty of citizens. apart from a few amendments, which the junilist supports, it is difficult to find anything that speaks in favour of citizens' right to freedom of expression and the exchange of information. that is why we have decided to abstain on the amendments where we had to choose between plague and cholera. the junilista group welcomes the protection of copyright but considers the commission's proposal to be a threat to democracy. the junilist therefore votes against the report as a whole.
in writing. we have voted against this report today. the main message is that we must drastically reduce our use of natural resources and that the best method of achieving this reduction is through comprehensive policy regulation. we moderates are skeptical of this. instead, we believe that the sustainable use of natural resources requires clear ownership rights, which allow the use of natural resources to be governed by market mechanisms, not political decisions. the use of natural resources in a market economy system encourages far greater household and technological development than can be provided by political regulation. human life and creation are meant to leave footprints. the triumph of humanity is that we have developed ideas and technologies that have increased productivity and reduced world poverty by two-thirds in 50 years. we moderates believe that through production and trade we can not only end poverty but also improve our environment. it is wealth and technology that gives us the will and the means to do so.
in writing. i am voting for tax reform as a matter of principle, even though it is unfortunately formulated in the text. i believe that taxes on capital and consumption are beneficial to welfare and justice and that the eu should allow member states to exchange taxes.
in writing. i have today voted in favour of amendment no 3 by the green group to the liotard report on the sustainable use of natural resources. i support the principle of redistributing tax revenues from growth-inhibiting taxes on labour, capital and consumption to taxes on activities with a negative environmental impact. in addition, taxation should be switched from labour taxes to taxes on alcohol and tobacco. however, i could not support liotard's report in its entirety. the reason for this is a number of unfortunate writings. among other things, long-distance transport of agricultural products and consumer goods is not desirable. the trade that these transports have resulted in has helped millions of people out of poverty. what should be limited instead are the emissions generated by these transports.
in writing. we believe that enlargement of the european union is a positive thing. however, this cannot happen until the candidate countries have de facto fulfilled all the requirements for membership. the latest enlargement, with the accession of romania and bulgaria, was premature, as the countries and their systems were not yet ready for it. croatia also has a long way to go, including ongoing reforms of its public administration and judiciary, before membership can be considered. it is good to see progress being made, but we must not rush through this important and irreversible process, for the sake of croatia as well as the eu. it is also regrettable that the european parliament is using something as important as enlargement to promote the eu constitution in an undemocratic way. recital g states that the current draft constitutional treaty should enter into force despite the clear and unequivocal opposition of the french and dutch populations. in addition, paragraph 7 points out that 'public support for eu accession is waning in croatia'. if that is the case and the majority of croats are opposed to eu membership, then in the name of democracy, eu accession should not be implemented. we have therefore voted against this report.
