for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur for a very good and extremely important report. we in the gue/ngl group believe that the situation of women with disabilities requires attention and action. disabled people are discriminated against, and women are discriminated against. disabled women are thus doubly discriminated against, not to mention the additional discrimination that exists if you happen to be a woman, disabled, of a different ethnic background and of a different sexual orientation. it is the responsibility of the member states and society to remove all obstacles to full participation in society for all people. particular attention must be paid to the situation of women with disabilities. the disabled person or his or her family members can never be responsible. we have a collective, shared responsibility to shape a society that is solidary and where we take responsibility for the full participation of all people. this applies in all contexts, including studies, work, social and cultural life. in several member states, women bear the main responsibility for the care of disabled children and disabled relatives. i saw a quote from a mother of a disabled child who was very vocal. she put it this way: i am not only a mother but also a physical therapist, an occupational therapist, a nurse, a neurologist, a teacher and a home improvement worker. women are not only carers but also have to fight for the rights of the disabled. i think it is very clear from this report that women often bear the main responsibility. this is not acceptable. it is not the responsibility of women but of society to provide all disabled people and their families with the conditions for independent life and self-determination. it is also a gender equality requirement. physically and mentally disabled girls and women are more vulnerable to violence and sexual abuse than other groups. so not only are they discriminated against because of their disability, but they are also abused. women with mental disabilities are particularly vulnerable, often to the abuse of someone in their immediate vicinity. it's a terrible deprivation these women live under. we need to see and expose this violence in order to stop the abuse. it is not our compassion that women with disabilities need or demand, it is their self-evident right to participate fully in society.
in writing. this report deals with the community strategy to promote the urgent objective of improving the situation of women with disabilities in the eu. we have voted against the report because we have confidence that the national parliaments of the member states will deal with this important matter in an appropriate manner. we are opposed in principle to the general trend of the eu institutions seeking to gain influence and competence in an increasing number of areas.
in writing. i voted in favour of the report because i believe that the rabit mechanism is necessary to respond to the extraordinary migratory pressure that some of the eu member states are periodically subjected to. rabit is based on volunteerism and is not an attempt to build walls against the outside world. i believe that it is rather important for the member states to work together to combat effectively trafficking in human beings and illegal smuggling, which are often associated with the danger to the lives of those who have been forced to leave their home countries in desperation. thousands of people die each year trying to reach europe by unsafe means. rabit is part of the effort to prevent people from dying at the hands of cynical human traffickers. as a social democrat, i am committed to a generous and humane refugee policy and want to increase the opportunities for people to seek legal asylum in europe.
in writing. we have voted against the report because we believe that the tonnage redistribution levels proposed by the commission are sufficient. we also question the increased support for an already over-established sector. the eu already has a large overcapacity in its fishing fleet and limited fisheries resources. the difficulties in checking that the fishing capacity does not increase in the event of conversion, which have been discussed in the council, are therefore one of the reasons why we have chosen not to support an increase in these levels.
in writing. we believe that human rights are universal and inviolable, and that they must be one of the cornerstones of all societies. unfortunately, violations occur in many places around the world, including in the eu member states. a large proportion of the european parliament and its members see themselves as the guarantors of human rights in the world. there should be no shadow cast on them, because they are certainly well-intentioned and it is necessary to fight for these fundamental rights. the problem arises when human rights are used as a means of advancing eu positions on, for example, the development of a common foreign and security policy. we have voted against a number of amendments which we consider to be logical, reasonable and in some cases even desirable, but which should under no circumstances be expressed or decided by this house.
in writing. we voted in favour of this resolution because it expresses the importance of preventing discrimination on grounds of sexual orientation. we do not support the conclusions of the resolution in the parts which propose greater powers and decision-making powers for the eu. our support for this resolution is only a vote against the abhorrent discrimination against people on the grounds of sexual orientation. we are opposed to all the conclusions of the resolution on the question of more powers for the eu in various areas.
author of the motion for a resolution. president. - the debate is closed. last year, a record number of journalists were kidnapped and killed around the world. it is now our duty to ensure that that record is not beaten in 2007. it is our duty to help alan johnston be released. i address his family, relatives and colleagues, and i must say that my hopes for his release are high. we know that palestinian leaders condemn the kidnapping. we know the kidnappers don't have the support of the palestinian people. we know they understand that alan johnston has done more than anyone to report on their suffering over the last 16 years. with these factors behind us, we can also help to get him released. i've been to gaza myself, and i know how hard it is there. but its best chance of getting us all to help the people to a better situation is to make sure that as many journalists as possible are there and that as many as possible are writing about what is happening there. since the palestinian people know that this kidnapping is against their interests, i am sure that the kidnappers will not receive any support and will eventually have to release alan johnston.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the eu's structure and cohesion policy are seriously flawed. its existence should therefore be strongly questioned, primarily for three reasons. firstly, these measures are artificial breathing which do not lead to longterm and sustainable development. we must accept that the world is changing as a result of globalisation. whoever's competitive today could be out of the game tomorrow. the second reason concerns funding. money flows from the poor in rich countries to the rich in poor countries. this is not acceptable either. thirdly and lastly, the rules governing the allocation of structural funds are far too rigid and narrow. the member states, regions and local authorities therefore have problems in being able to use the money where it is really needed. the situation in the eu member states varies widely. therefore, the current policy is not sustainable. i therefore believe that structural policy should be re-nationalised.
in writing. the junilists believe, as is well known, that neither the eu nor its institutions should conduct foreign policy. a pattern that is repeated in the european parliament is that relations with a third country start at the level of trade policy. we are positive about this. the problem arises when policy areas are extended from being trade-only to, for example, foreign, aid or fisheries policy. the eu will be strictly concerned with trade issues as well as cross-border environmental issues. amendment no 9 by the verts/ale group is desirable in itself. however, it should not be dealt with by the european parliament, as it is a matter of foreign policy. ahtisaari has, on behalf of the un, drawn up a plan for kosovo. however, it is not the task of this assembly to give its opinion on either the plan or the course of action to be taken by russia. given the problems in russia, including the lack of respect for human rights and the rule of law, the expansion of trade is positive. however, when it comes to purely foreign policy issues, it is the national parliaments and governments and the un that should lead the way.
in writing. the european parliament has today adopted a position on how the eu should act to bring about reforms in the arab world. we strongly oppose this, because this kind of issue must be resolved through the united nations. the alde group's amendment no 20 proposes that the eu should not support 'fundamentalist and extreme nationalist' movements. this goes without saying, but the eu should not be pursuing its foreign policy by supporting movements in other parts of the world. that is why we voted against that amendment. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. the eu as an institution should definitely not have views on the system of another country. as we do not consider this to be an eu issue, we voted against the report as a whole in today's vote.
in writing. since the european union is also a union of values which must safeguard the human rights of every individual within the union, we have chosen to vote in favour of this report. we believe that this is a reasonable balance between pointing out what we consider to be the ethical-political necessity for a member state and respecting the sovereignty of the member states. we therefore consider it to be the duty of denmark to take medical responsibility for those who, on the orders of the danish state, carried out work which may have caused radiation injuries in connection with the thule accident in 1968.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report on housing and regional policy. it is positive that the report highlights the role of housing policy in strengthening social and territorial cohesion. housing policy is an important instrument for member states to combat segregation, and the right to housing is fundamental. we believe that housing policy is an important part of the welfare policy of the member states. in sweden, the utility is a socially motivated form of enterprise, and the swedish rental legislation is a social protection legislation. the eu should consider the housing policies of the member states as an integral part of welfare policy and thus exempt them from competition rules on state aid. we also believe that the eu should define the concept of social housing broadly, so that it also covers the swedish model of public housing.
in writing. today we voted in the european parliament on a report which describes the need for housing of reasonable quality and affordable rent for all. for me, as a liberal, it is of course very important that people have a roof over their heads, but this is a matter that should be dealt with locally or regionally, not at eu level. that's why i dropped my vote today.
member of the commission. madam president, i am very pleased to have the opportunity to discuss this issue again with you. we have discussed it on numerous occasions, including in committee. we are dealing with a real situation today. on the one hand, there are a series of judgments by the european court of justice and the jurisprudence created in this field and, on the other, the decision of the european parliament, which the commission has accepted and agreed to, not to include health services in the services directive. as promised, at the time when the debate was taking place and upon the exclusion of health services from the services directive, we initiated action in a specific area of healthcare at european level; hence our initiative to begin with a consultation document, to start public consultation and then to continue with a more specific proposal. the public consultation is finished and we already have the results. we have already had two ministerial discussions on this issue and, with your debate today, we will have a more complete picture of the position not only of the institutions but also of the european citizens and we will then be ready for the next phase, which will be the drafting of the proposal. i can assure you that today’s discussion and of course the report will make a significant contribution as to how we will proceed in this respect. therefore i should like to thank the rapporteur and congratulate her on a very thorough and comprehensive report and i should also like to thank the other committees for contributing to it. we are in the middle of this process, which allows us to bring the setting of policies back to the policymakers – those who have the mandate to decide and propose policies will do so in this important area. as i have said, we have finished the consultation process. we had more than 300 contributions from member states, regional authorities, organisations representing patients and professionals as well as healthcare providers – even hospitals and individual citizens. even though there were different views depending on the background of each contribution, there was nevertheless one common approach: there is added value if european action is taken in this respect. the debate goes beyond patient mobility and covers many other areas, such as information for patients, patients’ rights, the movement of professionals, cooperation of healthcare assistants, centres of excellence, exchange of best practice in all areas that could contribute to effective cross-border healthcare for the benefit of patients, the citizens – our foremost concern – without creating any unnecessary burden on the healthcare systems in the member states. all these contributions and your report today will be a very important reference point for our next step, which will be a specific proposal. we recognise that health services have a specificity – they are distinct from other services in the european union – and therefore the challenge is how to make a choice between the internal market and social values and put in place a framework that can bring both the benefits of freedom of movement on the one hand and respect for health objectives and social values on the other, especially as this was recently confirmed by the health ministers at the informal council meeting in aachen. i believe that parliament’s report broadly reflects the issues that were identified by many stakeholders and the ministers. this underlines the need to take action at european level. concerning the instrument we can use, as i said at the beginning, last year health services were excluded from the scope of the services directive at the request of the european parliament, and the commission was invited to come up with specific proposals on health services. the commission agreed to this approach and therefore it does not intend to reopen the discussion regarding possible reinclusion in the services directive. on the contrary, we are now in the final stages of preparing a specific proposal directed at those specific issues. it will be a package with various measures, but the first one will comprise, as called for in the report, specific legislative proposals in this area. we will follow this up with various other steps. however, as i have said, the main objective for all of us is the benefit of european citizens, of european patients, with the principle of subsidiarity always being taken into account.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. this case is not about the services directive, although you might think so by listening to the debate. it is really a question of how we are going to solve the problems caused by the fact that health services are not covered by the services directive and, not least, how patients and service providers are going to be able to maintain their crossborder rights despite this. today's rights are based on the treaties and on different cases and must not be undermined by secondary legislation, at least not without the citizens being informed about it. it is not a question of introducing new services or new rights, but of upholding the rule of law and existing rights. according to the court, the order for reference is a standard method of limitation. we in the ppe-de group do not want to increase the use of advance notice. we consider that the few cases which the court has accepted as legitimate obstacles to the principle of free movement of persons and services are sufficient. some advanced health care requires planning with fixed structures and funding. i think that the member states must have a certain degree of freedom of movement in this area. as many speakers have already pointed out, there are great differences between the member states. the commission should choose the appropriate instruments to deal with the different elements of this major package and focus on solutions that promote mobility, freedom and security for the individual eu citizen. it is the people who must be protected, not the national bureaucracy. we are not opposed to the fact that specialisation in the eu leads to a higher quality of care for patients. as european citizens, we have crossborder solutions to tackle. we have a right to them as patients and as staff. the commission is responsible for proposing solutions, and the council is also responsible for the implementation of the european social fund. i call on the commission to codify the case law and to emphasise the responsibility of the member states for the content of health care and not to accept fewer rights for citizens than today. parliament and the commission must stand side by side.
it is not long since the two major groups came to a compromise on the services directive and some described the exemption for health care as a tremendous success. now, however, this deregulation is being tried behind the scenes, which means that people's health and care are being transformed from human rights into commodities in a market. under the treaties, health care is the exclusive responsibility of the member states and legislation at eu level is neither required nor desirable. cooperation is good, but not legislation in this case. i hope that those who thought that the removal of health care from the services directive was a success will make sure to follow up that success by supporting the gue/ngl amendments when we vote.
i would like to thank mrs gröner and commissioner frattini for their excellent work. i was responsible in the libe committee for sending this report back to the women's committee where it belongs. for us swedish social democrats daphne is a matter of the heart because it was swedish commissioner anita gradin who was so strongly committed to the eu's role in combating violence against women. as long as men are committing violence against women and children, as long as there is sex trafficking of women and young girls, as long as not all women in the eu have the right to free abortion, as long as women in the eu are victims of honour violence and as long as women's rights in the eu need to be strengthened, we social democratic women will continue to fight for daphne and for the programme to receive proper financial support. i regret that we did not receive support for the establishment of a network of children's ombudsmen, but i will return to this issue with commissioner frattini and others when we decide on the children's strategy. the great thing about daphne is that it is an opportunity for organisations to try out new ideas and to be inspired by good examples that we can use in the fight against violence across the eu.
first of all, i'd like to start by thanking daniel caspary. i have to say that this is one of the best reports on trade policy that i have seen in this parliament. it is clearly stated here that free trade spreads prosperity and that protectionism spreads poverty. it is a good step forward that we can say this together in the european parliament! the commission's global europe initiative on the external dimension of competitiveness is also very good. trade policy should have been part of the lisbon agenda from the outset. otherwise, to talk about european competitiveness without talking about our trade with the rest of the world will be mere words. and now that you're here, i'd like to take this opportunity to send you two comments, peter mandelson. firstly, i have noticed that the commission often talks about competitiveness, but then almost only about the importance of increasing market access and lowering tariffs in other countries. but it is just as important to increase access and opportunities for companies from other countries to come here and to lower our own tariffs. it would not only provide european consumers and businesses with cheaper goods, but also increase competition and thus strengthen our competitiveness vis-à-vis the rest of the world. so it is at least as important that we lower our own tariffs as it is that others do so. the second point i would like to make is the need to reform the trade defence instruments. on that point, i do not agree with the report before us. i understand that the pressure on the commission has been very great since this initiative was put forward. unfortunately, this pressure seems to be leading to a lower level of ambition on the part of the commission. the rhetoric has gone from talking about reform to talking about review, but i expect the commission to present a reform of the trade defence instruments that is worthy of the name. the idea that the eu is supposed to protect us from trade is as silly as it sounds. i also want to mention one of my favorite examples of absurd trade barriers, namely the eu's strange tariffs on low-energy light bulbs. because while we are worried about the greenhouse effect and talking about banning ordinary light bulbs, we are protected from importing low-energy light bulbs by high tariffs! no, peter mandelson, we must urgently reform the trade defence instruments in a major way!
there are three keywords that the commission needs to follow in the negotiations: listen, do not force and do not stress. we'll listen to their demands. these negotiations are not equal. you, peter mandelson, have hundreds of experts at your disposal. they have a few. we have enormous economic power. they're just a budding business. we can buy their entire business. they barely have enough money to buy food for the day. with such different conditions, it is important that we listen to their demands and try to meet them. the epa is supposed to be for their development, not our profit. so we should not force them. if they do not want to open up a market, then, as stated in paragraph 17 of the resolution, we should not force them to do so. all trade experts trained in neoliberal thinking believe that reduced tariffs are always good and that free trade is always better than fair, but the reality shows that this is not so. and that is the reality of our negotiating partners. a wrong liberalization can lead to people dying. you can explain your views, but let them make the decisions. if they're wrong, it's their fault. it is easier to live with the defects for which you are responsible and can change than to live in misery imposed by others. reciprocity is not necessary. let them decide. we can live with it and we can live without it, but they can die from it. finally, let's not stress. i therefore hope that the house will strike out recital f and approve amendment no 4. let the negotiations take the time they need and in the meantime let the system of generalised preferences continue. then they can continue to sell to us without having a sword of damocles hanging over them. we the 27 together with the acp countries form a dominant group in the wto. if we wish, we can say together that we need to extend the time for negotiations or to work out alternatives to economic partnership agreements.
in writing. i am voting in favour of the report because, unlike the commission's proposal, it preserves the possibility of national labelling, but i regret that it nevertheless advocates that eu labelling should be the dominant label.
for the gue/ngl group. the gue/ngl group naturally deplores the disproportionate reactions in russia and we naturally demand that russia comply with its international commitments under the vienna convention. that said, it must also be stated that no party is served by an escalation of the conflict which may result from acts or unannounced resolutions and statements. building bridges and dialogue between parties is better than erecting walls and barriers. it is also indisputable that the discrimination and the restriction of civil rights of the russian-speaking population in estonia is the main breeding ground for conflicts. the responsibility for the conflict we are discussing also lies with the government of estonia, which does not respect the civil rights of the russian-speaking population. it is responsible for the fact that no dialogue was held with the residents of tallinn before the monument was moved and that the significance of the monument to a huge number of people as an important reminder of all those who lost their lives in the fight against nazism and fascism was ignored. it is also regrettable that the escalation of the conflict was a reaction to the fact that the estonian authorities did not allow peaceful demonstrations and the further escalation was a consequence of the actions of the estonian police against the demonstrators. the estonian move to move an anti-nazi memorial and the remains of soldiers who died in the war against hitler's troops is perceived as a provocation by all those who think that the fight against nazism should be remembered in the future. no one has the right to rewrite history, and political views must not prevent serious analysis and understanding of how conflicts arise. only then can we solve conflicts in the future.
i think it is perfectly clear to everyone what has happened in tallinn since april this year. however, i'm not sure everyone understands what that means. the previous discussions in parliament on this matter indicate that not everyone understands why some have reacted so strongly. so let me explain. even at the time of his election as president, putin hinted that he would do everything in his power to restore russia to its former military strength and the power that he considered to be its rightful own. if i remember correctly, it was only a french newspaper that directly picked up the signals from russia. the other media didn't see them. we who speak russian and have experience of living near or under russia - we understood. over the years, however, it has become increasingly clear that developments in russia are going in the wrong direction, both in terms of human rights, media independence and political freedom. otto von habsburg, one of europe's most respected political figures, described it as a return to the stalinist era. he's 95 years old today, he has perspective, he remembers. but others seem to have forgotten. even in the european parliament there are members who seem to have forgotten the trials that the eastern european states had to endure on their way to freedom, independence and sovereignty. all the threats and boycotts, the interference in the internal affairs of other countries and the blackmailing by means of the lack of energy supplies seem to have been forgotten. some of these events have taken place quite recently and are taking place at this very moment in some of the countries which i refer to as the new europe. but some of the old europe seems to have forgotten its historical lesson - that only through a common foreign policy and solidarity and with one voice can we defend peace. this is not about the monument in tallinn. the debate is not about estonia. the debate is about europe and european sovereignty and independence.
elmar brok preaches the importance of the eu having a foreign minister and its own embassies around the world. he said that only 18 countries had ratified the constitutional treaty, but that it "must be fully ratified". taste that wording: 'it must be ratified in full'. this means that the will of the people of two of the eu's founding countries is being ignored. that is what happens here in strasbourg and in brussels. and as always, the mantra is that europe must speak with one voice. that means 27 votes to be silenced. what will the remaining voice say? to take one example: the invasion of iraq was supported by a coalition of will, including the uk, italy, spain, poland, the netherlands, denmark and several other countries. a united european union would have probably agreed to the invasion and german soldiers would have been forced into war against the determined will of the bundestag. think about it and think about it! to speak with one voice, there must be a people with one identity, but we europeans have different identities.
i would just like to raise a matter of overtime. all speakers must be treated equally. if everyone else is allowed to speak, then my colleague on the left must also be allowed to speak, even if he does not share the views of the president.
for the alde group. astrid lulling, you have many good points, but you also sometimes strike a blow, and you do so with this report. i will recommend to my group that we reject your proposal as a whole and follow the commission's lead. a report on alcohol price levels must also take account of the public health implications of increased drinking. but there's no mention of public health. the abolition of excise duties on alcohol and beer would send the wrong signal. we know that the harmful effects of alcohol are increasing and we know that more and more young people in europe are literally being torn apart, not just in the nordic countries. every day rivers of beer, wine and spirits roll into sweden, half a million cans of beer and 65,000 bottles of spirits. this is due to the wide differences in alcohol taxation. higher common excise duties within the eu can play an important role here. the cost margin matters a lot in this context because the consumers we are talking about are price sensitive. europe must not grow together through alcohol tourism. i am not asking the eu to impose swedish taxes, that would be foreign to me, but what i am asking for is consideration and solidarity also with other countries and their inhabitants. that is why excise duties on alcohol must be increasingly harmonised. we europeans drink the most alcohol in the world. last year the commission presented a strategy to reduce alcohol-related harm. if we in this house want to follow the commission's line, we have only one thing to do: reject this report.
for the verts/ale group. those who want to abolish the minimum tax on alcohol in the name of subsidiarity must accept that at the same time, in the name of subsidiarity, a restriction on the private importation of alcohol is allowed. otherwise, countries with high excise taxes will be completely flooded with cheap privately imported liquor and will have to pay the bill with high health care costs. without import restrictions, the country with the lowest taxes will be the one imposing its alcohol policy on everyone else. how can you bring 200 kg of wine, beer and spirits but only 15 kg of fresh fish across the eu's borders? furthermore, it is absurd to have zero tax on wine when there is tax on beer and spirits. it is even more absurd when sweden is taken to court for favouring the beer sector by imposing high taxes on wine. the eu is distorting the single market by its zero tax on wine. alcohol is not a normal commodity. it is irresponsible and uneconomic to see this as a matter of the internal market, as the committee does, without regard to public health.
heavy alcohol consumption is associated with severe social penalties, so-called negative externalities. to help us understand the problem, a parallel can be drawn to the taxes on motor fuel. these fuels are taxed because they have harmful effects on the environment. by imposing a tax, the environmental damage caused by incineration can be counteracted. in this way, the cost is internalized and the polluter pays. the same principle can be applied to the sale of alcohol. countries' health systems are being strained by alcohol-related diseases. the alcohol tax has two effects in this case, firstly it is consumed less by a product if it is taxed, and secondly the tax revenue represents an addition to the state treasury. this money can then be used to provide care for people with alcohol-related injuries. as far as the minimum cat is concerned, we believe that, where they exist, it is better to keep them than to remove them. nor should exceptions be made for some alcoholic beverages, as is done today.
i think that the commission's original proposal for a 1993 census was the best, but i realise that in order to reach a compromise in the council, a 2004 census is the only possible one. why am i in favour of the commission's proposal? yes, because of the arguments that have been made earlier. i want taxes, minimum taxes on environmentally harmful activities, because i want to reduce environmentally harmful activities. so is alcohol. alcohol costs us a lot in terms of public health. the commission has already proposed a number of measures to reduce the level of pollution. a strange argument has been made in this debate. it is said that this moderate increase would affect inflation. for this to be true in any one country, the consumption of alcohol and thus the alcohol problem there must be so enormous that it would really be time to raise the tax.
in the last 15 years, we have seen a radical change in europe with the reduction of mobile phone prices. there are few areas where prices have fallen as much as in this particular area. this has been true in particular of national calls, but also international ones, which is worth remembering on a day like today. the problem has been that, as the commissioner pointed out, we have 27 different markets instead of one. i therefore congratulate both the commissioner and the rapporteur, mr paul rübig, on the fact that we have today a decision which means increased transparency and also that we have a regulation of competition between mobile operators and retailers. it solves the crucial problem that has led to excessive roaming charges. this will create better conditions for competition. i think we should have stopped there and i regret that we now also have a proposal for consumer price regulation. all experience shows that price controls do not achieve the desired result. we risk locking the price trend around the regulated level. we risk reducing the pressure of increased competition between different operators to lower prices for both national calls and roaming. we risk not seeing the kind of rapid price reductions we have seen in the past. without exaggerating these risks, i would say that it would have been better if we had simply regulated where we have the problem, namely prices between retailers and operators. i say this because the task of the european union is not and should not be to regulate prices at the consumer level. the eu must do what the commissioner pointed out earlier today, namely to make sure that we go from 27 different markets to one market. that is the dynamic and that is what we should be focusing on, not regulating consumer prices but regulating so that we have a comprehensive and good competition between operators.
it is a fundamental principle that the european union is not responsible for regulating prices everywhere on the market. the treaty gives us the power and the responsibility to protect consumers, to ensure that the market functions and that we have fair and open competition. in this case, for many years we have received very clear signals from desperate parents with children studying abroad, elderly people who have not read the fine print in their telephone contracts and also from parts of the industry that this market is not working. it's not transparent, it's not fair. that is why we have been working on the roaming issue since we were elected to the european parliament three years ago. the problem was raised in our first discussion with commissioner viviane reding. for three years we - the commissioner, the council and parliament - have been working together to ensure that the market voluntarily corrects the errors that exist by means of price comparisons and open benchmarks. that has not been the case. the major distortions have remained unchanged. it is therefore no surprise that the legislative proposal is coming and i welcome it because it is our job to ensure that we have a functioning competition in the internal market. we are tasked with making strategic and systematic decisions that pave the way for an open market function and i hope that in these three years the industry will see to it that its business practices are changed and that it is reported and explained to its customers in such a way that this three-year legislation becomes a one-off and that we can leave this kind of intervention on the market. i would like to thank you for the cooperation we have had between the political groups and with the commission and the work that has led to this, in my opinion, generally sensible regulation.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, but also the commission, for a very well balanced report and also for the commission's position. the fight for decent work starts in the eu. it is about fighting undeclared work, but also about decent working conditions in the eu, about a living wage, about having the opportunity to develop in one's work and to have an influence in the workplace. if we are to pursue these issues globally, we must first of all live up to the principle of decent work at home, and we are not doing that fully. we must also constantly work with the situation in all eu member states. in international work we have ilo conventions that are good to work with. it's important to have open borders and trade and that's something i'm a big supporter of. but it is also important that we work at the same time to ensure good environmental and working conditions for people in countries that are poorer than the eu countries. there too, the right to organise and to decent wages and working conditions is at stake. what are the responsibilities of the various actors? it is clear that the eu has a responsibility as an international actor in the context of trade and other matters. the member states have a responsibility, but companies also have a responsibility, including a social responsibility. sometimes, contradictions are built up between the opportunity for growth and decent work. but i think they are interconnected: without decent work, we will not have long-term sustainable growth.
in writing. the report contains many concrete proposals to improve the situation of consumers. junilista welcomes these proposals which benefit citizens in terms of consumer information and easy access to price information. roaming charges certainly seem unreasonably high today. however, before deciding on policy measures, it must be clarified which market failure explains the abuses. is it a case of lack of competition through so-called implicit collusion? in that case, it is absolutely wrong to introduce price regulation, because that does not solve the problem at all. suggesting a treatment without prior diagnosis is unreasonable. researchers in this field should be able to understand the market failure we are facing before populist measures are taken. in the long run, it is free competition and not price regulation that benefits consumers the most. letting politicians set prices instead of the market is almost never a good long-term solution. i also believe that the proposal will affect the part of the population which, for economic or other reasons, rarely travels abroad.

in writing. today i voted for the roaming directive. anyone who has been abroad knows that it is very expensive to make and receive calls, but there are risks in the eu's price caps. i could have accepted regulation at wholesale level, i.e. the relationship between, say, telia and a company in spain, but i do not accept that a ceiling is put on the price for the consumer. it's not a roof, it's a floor. if you set the ceiling at 49 cents, that's obviously what the companies will charge. the profits of the companies will be reduced as they have withdrawn money that could have been used to compete on the national arena. if profits disappear, this may lead to companies having to raise their domestic prices. the eu has instead weakened the vulnerable consumer it claims to be protecting. the mobile market is a relatively new market and in sweden the market has gradually adjusted and prices have fallen significantly. if only the transparency of the system had been increased, if better technical solutions had been sought and the information systems mentioned in the proposal had been introduced, this would probably have been sufficient to put pressure on prices. roaming charges have fallen substantially, albeit unevenly across europe, but this is nevertheless a sign that the market is self-regulating.
in writing. we swedish social democrats would have liked parliament to have supported the commission's original proposal to raise the minimum tax rates in line with inflation since 1993. the vote was expected to be very evenly split, so we decided to support the amendments on raising the minimum rates to take account of inflation since enlargement in 2004. we welcome the fact that the report has now been referred back to committee and hope that this time the committee will come to the conclusion that alcohol taxes are needed to reduce the harmful effects of alcohol in the eu.
in writing. we have voted against this report because it does not deal with the conflicts of objectives which are at the heart of the problem. the fact is that the right of the member states to decide on such a central issue as alcohol policy is in conflict both with the requirement of a free internal market and with the right of the member states to decide independently on their tax systems. alcohol is not just any commodity, the internal market is the heart of the eu and the right to tax is one of the most important assets of a sovereign state. the rapporteur has made no attempt to analyse and resolve these conflicts of interest. the issue should therefore be seriously considered by experts and political representatives with insight into the subject before new proposals are put to parliament.
in writing. we have voted against this report, partly because we believe that the member states should be able to decide for themselves which care services can be subject to prior notification. we believe that a system of planned care whereby a person can receive a prompt advance notice after a medical examination would be desirable for the people receiving care, a system which provides equal access to crossborder care for all and not just for those who can afford to pay for it themselves and then wait for reimbursement. we have voted in favour of amendments which favour political decisions over the case law of the court of justice, although we doubt that all political initiatives should be subject to a codecision procedure by parliament. we also voted in favour of a letter which contains references to freedom of establishment, but stresses that we do not believe that this should necessarily mean access to public funds.
in writing. we swedish social democrats do not think that the elmar brok report is the right forum for discussing the treaty and what it should and should not contain. we believe that foreign policy cooperation should be strengthened, but we do not think it appropriate at this stage to commit ourselves to having a common eu foreign minister. that is why we have decided to abstain on these points.
in writing. i voted against elmar brok's report a6-0130/2007, partly because the rapporteur has chosen to bring up the question of the constitution, but also because he raises the question of the eu having a common foreign minister. we do not have ministerial posts in the eu today and we should not have them. all the member states already have their own foreign ministers. what's the next step? a common environment minister and eventually a common prime minister?
in writing. we swedish social democrats in the european parliament gave our support to the report. the report is good and we give our full support to the eu's commitment to decent work. it is important that we can guarantee people productive work, carried out in free, fair and secure conditions, both within the eu and internationally. we would, however, like to make a number of clarifications. it is important to point out that a tax on financial and foreign exchange transactions must be international, because a european tax would disadvantage countries outside the monetary union. we oppose common standard definitions of forced labour and exploitation of the vulnerable. the ilo definition shall be the basis and further definitions shall be submitted to the member states. the report calls on member states to consider minimum wages. we have already done this in sweden and have actively chosen a solution where this is left to the social partners.
in the summer of 1787, a number of individuals sat in philadelphia, pennsylvania, and worked out a constitution for what would become the united states of america. they were at the forefront of their time's knowledge of politics, philosophy, and democracy. they presented a proposal openly and honestly, and they got popular support and support from the states. how different is the process now under way to impose a constitution on the eu countries? the eu's political elite knows that they lack popular support for their plans for a united states of europe. the negotiations are being conducted in secret. it is not a question of democratically anchoring the european project, but of a game of blinds. for example, angela merkel asks whether it might be a good idea to change the terminology of the constitutional proposal without changing the legal substance. the flag and anthem proposals shall be deleted. the whole thing is designed to enable the political elite to push for a united states of europe without having to win the confidence of the people in the project. this is fraudulent.
i want to thank everyone for finally reaching an acceptable compromise. india, pakistan and kashmir are not traditional issues for me as a swedish social democrat and eu parliamentarian, but my commitment becomes very strong when i read the first draft and hear representatives of various parties from the uk in particular debate. our job is not to live in the past or take a stand for or against india and pakistan. we have a responsibility for what is happening in kashmir and for human rights there. our task as members of the european parliament is to see if we can play a role and then offer our support and experience and thus help to resolve the kashmir conflict. what's going on, and has been going on for so long, is a scandal! i believe that two civilized countries like india and pakistan should be able to take greater joint responsibility and draw up a timetable for concrete results and, of course, be helped to fight terrorism. if this does not happen, the un and the eu must intervene more seriously than before. the eu's strategy is to put pressure through agreements, support and contacts. i wish we had gone further and been prepared to support a referendum, but i realise we are not there yet. what kashmir wants must guide our future work and that of all who wish to take responsibility for the region. i would like to urge everyone to think about peace, not prestige and history, but the present and the future. and as jo leinen said: if france and germany could bring peace to europe and start the eu, then i am convinced that india and pakistan can bring freedom and peace to kashmir.
in writing. it is good that the legal framework is being simplified, but at the same time export subsidies and other intervention aid are still in place. i can never accept that, and that's why i'm not going.
in writing. once again, the european parliament's committee on agriculture has drawn up a report opposing the reform of the common agricultural policy. we want to abolish the common organization of the market in cereals. however, we support the commission's proposal to the european parliament's committee on agriculture, because the committee now wants to oppose the abolition of intervention buying of maize. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competition' in the definition of the term 'competition'.
in writing. since we believe that foreign policy, and therefore foreign policy statements, should not be conducted at eu level, we have voted against all the amendments and against the report as a whole. there is no doubt that many of the proposals are desirable in themselves, but neither the eu nor the european parliament should have competence in these matters.
in writing. i support the resolution because we consider that russian interference in what has happened in estonia is very serious and unacceptable. i am opposed to paragraph 8. it is up to each sovereign state to decide whether or not to enter into a dialogue. paragraph 7 comments on the internet attacks that have been carried out. it is a very serious matter, but i do not think that the eu should draw up a plan for this, because the matter is best handled at member state level.
mr president, i have three points. firstly, it is important that we do not let the problems with the new treaty overshadow developments in the european union and our achievements. in a way, our achievements are the best arguments for a new treaty. they give us new responsibilities and underline the need for new power to make decisions. if the european union were a failure, no one would have turned to us to deal with climate change or to contribute to stability in the balkans. we would have had no new applications for membership. let us not forget that achievements are the main arguments for a new treaty. secondly, we must ensure that the new treaty results from political challenges and not from different political symbols. we must be able to welcome new members and to have clear, democratic control and accountability with regard to the common decisions we make. we must have the opportunity to make the necessary decisions in order to fight crime, to meet the challenges of environmental and energy policy and to secure stability in our neighbourhood and in other parts of the world where it is needed. thirdly, we need to understand and make sure that it is more important to have a common foreign policy than a common foreign minister. it is more important to be able to make crucial decisions than to have an elected president of the european council. it is more important to ensure that we are able to make decisions in all the areas where we face challenges today. that is more important than symbols are. we need a treaty, not necessarily exactly the one under discussion.
thank you very much, mr. speaker. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs for presenting a well-balanced proposal which means that the european parliament is firm in its views on the treaty but at the same time is realistic and understands that there will be changes. these changes must not be made at the cost of a mini-treaty dealing only with institutional matters. we must take account not only of the two countries that have said no, but also of all the countries that have said yes. a new treaty must reflect the issues that citizens consider important. the climate threat must have a place, social europe must have a place, and it is important - as others have said - that enlargement can be achieved. however, i do not think that the solution is to incorporate the copenhagen criteria into the treaty. what is needed instead is institutional reform. it is also important that we do not just talk about openness in europe. the process now under way must be open and in dialogue with the citizens, so that we can have a debate during the process. i would also like to say this to the far right, which often preaches interstate versus supranational: the most supranational proposals of all have been put forward from the far right. the aim is to force member states to hold a referendum. it is up to the member states themselves to decide how to deal with this constitutional treaty. the right wing has gone too far.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. thank you, mr president! as the rapporteur on cod fisheries in the committee on the environment, i would first of all like to express my regret that so few of our views are included in the report on which we are voting tomorrow. we have a shared responsibility to ensure that cod fishing in the baltic sea will continue in the future. the situation for cod is serious. despite reduced quotas, the cod stock is close to the critical limit. if we are to prevent the stock from collapsing completely, we need even stricter allocations of quotas. these quotas must be based on science, not political nightmares, because the focus must be on the environment and the long-term sustainability of fisheries, not short-term labour market policy objectives. i can only regret that the committee on fisheries seems to be reasoning exactly the opposite. the commission's proposal for reduced fishing quotas is a step in the right direction, but it will not help if we do not see that it is respected. because while there are many threats to cod, the biggest threat is poaching, illegal fishing. it is said that between 35 and 45 percent of the total amount of cod landed is poached. the most important thing is therefore not new rules but guarantees that the rules are being complied with at all. i therefore cannot understand why the committee on fisheries refuses to accept, among other things, my demands that we take a hard line against those countries which turn a blind eye to poaching - and we know who they are, that is the scariest thing of all. in the long term, however, i am convinced that we need a completely new system of fisheries policy, one which makes fishermen feel responsible for the survival of cod. one system that has proved effective is the system of individual transferable fishing quotas. provide fishermen with an incentive to take responsibility for the survival of the stock. i believe that this will lead to a better fisheries policy in the eu. as sad as it is that the committee on fisheries does not seem to want this system, it is equally gratifying that the commission seems to be positive about the possibility of introducing individual transferable fishing quotas. in conclusion, i would just like to say something that i welcome about the report by the committee on fisheries, namely that my proposal to increase the minimum size of cod caught has been accepted. it gives the fish better reproductive opportunities and a more stable stock. unfortunately, however, i cannot vote with the moderates for the report before us today.
for the ind/dem group. thank you, mr president. the situation of cod in the baltic sea is very serious. the icss has recommended that no cod be fished in the eastern stock for 2007. despite these recommendations, a significant amount will be landed. the western stock will be caught 30% above the recommended level. in addition, there is unreported and illegal fishing. the committee on fisheries is trying to water down the commission's proposal. a proposal which also fails to take account of the consequences for biodiversity. as always when fisheries issues are discussed, the socioeconomic consequences of fishing are mentioned. it is true that a cod ban could have negative consequences for professional fishermen. on the other hand, overfishing means that soon there will be no fish left to fish. in the age of globalisation, it is necessary to be flexible and prepared for structural changes in society. i shall vote against the parliament's proposal tomorrow, even though i do not think the commission's proposal is satisfactory either.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i myself live by the baltic sea and have therefore been able to follow closely the plight of the baltic sea. it is a fact that the quality of life of people is being reduced, but also, as commissioner joe borg himself pointed out, that economic development along the entire baltic sea coast is being threatened. citizens around the eu expect us to take strong action here to save the ecosystems of our seas. i would therefore have preferred the house to support a cod ban, as the committee on the environment proposed in its opinion. moreover, with a stop, it would have been easy to access the illegal fishing that many here have spoken about. now there is obviously no majority for this in the house, but i hope at least that a majority can support amendments nos 20 and 22. they imply, among other things, that the maximum allowable catches should follow the advice of the international council for the exploration of the sea. it should be a given. the fact that we are not acting on the basis of the scientific knowledge available is one of the reasons for the threats to cod and the entire baltic sea ecosystem that we see today.

in writing. in the first amendment, lundgren points out that the travelling circus between the three different workplaces results in exorbitant costs. that's as far as i go. the problem is the very accusation, which is made in the third amendment, that the eu is engaged in large-scale property speculation. the situation is the other way round; ep saves money on ownership. since there were parts of the proposal with which i agreed, i therefore abstained in the first case and voted against in the second. the second amendment proposes that the eu should refrain from carrying out central campaigns. here again, i agree that it is unlikely that the eu citizens will be more positive simply because of the commission's communication strategies. on the other hand, i find it difficult to object to the eu choosing to devote resources to informing about its activities in the context of european parliament elections. we all do our best to inform the local community when we are at home during the midterm elections and by working constructively. the best way to communicate politics well is to do good politics.
rapporteur. – mr president, first of all i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs who have contributed to this report. the internet and broadband emerged, in the way we know it today, in the middle of the 1990s; it was, then, only about 10 years ago that these phenomena started to change the world. no one at that time could have foreseen the impact of this change or how it would transform societies and change the pattern of the world economy. no one at that time could have predicted what would come of it and what opportunities it would give to individuals all over the world. i think it is important to say the same thing today. no one knows how big the changes will be. we only know one thing: the changes arising from broadband development will be much more far-reaching this time than those of the last 10 years because the speed of change is greater today than it was then. this provides important grounds for decision-making in the european union. it is important to have stable rules and institutions that allow for new entrepreneurs, new ideas and new technologies to come along, and that allow for the dynamic, not only in the economy, but also in society, to make full use of broadband. i think that the development of broadband is a unique opportunity above all for the european union, because it combines growth, and opportunities for growth, with the cohesion of different regions. it binds people and regions together at the same time as giving new opportunities for growth and new jobs. it distributes opportunities for growth in different areas, and we can see from globalisation what this brings us. it brings countries and regions closer to each other, but also provides a huge number of new opportunities. for the european union, this is a special thing, because we can be at the forefront of the development of e-health, e-learning, e-government, e-trade, e-medicine etc. if we ensure that we are the region in the world that is moving most in this area. there are some important points of departure that must be based on the fact that the value of broadband depends on the number of users. therefore, it is of utmost importance for us to get people all over the union connected as soon as possible. it is of importance to us all, wherever we live. the second thing is that the value of broadband is also dependent on the content, which is why it is important that it is open to as many as possible. there are five points that i would like to highlight that are underlined in the report. first of all, the more competition there is, the faster the markets today develop the deployment of broadband. in those countries and regions where there is a lot of competition, the deployment of broadband is faster than in other parts of the union. that means it is important not to distort competition but to open up more competition in this area. second, there are few areas where it is better to use european funding than in this area, provided certain preconditions are fulfilled: for example, where the market cannot provide for deployment or when it is a question of increasing the level of advanced technology in this area – which is not just about deployment as such but ensuring that we are in the technological lead. i am sure that the eu can be the leader but that also means that we should highlight some other points. first of all, two preconditions for eu funding: there should be a level playing field where we are funding, and we should use the funding in such a way that it will increase the technology, not only deployment. we do not need the old technologies, we need the new technologies because they are the best, especially for outlying regions, where costs decrease the more advanced technologies are. lastly, we need to have neutrality between different technologies, different operators, different content providers and different services. if we do that, we can make full use of what this technology can give the european union. we can be at the forefront technologically and in terms of making use of opportunities and we can ensure that we combine growth with cohesion, which will make european citizens stronger in the society and the world in which they live.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank mr schnellhardt in particular for the patient work we are doing. what patience has not been shown by all with the lobbyists of the liquor industry! it's gonna be really nice to get rid of them. the whole package is really about us politicians regulating sales benefits through origin labelling and similar measures. that said, i believe that origin labelling and similar measures can also play a major role in defending local production patterns against unfair competition, even for spirits, which are not in themselves useful products that we politicians should promote. what we have so far forgotten to mention in the debate, i think, is the work we have done to reduce synthetic flavourings, to reduce the sugar content of spirits and to reduce colours. this is also an important part of the compromise to make less synthetic spirits. which brings us to the vodka. i have also tabled amendment no 149 to remove the sweetening of all non-traditionally sweetened spirits. but let's get back to the vodka. the compromise is that grain and potatoes are the basis of vodka, and that the product should be labelled if it is made from something else. if the producers start cheating the labelling and try to sneak away and sell non-traditional vodka as ordinary vodka, we have to come back to this question, because that is not the point. if you make vodka from anything other than grain or potatoes, it should be shown on the bottle. the amendments seeking to protect vodka even more have added sugar beet. this is not a traditional method in my country. why should i support amendments that add sugar beet and equate it with cereals and potatoes? that is why i am voting against the amendments.
we shall now adopt a definition of vodka. my question is: why would you do that? as a member of the committee on the environment, i want to devote all my time and energy to working for a better environment and better public health not defining alcohol types. the public health is really at risk. europe is by far the continent with the highest alcohol consumption in the world. in my own country, sweden, consumption has increased by over 30 per cent since we joined the eu. a definition sanctioned by the eu will only give vodka an image of something fine. it's not. vodka is alcohol. alcohol is dangerous in large quantities. if there is to be a vodka definition, why can't industry come up with it? with all due respect to rapporteur schnellhardt, this definition should be returned to the alcohol capital. they can sit there and come up with as many definitions as they want. we politicians have other things to do.
we swedish social democrats support the compromise on the definition of vodka for the same reasons as the swedish bourgeois government does in the council of ministers. we do this because it is the best solution for swedish consumers and producers, because it differentiates vodka produced in the way we swedes expect it to be - namely from potatoes or grain. if the compromise fails, there will be no special treatment for vodka made from potatoes or cereals, and it will remain unclear to consumers what they are getting when they buy vodka. some may ask why the eu should decide how vodka is marketed. we have a single internal market in the eu, and therefore we also have common rules for what and how things can be sold on this market. democracy must set limits on the market, including how to market different products. however, i deeply regret that not even a majority in the committee on the environment could support my amendments to put warning labels on alcohol. i've been meaning to come back to that.
mr president, i would like to thank you. economic relations promote not only prosperity but also peace. russia is the closest major power to the eu. if russia again became a threat to peace and democracy, it would be devastating for the future of europe. that is why we shall work for increased trade with russia. that is why we must work for russia's accession to the wto. but we also need to send the right signals. russia has recently cut off supplies of coal and oil to estonia. the aim was to force estonia to submit to moscow on a domestic policy issue. the eu's reaction was lame. russian oil and gas to major eu countries was more important. "first comes the food, then comes the morality", became the message. the eu did not make it clear enough that estonia is an eu country, that the russian measures were therefore directed against the whole union and that this is not acceptable to the eu. that's not how it's going to be.
at the moment there are many important issues on the agenda concerning the eu's economic relations with russia, not least the partnership agreement and the negotiations for russian membership of the wto. in the debate on economic relations with russia, two extremes seem to be opposing each other. on the one hand, those who see russia's emergence as an economic superpower as a threat and want to stop it and make us independent of russia. on the one hand, those who want to trade with russia at any cost, and are ready to do anything to get in the way of the leaders in moscow. they're doing everything to secure their own access to russian natural resources. i don't subscribe to either of those perspectives. we have much to gain from increased trade with russia and increased economic integration. but russia has even more to gain. it's not just about the economy. it is about bringing us closer together, strengthening democracy and the rule of law in this country. we are thus anchoring russia in the european community of interest. but we must also make demands on russia. we must demand that they respect democracy, the market economy and the rule of law. too many people today seem to believe that russia is fated to reject universal principles, and blindly accept that russia is violating them, but that is wrong. to give in to russian pressure or provocation will lead nowhere. it actually weakens the good forces in the country. if we act together, with a united front in the eu, we will get them to listen. then they must listen. russia is an important trading partner for us, but many people forget that we are an even more important trading partner for them. the same applies to russia's membership of the wto. russia in the wto is important. they are then integrated into the rules-based world trade. but we have to make sure that they intend to live up to the rules, even when they're in. it must not be like the council of europe, for example, where they first become members but then do not care about human rights. we can't afford that. the wto cannot afford russia to do the same there. last but not least, the european energy charter. i would like to elaborate on this at another time. the eu must show a united front and force russia to listen.
the rapporteur. president. - the debate is closed. you guys! tomorrow, when we vote on this report, we have the opportunity to make a major contribution to putting an end to the terrible cruelty to which our pets, dogs and cats, are subjected. as rapporteur, i am both pleased and satisfied with the report that we parliament, the council and the commission have now agreed. i would like to thank the commission and the council for their constructive cooperation. i would like to thank the chairman of the committee on the internal market, mr mccarthy, and my colleagues. i would like to thank mr stevenson, who i know has worked for many years to bring this matter to fruition. everyone has shown great commitment and a desire to achieve an outcome that is good for our pets. it has been a very interesting work, especially in view of the interest shown by the public in this matter. i am absolutely convinced that the great public opinion and the animal rights organisations have been very important in bringing us to this agreement. i would like to thank all the citizens who have been involved in the name-calling and campaigns. they have shown that we no longer accept this cruelty to animals. thank you all. your protests have paid off. many of us have seen documentaries, films, and photographs that show how even still-living dogs and cats have had their fur shorn. if you have seen a single picture, a single documentary of this animal cruelty, you realize that everything must be done to stop it. the organization "the human society" estimates that more than two million cats and dogs are kept in abominable conditions and then killed for their fur. these furs and skins are then sold under false names, such as china wolf and asian wolf. these furs and skins are used, for example, in baby pets, ornaments, clothing, and bags. we must now put an end to this trade, particularly because of the extreme cruelty of fur production. that reason is sufficient, but i can add that there is also no need for cat or dog fur in any product, as it can be replaced by other synthetic materials. the false trade name used is a major fraud against consumers. no unsuspecting consumer can in good faith buy a toy for his children without having the slightest idea that a cat may have been skinned alive for use in that particular toy. it has been argued in the debate that we need these skins for example to relieve joint pain, i.e. for medical reasons. my answer is that no patient in 2007 is prescribed cat skin for rheumatic pain when he or she visits a doctor. we've come a long way from that. i, as rapporteur, and all concerned citizens, therefore had high hopes when the commission's proposal was received. we felt that we needed a total ban, but the commission's original proposal allowed for certain exceptions, for example where the labelling states that the fur comes from cats or dogs that have not been bred or killed for production, or are personal or household items. i have always felt that if we leave room for such exceptions, the regulation will be a blow to the wind, which is completely impossible to control, and the exceptions would have opened the door to continued trade and to this terrible treatment. it was not the kind of regulation that citizens wanted or demanded from the eu. i am therefore pleased to say that we now have a proposal for a total ban after consultation with the commission and the council. i hope that parliament will support the proposal tomorrow by voting in favour of the amendments which have been fully agreed by the committee on the internal market. we also have proposals on how the member states should apply these rules and that the member states should also report to the commission on how they are being followed up. i am convinced that the public will be watching us when we vote tomorrow because there is a real demand for action on this issue.
madam president, i would like to thank you. thank you, kyprianou and eva-britt, for a wonderful job. most cats and dogs raised in europe can expect to be patted, fed, rocked and well cared for. on the other hand, those who buy stuffed animals or fashion bags made of cat and dog fur are contributing to a trade that torments, beats, and skins animals alive. this bill is unusual. firstly, the eu has finally listened to the voice of the people who want to ban this vile trade. secondly, it was felt that the single market would work best if we prohibited trade. for once, animal welfare has been put before the single market. i would like to stress the importance of not allowing exceptions to undermine the prohibition. finally, we have a precedent that shows the way for trade based on values other than market fundamentalism. when we are done with this, i hope and demand that the next issue be a ban on the import of seal fur. hundreds of thousands of seals are still being tortured to death on the icecaps of canada and norway.
thank you to the rapporteur for an important report, because education is an important factor in women's right to paid work. in this report, as in other reports on parents and children, the concepts of family life and family are central. no definition is given of the concept of 'family', but i assume that this concept refers to various alternative family structures, not only the traditional image of a nuclear family with a mother, father and common biological children, but that the concept of family also includes, for example, single people with children, parents of the same sex or how different people choose to live their lives. to clarify this, i hope that parliament will vote in favour of amendment no 8. the report proposes a number of measures to ensure that young women have equal opportunities for education with men. i support most of the proposals, with the exception of the text which emphasizes the importance of older family members, grandparents and grandmothers, and their efforts to help parents who are studying or working. today's generation of grandparents are often working professionals themselves. even though they are retired, they have the right to live their own lives, with the obvious joy and companionship that one can have with one's grandchildren. but they are not the ones to take on the responsibility of child care. society has a responsibility to develop childcare and other care so that all parents who seek care, whether they are students or working, have access to high-quality and educationally sound care for their children. both women and men have the right to study, work and have children. they're entitled to both.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the report highlights a serious problem that exists in many member states, namely that it is difficult to study if you have children. a number of measures are proposed. the point is, however, that these issues are not within the eu's competence. the committee is getting involved in strictly national matters, such as taxation, social security systems and parental insurance, which is totally unacceptable. the rapporteur also rightly points out that in some nordic countries, including sweden, there are systems which favour students who are parents. i am firmly of the opinion that these systems are the most satisfactory, but the eu should not impose any system on anyone in these matters. the countries that are in trouble will soon realize that the situation is unsustainable and will therefore look to the countries and systems that work. i am never ceasing to be amazed at the proposals presented in this house. the imagination is free, to say the least.
today, fewer children are being born in europe. this is due to the current work situation and the development of society, but also because many women wait to have children until they have completed their education. the older a woman gets, the worse her fertility gets. the possibility for students to form a family is very important from a gender equality perspective. the lack of adequate support from society results in many young women dropping out of their studies when they have children, or not starting any studies at all. this, of course, leads to a disparate labour market and also a loss for the economy as a whole, because the full potential of these women is lost. this pattern is particularly noticeable in sweden. over 60 percent of those entering higher education in sweden are women, but the proportion thins out the higher up the hierarchy you go. a particularly high proportion of women drop out of doctoral studies when they reach the age when they want to have children, because it is difficult for doctoral students to be parents. the level of education of women is crucial for europe's growth, innovation capacity and personal development of women. the member states must therefore make it easier for students with children to combine their studies with family life. this should be done through social and economic reforms, such as state financial support and well-developed, low-cost childcare.
