on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, first of all i express my warm thanks to the rapporteur, mrs mccarthy, for an excellent report. lately, i have noticed a tendency in the european parliament that i, as a european, find worrying: a weakened respect for the ecj rulings. the ecj takes no national considerations into account its judgments: it takes the interests of the union and its citizens into account. furthermore, it is one of the bases of the treaty that the case-law of the ecj is applicable in every member state. our role in the european parliament is mainly legislative. in fulfilling our task it is crucial for us to use the european approach of the ecj as our loadstone, rather than seeing the ecj as an enemy – a view that is not so rare nowadays, unfortunately. the legislation on public procurement is still suffering from quite a few teething problems. many of the problems are caused by the lack of, or difficulties with, national implementation. it must always be our goal, and the goal of the member states, to make the legislation as understandable and adaptable to real needs as possible. smes should be able to participate in the cross-border market in reality, not only in theory. we must make the framework for the public-private partnerships work and create legal certainty for companies as well as for public authorities, and check that all act according to eu legislation without imposing unnecessary red tape. it is our task to make sure that local and national interests will not overrun our european goal, which is, and should be, the best result for all european citizens and entrepreneurs. i shall finish by expressing confidence in the commission and in our future good cooperation.
for the ind/dem group. for once, this is a report that a member of the eu-sceptic junilistan party can express considerable appreciation for. this is precisely what the european union is supposed to do, that is, to ensure that we get economically liberal solutions in the areas where we have chosen the market, and this is what we should do by law. the report is therefore quite right in its approach. it is vital that the eu and the commission ensure that regulatory systems are respected and that attempts to favour domestic suppliers at the expense of foreign suppliers are absolutely forbidden. this is in the interest of consumers and citizens alike. but what would it be like to be an eu sceptic if you had no objections? i have two: firstly, i think it is wrong to start now demanding that more staff be employed at the commission for monitoring. in the committee on budgetary control, where i am first vice-chairman, we are going over the law in the opposite direction. the important thing is that we demand from the member states and the leading politicians, the finance ministers and the prime ministers that they can guarantee that they are really following the rules to which you are subject when you belong to the european union. secondly, we must also remember that countries have the right to choose other solutions. if you do not want to deregulate, if you want a state post office or something else under state control, you are fully entitled to do so and there is no need to call for such a call for tenders.
the committee on international trade has tabled an opinion. it is high time to take this important decision to ban the export of mercury. not many people know this, but the eu is actually the world's largest exporter of the substance. over 1 000 tonnes are exported from the eu to other countries every year. that's almost a third of the total world trade in mercury. the dangerous mercury ends up almost exclusively in developing countries, where it does much harm. i am pleased to take a position on a well-conceived report by my colleague, mr dimitrios papadimoulis. this report is not just about banning mercury exports. it is also a question of ensuring the safe storage of this dangerous substance, which i think is very positive. i am particularly pleased that mr papadimoulis has introduced some considerable improvements to the commission's proposal. some of these improvements are: to extend this provision to include products and mercury compounds; to extend the ban to include an import ban; to bring the ban into force earlier than the commission wanted, i.e. in 2010 instead of 2011; to establish a tracking system for mercury trade to enhance control of mercury; to support developing countries in switching to modern mercury-free technologies. these changes are in line with what i proposed as rapporteur in the committee on international trade, and i am very grateful for them. the only thing that worries me is the liberals' demand for a split vote. a split vote would threaten to undermine important parts of this otherwise excellent report. so stick to the rapporteur's line on protecting the environment and public health and do not go along with the liberal line on the market. tomorrow, therefore, the eu will take its responsibility and stop dumping deadly mercury into developing countries. it's about time. with our changes and improvements, the eu will also take up its historic responsibility to support the countries of the south to switch from mercury use to cleaner technologies. it will protect the health of thousands of people and our shared environment. it's not bad!
for the verts/ale group. mercury is dangerous to health and the environment, and we now have 12 000 tonnes of mercury to be stored. that may sound like a lot, but that volume could easily fit half the hall. so it would be strange if we were to send the mercury around and store it in a variety of places in storage that is not secure. that is why i am against the liberal attempt to get rid of mercury, because it would only be a temporary solution. a permanent solution must be absolutely secure. the only safe solution is to stabilize the mercury once and for all. mercury sulphide is almost completely insoluble, which allows it to be stored safely. if it is not stabilized, we cannot have a variety of stocks in poorly controlled forms. i therefore oppose amendment no 40 because it would undermine safety at work. it is important that we agree on a single date, 1 january 2010, as is clear from amendment no 43 tabled by my group. it prohibits both the import and export of mercury and mercury-containing products, which creates a holistic view. anything else would be a cynical export of mercury. we know that the chloralkali industry must stop exporting. if it has the chance to continue exporting one year after the ban comes into force, it will empty its mercury stockpiles and get paid for it. the mercury will then be used by gold-miners in the amazon, destroying vast river basins by contaminating them with mercury. it is essential that the ban be introduced by 1 january 2010, otherwise we undermine the whole idea of the export ban.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr dimitrios papadimoulis, for his excellent work. last fall i participated in a study in which a sample of my hair was taken to determine its mercury content. surprisingly, i actually had a relatively high amount of mercury in me, which is very worrying. i'm pregnant, and i have high levels of mercury! my high mercury levels now risk harming the baby i'm carrying and could lead to the child having trouble concentrating as an adult. the high mercury levels are also worrying because i come from a country that has come a long way in eliminating mercury. we no longer use mercury thermometers, and we use virtually no amalgam when we clean our teeth. yet i have high levels of mercury in my system. there are alternatives to mercury. given the serious health effects of mercury, we should be able to phase it out, not just here in europe but globally. tonight, we are talking about ensuring that we do not contribute to the global spread of mercury by banning its export and finding ways to safely store used mercury. i have worked to ensure that the export ban should come into force as soon as possible and that the export ban should be extended to other mercury products, so that the ban cannot be circumvented. i have also worked to ensure that we do not lock ourselves into a single repository, because we do not know at present which technology is the best for repository storage. mercury should therefore be stored in safe but temporary storage for the time being. in the future, i hope, not least, that all women in the world will be able to eat the fish in the waters near them without risking harm to the development of their children.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mrs oomen-ruijten, for a good and well-balanced proposal. complementary pensions are important in many member states and will become increasingly important in the future. i think the balance in the proposal is a good one, a balance between geographical freedom of movement between member states on the one hand and taking account of different systems on the other. some argue that this directive is not necessary. yes, you do. many of our systems around europe are built on a labour market that used to look very different. today we have a mobile labour market within countries, between companies and across national borders, and therefore pension systems must also change. i have always opposed the right to move for various reasons, and i think it is good that it has been removed. we have different tax systems, life expectancy varies from country to country and, moreover, a transfer tax could sometimes drain the funds for those who remain in them. it is more important to do as we have proposed, namely to concentrate on the rights at rest so that those who leave a fund are not discriminated against but their rights are fully respected. i think the qualifying period and the minimum age should be as low as possible because it hinders mobility. here we have found a balance in parliament which takes account of the fact that in the long term we will abolish both qualifying periods and minimum ages. the conditions for reaching an agreement are good if parliament votes in favour of the oomen-ruijten and social affairs committee proposals. we have tried to have a dialogue with the council, and we are not far apart. i hope we will accept this position. i think that will also be an incentive for the council, which will make it possible to reach an agreement at second reading.

in writing. we have voted against this report. the junilist wholeheartedly supports the proper functioning of the internal market. we therefore support the principle that a worker should be able to transfer earned pension points between member states without difficulty. however, we do not believe that the eu should have any say in the design of the pension systems of the member states, provided that all eu citizens are treated equally within the system in question. we are therefore critical of several individual proposals in the report, such as the views on the eligibility conditions for insured persons in supplementary pension schemes, the views on minimum ages for accessing pension rights or the eu rules on the qualifying period for joining a pension scheme. the way in which member states choose to design their respective pension systems should be a purely national matter, provided that all eu citizens are treated equally within the system in question.

in writing. in order to conclude galileo, the european satellite navigation system, eu member states are asked to pay another € 2 400 000 000. for many years it has been argued that this project would bring technological innovation, satisfaction of consumers’ needs, employment, and independence from the united states. these assets could justify the spending of community funds, instead of leaving it solemnly to profit-orientated multinationals or the american war industry. as long as galileo remains a peaceful process, we do not principally reject it. unfortunately, galileo will be developed 10 to 20 years too late, and in the meantime gps, the american counterpart, has conquered the world market. this makes galileo into a costly prestige project. spending a lot of community money on this, might sound attractive to big business, but voters and consumers will increasingly regard this as a scandalous waste of money. european companies refuse to participate in public private partnerships, because they do not expect any profit due to the arrears with gps. they do hope to receive a large community investment in 2012 for free. consequently, we do not want to spend even more money on galileo. if there are no real perspectives, we should dare to stop.

the president of the council. it should be noted that there is no legal basis on which the council could formally approve or reject projects such as those you refer to in your question. in other words, such projects can be undertaken without the need to obtain the council's prior approval. as you are aware, decision no 1364/2006/ec of the european parliament and of the council laying down guidelines for trans-european energy networks and repealing decision 96/391/ec and decision no 1229/2003/ec refers in several places to a gas pipeline from russia to germany via the baltic sea offshore route. i also assume, as is well known, that according to annex i to this decision a northern european gas pipeline is a project of european interest and thus belongs to the category of projects which have the highest priority. with regard to the specific provisions on projects of this magnitude, the council refers to the operative part of the abovementioned decision adopted by the european parliament and the council following the co-decision procedure. in particular, i would remind you, mr president, of article 13 of the decision, which reads as follows: this decision is without prejudice to the results of the environmental impact assessment of projects and the plans or programmes which set the framework for the future approval of the projects in question. where the relevant community legislation provides for an environmental impact assessment, the results of that assessment shall be taken into account before a decision is taken on the implementation of the projects under the relevant community legislation.as regards the second part of the request, the treaty gives the commission the task of ensuring the correct application of community legislation and, in the event of infringements, of taking the measures it considers appropriate. this principle is of course also applicable to the community rules in force on environmental impact assessment.

for the alde group. commissioner, i have to ask you to excuse me. mr president, i would like to thank you. few could have foreseen how the internet would fundamentally change the world, both politically and economically. despite attempts by totalitarian regimes to restrict the flow of information, the internet is gaining millions of new users. the way we use the web is also changing rapidly from passive information gathering to interactive. we buy and sell goods and services. we chat and meet. we've even been given a second life online, a virtual reality that for some people is becoming more important than the real world. there are statistics showing a yearly increase of 21 percent in internet commerce. although intra-eu trade is not large, there are strong indications that it will increase. over half of european households own a computer, an increase of about four per cent in one year. forty-two percent of all households have internet access, and again, the increase is four percent in one year. in my own country, over 75 percent of the population has an internet connection at home. the most encouraging thing is that the greatest increase is in the new member states. as many of my colleagues have pointed out here, the number of fraudulent acts is increasing in line with these developments. in this context, roithová's report is an important step towards improving consumer confidence in the digital market. we know that the commission, in parallel with parliament, is reviewing the whole of eu consumer law. paragraph 19 of the report proposes a european trust mark, a so-called trust mark. it's a good idea, of course, but i can see some problems. secondly, the cost of keeping such a quality label up to date can be high. the commission itself has estimated the cost at one million euros per country per year, which is a considerable sum of money. secondly, there is the risk that the trust mark can be hijacked, misused and forged. another important point is the establishment of an eu charter on the rights and obligations of users in the information society. this is not a new issue and is certainly an important area for the commission, as the commissioner also pointed out in connection with the current review. i believe that the wording proposed in the amendments better reflects the complexity and speed of change in the digital environment. it may go without saying that the digital environment needs a well-functioning framework in terms of rules and legislation. all actors involved in the network should be aware of their rights and obligations. we need to educate and inform consumers. we need responsible salespeople and entrepreneurs, but also bold innovators who see new opportunities and new jobs. of course, we also need laws that work in a changing environment. the commission has to balance all these interests for an eu that can better become a more competitive eu and meet the requirements of the lisbon strategy. finally, i would like to remind you that we must never forget that it is freedom and the free flow of information that have been the basis for the enormous success of the internet.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. trade, the exchange of goods, services and capital, is the very basis of economic prosperity. therefore, any technology that reduces the transaction costs of trade is also fundamental to the increase in wealth. with the world connected by modern communications, secure and fast payment systems, and the elimination of much protectionism, more and more countries around the world are being drawn into a worldwide trade. in the long run, this is for the benefit of everyone on earth. the eu's main task is therefore to facilitate trade in all its forms. firstly, within the eu, and secondly, between the eu and the rest of the world. on the first point, the eu is very successful and has made a major contribution to the economic development of the eu countries. the big exception is agriculture, where there has been no liberalization and where there are therefore major welfare gains to be made. the second point is much worse. the eu's protectionism against the outside world is extensive and covers not only agriculture but also labour-intensive industrial products. the economic costs for the world's poorest countries and for eu consumers are very high. it is therefore in these areas in particular that we want to focus our political resources to shape an eu policy for increased trade and increased prosperity. eu trade is a phenomenon of small scale today, but we all believe that it is a form of trade with a future. zuzana roithová is therefore in a field which will prove very important in the future, but the question is what the eu's role can be in this area at this stage of e-commerce development. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. maybe so, but do we know? every new area, every problem identified, is presented in this house as evidence that the eu must intervene. the tired phrase 'this shows that the eu is needed' is repeated like a mantra. but in a socioeconomic analysis of such problems, one starts by trying to determine what market failures are, whether they can be corrected by policy measures, what those measures are, and at what policy level they should be addressed. the starting point here in the house is usually that these are failures which can and should be addressed at eu level. any problems observed are taken into account in order to advance the eu's positions at the expense of the member states or international bodies of more global scope. you could always tell by the absicht. e-commerce is still in its infancy and we do not know what is holding back its growth, in which areas it will flourish and what forms it will then take. i therefore advise the house to oppose all these proposals for eu-led and eu-sponsored scholarship programmes and training and information campaigns. i therefore advise the house to wait to ask for charters of rights, dispute settlement mechanisms and harmonisation of contractual rights at eu level. i therefore advise the house to reject a logo for a european trust mark in this area. the countries and regions that are creative and pioneering in different areas are those that offer entrepreneurs the freedom to seek solutions that no politicians or officials can normally foresee. in the case of e-commerce, we will find that there are very strong incentives for businesses to provide certainty to consumers. therefore, such security will be produced by the financial market through new insurance services and through the producers' own brand development.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the commission and commissioner markos kyprianou for the good cooperation. i would also like to thank the german and portuguese presidencies and the council of ministers for their willingness to find common agreements on these matters which are so important for so many operators and consumers. i would also like to thank the rapporteurs of the other reports in this package, avril doyle and mojca drčar murko, and the shadow rapporteurs of my two reports. we have had many valuable meetings and the atmosphere of cooperation has been very good. i therefore have high hopes that the continuing discussions on these matters will flow in the same constructive spirit. i shall begin by talking about the proposal for a regulation on food additives. many times the use of additives is absolutely necessary to increase the shelf life of food or to improve its consistency. but it is far from clear how all additives affect our health and the environment, and sometimes additives are used to mislead us consumers. i therefore want to strengthen eu legislation on food additives, in particular by tightening the requirement that consumers must not be misled and that the authorization must take account of the impact on the environment and the situation of allergies. the current legislation already requires that the additives used do not mislead consumers, but unfortunately this happens all too often. one example is the use of coloring agents to trick consumers into thinking that the cheap yogurt contains more fruit or berries than it actually does. therefore, the requirement that additives must not be used in a way that misleads consumers must be tightened up. this was also the view of the committee on the environment, and i hope that parliament will also support this position tomorrow. what we eat does not stay in our own bodies but is also spread out into nature. therefore, the environmental impact of additives must be taken into account in the decision whether or not to use them. environmental protection should also be one of the objectives of the regulation. to further clarify this, the legal basis for food law should be amended to make it clear that it aims to improve human health and the environment and not only to promote the free movement of goods in the eu internal market. the committee on the environment has supported a number of proposals to increase environmental consideration and i hope that parliament will be able to do the same tomorrow. now, to the allergy sufferers and their situation. today, all food containing allergens must be labeled. that's good, but it's not enough. allergic and intolerant people should be able to eat food sold in normal food shops and restaurants and not be subject to special dietary restrictions. therefore, the choice of additives that are inconvenient for allergy sufferers should not be restricted by authorisation, unless there are other compelling reasons for authorisation. the committee on the environment has partly supported my demands on this and i hope that tomorrow parliament will take the opportunity to make things even easier for allergy sufferers by supporting my amendment and others to take greater account of allergy sufferers. so a few words about a group of dyes called azo dyes. they were previously banned in sweden because they can cause problems for allergy sufferers. recently, the british newspaper the guardian also drew attention to a still unpublished british study that again sparks the rather frightening discussion about a possible link between azo dyes and hyperactivity in children. i therefore support the requirement that foodstuffs containing azo dyes be specifically labelled. in conclusion, we must ask ourselves: who will decide on the contents of our food in the future and how will this decision be taken? i would urge all members to vote against the amendments which seek to limit consumer transparency in the approval process and to vote instead for the amendments which seek to increase transparency and openness. the secrecy surrounding our food risks seriously damaging public confidence in both the eu and the food industry. when decisions are taken jointly by the council of ministers and the european parliament, the decision-making process is also more democratic and more transparent. the committee procedure risks the democratic possibility of holding responsible decision-makers to account and the transparency and possibilities of influencing decisions are severely limited. in addition, unfortunately, there have been cases where decisions taken in committee have exceeded their powers and therefore not followed the framework agreed by parliament and the council of ministers. an example of this is the use of aid funds, which was brought to the attention of the european parliament just this week. i therefore advise parliament to be involved in these matters, even if there are advantages to the new regulatory procedure with scrutiny.
for the verts/ale group. i would like to thank the rapporteurs for their good cooperation. this has led, among other things, to my amendments limiting the use of flavour enhancers, warning against azo dyes and requiring labelling and risk assessment of genetically modified additives and pesticides used as preservatives to be incorporated into the report. i hope that these amendments will be adopted in plenary. i support the rapporteur in proposing a double legal basis. the environmental impact of additives must be taken into account when authorising. this may be necessary to ensure the proper functioning of the purification process in the purification plants. the main thrust of the commission's proposal is to introduce a committee procedure for additives. the risk is that too many additives will be approved and only those substances which are proven to be dangerous will be banned. the experience of efsa shows that the precautionary principle is not well understood. in addition, one of the basic requirements of the regulation is that consumers should not be misled. it is a political question how to interpret it and therefore a question for parliament. we have the right to natural, good and safe food. often, additives are used in foods that are not fresh or natural. consumers have every reason to believe that natural and fresh food will not lose its competitive edge through lax legislation on additives, which favours food made for large-scale and centralised production with long transport times, which is hardly good for the environment either. it is therefore absurd that the commission should state that colour in otherwise colourless food is a particularly important reason for allowing the use of colours. the proposal to use longer shelf life as a specific reason for the use of sweeteners is misleading and should be deleted. in the interest of protecting children, i hope to get support for restricting the color of food to children. and nanoparticles should not be authorised under this regulation as it is not adapted to handle its properties. we should also take special care to avoid allowing additives to limit the food choices of those with allergies. of course, manufacturers who do not provide sufficient information on their additives should not be allowed to use them. there should be no profit in withholding information. all these things have been put forward as amendments by the green group. if these are approved, we will have legislation to protect consumers. the same logic has been applied by the green group to the other regulations. naturally spicy food must not be disadvantaged, as it is in the commission's proposal. i urge everyone to support natural seasoning and not be fooled by the synthetic additive industry.
the eu's role and responsibility in the field of health is and should remain limited. but perhaps the most important thing the eu can do is to enable its citizens to seek healthcare in other eu countries. for many patients, healthcare in another eu country can be a matter of life and death. it is therefore incomprehensible that so many member states are doing everything in their power to limit that very possibility. european healthcare consumers should have access to the full range of healthcare services in europe, but this requires knowledge and information. here, the health programme could play an invaluable role in disseminating information on health and healthcare to all patients across europe. i therefore think, and many like me do, that it is regrettable that this very aspect seems to have been largely ignored when the commission revised its proposal for a new health programme for budgetary reasons. i understand, however, that the opposition to this has been very strong. but what is so sensitive about increasing transparency between the different health care systems in the member states? why don't you focus on measuring outcomes in health care - how many people are actually getting help to get better - rather than available resources like beds and hospital days? the only explanation i can see is that you want to keep patients uninformed and powerless. it is equally incomprehensible that the compromise reached by the member states of europe with our rapporteur, mr antonio trakatellis, has required that the documents i have received to strengthen the position of patients be deleted. why, for example, do they not want to confirm that patients have rights also as consumers of health care? they deleted that very sentence. i find that embarrassing. in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, health decisions should be taken at the lowest possible level. for me, it is the patients, regardless of what politicians and bureaucrats in the member states say. that is why we should empower patients through european cooperation, giving them more knowledge and more power. in short, let the patients take control of their own illness.
madam president, i wish to begin by congratulating the rapporteur on a report which has a strong message for a vital internal market for electricity and gas. there are two main points that should be underlined. first, the importance of an efficient gas and electricity market is greater than ever if we are to fight climate change, if we are to secure the opportunities for renewable energies and if we are to take care of european competitiveness. this also applies to consumers. second, we need an energy policy that can face the challenge of the risk of dependency on either big domestic producers or on foreign stakeholders that try to combine economic power with political pressures by controlling production as well as distribution. those are two elements that we need to deal with if we are to have a strong common energy policy. those are the reasons why the european union needs an energy market with fair and undistorted competition, with open markets, without economic patriotism, but rather characterised by liberté, égalité et fraternité. we need unbundling in order to ensure there is competition and new energy sources, and we need a common electric grid and gas networks across the whole of europe in order to ensure that there is an efficient market and solidarity. in these ways we can combine solidarity with efficiency, competition with cooperation and the efforts of fighting climate change with economic growth. that is why it is important to support the rapporteur and his report.
for the verts/ale group. i used to play with mercury when i was a kid. i hope that this directive will ensure that future generations of children do not do this. now there is a compromise with the council and it is high time we accepted it. we know that mercury is harmful to people and nature. we know that mercury can almost always be replaced by less dangerous substances. the compromise in the council of ministers is balanced and comes 14 years after sweden introduced its national ban on mercury thermometers. there's no more time to lose. those who are now considering voting on a technically unjustified proposal from the barometer lobby should know that the whole ban risks being delayed and made more difficult if it is approved. it would be irresponsible. dentists, laboratories and hospitals have phased out mercury. there's no difficulty whatsoever measuring air pressure without mercury. if you want to be nostalgic, you can buy an old barometer. it remains legal in the internal market even with this ban which does not apply to antiques.
it's actually surprising that we have to have this debate. mercury is one of the most dangerous poisons that we have in our midst. of course, it should not be used unless absolutely necessary. i am surprised that anyone would consider that preserving the manufacture of old barometers and thermometers is more important than protecting the health of the people. as a pregnant woman, i know all too well the dangers of mercury. i know that no one who is to bear children should eat the fish that i and many others have eaten throughout our childhood. is that how we're gonna do it? shouldn't young women be allowed to eat normal food? should a pregnant woman worry that her fetus is being harmed by mercury, which comes from products that unnecessarily contain this substance? before this debate, we have received letters saying that we must protect the traditional manufacture of barometers. this is really embarrassing! how can anyone think that i value the possible pleasure of having a barometer more than the risk of using mercury for future generations? i hope that all my colleagues will show in the vote tomorrow how embarrassing these letters are and that we will take our responsibility for the environment and human health seriously by voting against all the amendments which aim to maintain the use of mercury.

i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for a good report and a well-done job. it seems that we in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs are systematically going through all the areas of society which affect the threats to citizens from terrorism. this was also raised by the president today. it is essential that we do not look at each measure individually but look at all areas together so that we have common security rules covering everything from visa rules and security checks on board aircraft to how to better protect ourselves against threats to airports, public transport and ports and the whole infrastructure which, because of its large number of passengers, could be hit by a major disaster in the event of a terrorist attack. the question i want to raise from the rapporteur's proposal is whether three or more member states should be required to suffer from disruption or destruction or whether two should suffice. the rapporteur's proposal increases the number of directives from two to three compared with the original proposal. i do not think that this tightening is reasonable because a threat or an accident or a disaster can affect many people, even though it affects a few countries. moreover, for the affected location, it may be more significant than more centrally located locations in the eu. this proposal also makes it more difficult for smaller eu members to be taken into account, despite the fact that they are at risk of being hit by at least as large and severe crises. i also believe that as we close the holes and make it more difficult to attack the aircraft, the terrorists will target other targets and central locations within the infrastructure where very great damage can be done. it's our responsibility.

in writing. the votes on the westlund reports are not just about which food additives may be used. they concern, in particular, who is to decide on individual matters concerning food additives. unlike the rapporteur, we moderates do not believe that the european parliament should evaluate and decide to approve individual food additives, for example on the basis of details such as the risks posed by individual food additives to allergy sufferers. it means politicizing important issues that should be decided on a scientific basis and at the level of government. we therefore reject the proposal for extended co-decision by parliament. consequently, we voted against the overly detailed rules in today's vote.
i would like to thank all those involved, the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs, for the good work that has been done in the committee to produce a report on which we can have a great deal of agreement. we shall be looking at this report in the light of the global challenges and demographic trends which mean that we must change europe. i have said it before and i will say it again: the commission has started from the wrong end. the important thing is to feel safe in the change. then labor law is not the most important thing. the most important thing is an active labour market policy. it is about training, lifelong learning and good safety systems so that the individual can move to new activities in the old company or to a new company in various ways. it must be seen as a positive and not as a threat. the commission therefore started at the wrong end by challenging the labour law. a good labour law is needed in europe to ensure the safety of workers. how's it looking today? we have growth and we have employment growth in europe. that's good. but we also see another development. the number of bad and unsafe jobs is increasing, jobs where there is no livelihood, jobs where there are poor working conditions and so on. we must deal with this and we will not do so by weakening labour law. instead, we do it by taking the bad jobs, making them safer and better so that they're more like the norm of full-time jobs. that's the way we have to go. the commission has also since focused on individual labour law. it's unfortunate, you know. collective labour law plays a major role in many european countries. the social partners play a major role in labour law. it would also be appropriate to highlight collective labour law and the important work of the social partners in the form of social dialogue.
the rapporteur. i think it was jacques delors who said that the internal market for goods and services can never survive without a strong social dimension. why did he say that? it is about not competing with each other in the internal market with bad working conditions, low wages, etc. why not? because the wage-earners of europe would never accept such a policy. the flexicurity debate also shows that we cannot compete with non-eu countries through poor wages and working conditions. then we cannot do it within the eu either. that is the perspective in which we should view the posting of workers directive. the posting directive was created to ensure fair and good working conditions for employees: pay, working hours and other working conditions. there's been talk of minimum rules, but if we're to follow the opinion of the attorney general in the laval case, it's not just minimum rules. it's actually a rule of thumb. that is the extent of the advocate general's opinion. it's important to the mercenaries. it is important for them to have good conditions, but it is also important for the companies. if we had not had this regulatory framework, we would have favoured the companies that pay bad wages and the companies that have bad working conditions. it would not have been neutral in terms of competition. this is important for both employees and companies. the commission has made an interpretation. i can say that there are some things we agree on, but there are also some things we disagree on. let me start with the parts we agree on. there is a need for better information exchange between the authorities of the various member states. companies which temporarily have a presence in another country need to be much better informed about the conditions in the country they are coming to. there are a lot of things we can do there in the future. the commission has looked at the case law and has sometimes over-interpreted it, but sometimes also correctly interpreted it. as far as third country nationals are concerned, we have no comment on the interpretation given by the commission, but on some other matters we do not share the same view. let me give you two examples. one is the requirement for a representative. the commission has thereby over-interpreted the existing legal conception. the case in point is that the representative would have to be resident in the country where the activity is carried out. we do not make that demand. it is important, however, that countries can demand a representative who is not just anyone but who has a real mandate to represent the company. it's important in my country where we have collective bargaining agreements. but it is also important in other countries from the perspective of the authorities, for working conditions and other things. it must be possible to require a representative, that person, to be authorized to represent the company. the next question concerns social documentation. these are cases which the commission refers to. it's about belgium and if you were to keep records for five years. it's an unreasonable time. i can agree with that. but there is a need for documentation showing which employees are employed and working and what wages and hours they have. it is certainly needed during the time the work is being done and also for a reasonable time afterward. we know that some of this is rigged, so we need a reasonable amount of time afterwards. the commission's mistake is to over interpret its communication. you're over-interpreting the law. it's important to have a representative. it's important to have social documentation. the commission says that we should not challenge any labour market model in europe. that is an important statement, but it is precisely what is being made when criticising the member states which require representatives and social documentation. if we are not allowed to do so, it will be impossible to have an orderly labour market and impossible to maintain our labour market model. then we're talking about a different model. the commission should take this into account. germany has also been the target of the same criticism as the nordic countries. i would just like to raise one last point about balance. the countries which have no controls at all should also be criticised. there are countries that do not have sufficient controls, which may result in workers working under conditions that do not meet the requirements of the posting of workers directive. these countries should be criticised.
that's a positive answer. i would like to ask the commissioner to take an initiative with the member states to establish a more regulated form and to ensure that writers and journalists who have the opportunity to come to europe can also do so. i think that is our duty in view of the extremely difficult situation in the world. let me remind the commissioner that last year 113 media workers were murdered, 807 were imprisoned, almost 1 500 people were physically attacked, 56 were kidnapped, 18 writers were killed, 144 were imprisoned. it's time to act. the commissioner's initiative of a scholarship at eu level could be considered.
i am convinced that the fears of china's economic growth are actually exaggerated. i think that those who have a sufficiently long historical perspective on trade policy will recognize much of the anxiety and arguments from the debate on the integration of japan and other asian tigers like korea and taiwan into the world economy. it is easy to be intimidated by their relative size, is it not? but as the commissioner pointed out, their economies complement europe and their size is probably rather a great opportunity for europe. i am convinced that the long and strong growth and boom that the world is experiencing right now is largely due to the fact that china, india and other major countries in the world are being integrated into the global economy. this does not mean, however, that there are no problems in trade relations. i think we have many important problems to deal with, such as copyright infringement, intellectual property issues and closed chinese markets for everything from financial services to car parts. but i am almost more concerned about the tendencies towards protectionism that china's rise is causing in europe. not that i think the commissioner stands for it, but there are many voices now calling for more trade barriers to stop imports from china. talks about stopping chinese textile exports and other things have been heard here tonight. i am convinced that european trade barriers against china are costing european consumers and european businesses more money than the import of chinese goods. that is why we should continue to fight european trade barriers rather than chinese exports. in fact, the growing prosperity in china and the trade with that country is not only giving thousands, millions, hundreds of millions of chinese people a more dignified and richer life. it also makes europe richer. we must not forget that trade is never a zero-sum game. we're both winners.
in writing. although we are against parliament sitting in strasbourg, we have voted against the proposal to remove thursdays from the strasbourg sessions. we want parliament to move to brussels, but a change to thursdays would make parliament's work less efficient. the savings to be made by such a change are marginal and the environmental gains are non-existent, since the same transport will be carried out and the same premises will be needed in the future. we want a real change with a single seat and also a working place for parliament in brussels, as we also proposed in the leinen report on the intergovernmental conference.
in writing. we voted in favour of the report, but we do not share the view that it is regrettable that symbols such as the flag and the anthem have been removed from the new draft treaty. we also do not believe that the proposal gives the impression of mistrust of the union and sends the wrong signals to the citizens. the good thing is that the main content of the original draft constitution is preserved. the facilitation of enlargement, the strengthening of collective agreements and the right to take military action in accordance with national practice, and the increase of transparency within the union are particularly important.
in writing. i have voted against this report because i think it is completely out of touch with reality. the people of france and the netherlands have rejected the proposal for a european union foundation in referendums! if a referendum had also taken place in, for example, sweden, denmark and the united kingdom, a majority of voters in those countries would certainly have rejected the proposal. now the summit has removed the symbols and thrown out the proposals for an eu anthem and a european flag. but as far as the future legislative powers of the eu over the member states are concerned, there is no difference between the rejected proposal and the one now proposed. it is also interesting to note how the federalist majority in the european parliament's draft report is now trying to circumvent the rejection of the anthem and flag. i voted in favour of extending the mandate of the intergovernmental conference to allow the seat of the european parliament to be moved from strasbourg to brussels. as regards the question of referendums on the new treaty, i believe that each country must decide for itself whether or not to hold a referendum. i think they should do so, but the european parliament as an institution should not interfere in this.
in writing. i do not think that the new draft treaty makes any major changes compared with the previous one and i cannot therefore support the report. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union's institutions. although i am in favour of a referendum in sweden on the new treaty, i think it is wrong to demand this at eu level. it is not for the european parliament to decide whether or not member states should hold a referendum.
in writing. in the final vote on the ferber report on the liberalization of postal services, we decided to support the compromise adopted. we are therefore supporting the fastest possible path to achieve full liberalisation of postal services in europe. we also regret that there is strong resistance to the earlier objective of liberalisation by 2009. today's decision is a step in the right direction, even if we had hoped to have succeeded in completely deregulating postal services within the ten-year period set aside to make the eu the most competitive economy in the world through the lisbon process.
in writing. i am generally sceptical about the consequences of the liberalization of postal services for the average consumer, especially in rural areas. i have voted in favour of the whole of the amendments tabled by the european parliament. but i have also supported important amendments which say, among other things, that the collection and delivery of mail should take place at the residence or premises of every natural or legal person every day, and that in a competitive and liberalised market there should be an obligation to provide free services for the blind and visually impaired. i have also voted in favour of some proposals which give member states greater freedom to choose how to balance the liberalisation of the postal market with universal service.
i support the report, but i would like to raise three points of principle which were not put to the vote in plenary. the first is the view of small businesses. it is quite obvious that we can go towards both greater flexibility and greater security for workers and still have simpler rules for small businesses. i am deeply sorry that no such consideration has been given in this report. the second point i would like to raise is the scandinavian approach, or as the report calls it, the scandinavian situation. but there are very big differences between the scandinavian countries. the previous swedish social democratic government did not work sufficiently with both flexibility and security, which has led to a radically different situation in sweden compared to denmark. this is particularly true of youth unemployment, which is more than three times higher in sweden than in denmark. the last point i would like to make is the view on the position of women in life and in the world of work, where the report expresses a very old-fashioned view on gender equality.
in writing. the draft report contains a number of interesting points. first, recital n states that: "hundreds of thousands of women have no choice but to accept illegal working conditions because they are doing household work outside their own home or have caring responsibilities for elderly family members". at the same time, the scandinavian countries are cited in two places as examples of strong employment protection and good employment standards being fully compatible with high employment growth (paragraph 13) and of the right to take collective action applied by some nordic countries being compatible with the ec treaty and to be respected by the commission (paragraph 18). i would just like to point out that the situation on the labour market for women is very different in the different member states. the debate and the results of the general elections in each of the member states must determine how child and elder care is to be organised. i personally believe that sweden has come a long way towards an equal society and a model for women's access to the labour market. but it is up to the voters in each member state to decide which model they want to support in their own countries.

i would like to begin by thanking both rapporteurs for their good work. the ecb shall perform its tasks properly. although the waves of debate are not always high, jean-claude trichet always manages to calm the emotions with a language worthy of a diplomat. the ecb has become much more open and transparent than before, but parliament's requirement for public records remains. it works well for other central banks and would give the ecb further strength and prestige. we also reiterate our wish for a more transparent procedure for the election of the ecb's executive board. safeguarding the ecb's independence and upholding the objective of price stability is of paramount importance. the group of the alliance of liberals and democrats for europe gives the ecb, jean-clude trichet and jean-claude juncker its full support on this very central issue. we know that a firm stability policy is a fundamental prerequisite for a growing europe. unfortunately, many of us are concerned that the high-pitched tone of the french presidential campaign will now continue. monday's meeting in brussels will not reduce these concerns. it is also in the committee, although jean-clude juncker is trying to calm us down, and it is heard in the debate here today, where there is an expansive discussion. there are phrases in the report which could be taken as a way of questioning the independence of the ecb and having more political involvement. we reject this. i refer to the wording of paragraph 10, which in turn refers precisely to article 111 of the treaty, as benoît hamon says. it would be extremely unfortunate if our report could be interpreted in any way as a challenge to the ecb.
it is now barely four years since sweden held a referendum on the euro and by a very large majority voted no to the introduction of the euro as a currency. the arguments against joining the eurozone were and are many. on the one hand, it is a very unstable political project, and on the other hand, it is extremely problematic for a country not to be able to control its own interest rate in order to be able to react to economic cycles. these fears have been confirmed, and we are now seeing the impact on countries that have switched their currency to the euro. has sweden lost the battle to keep the crown? recent studies show that trade has been affected, but to a negligible extent. the benefits of sweden not adopting the euro are much more valuable. we have a completely different possibility to control our development because the interest rate can be adjusted to the prevailing conditions in sweden without the riksbank having to take account of the economic situation in other countries. i therefore believe that, contrary to what commissioner joaquín almunia has said here in the house, it is necessary that the swedish veto be respected and that sweden too be given a formal derogation from the euro, just as denmark and the united kingdom have been given. anything else would be highly undemocratic.
acting president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as you know, in december 2005 the european council decided to grant the former yugoslav republic of macedonia the status of candidate country. in doing so, it took into account in particular the substantial progress made by ukraine in completing the legislative framework related to the ohrid framework agreement, as well as its performance in implementing the stabilisation and association agreement. the european council also stressed at that time that the new stages would have to be weighed against the fulfilment of certain conditions set out in its conclusions. a year later, in december 2006, the council welcomed the progress made by the former yugoslav republic of macedonia but regretted that the pace of reforms had slowed down in 2006. in these circumstances, the european council of december 2006 called for an acceleration of the pace of reforms in key areas and the implementation of the priorities identified in the european partnership in order to move forward with a view to the accession process. the next meeting of the stabilisation and association council with the former yugoslav republic of macedonia, to be held on 24 july in brussels, will provide an opportunity to review the application and implementation of the stabilisation and association agreement and to address important issues arising under the agreement, as well as any other bilateral and international issues of mutual interest. on the basis of the commission's annual reports, the next of which is due in the autumn, the council will make a more complete assessment of the progress made by the former yugoslav republic of macedonia towards meeting the conditions and requirements laid down by the european union. as regards developments in the situation in the country, the european union attaches great importance to the existence of stable and functioning democratic institutions and to effective political dialogue. these are the essential elements for the continuation of the process of integration into the european union. in this context, i would like to welcome the fact that the two parties, evmro dpmne and dui, reached an agreement on 29 may 2007 on the way forward on certain issues of mutual interest, and subsequently witnessed the return of dui to parliament. the council now expects political dialogue on national issues of fundamental importance to continue between all political parties represented in parliament and between all stakeholders at the level of the legitimate political institutions. maintaining a constructive political atmosphere will be important to allow the country to focus on the essential reforms needed to move forward on the path towards european union. the areas to be promoted include the strengthening of public administration, the effective rule of law, judicial reform and the fight against corruption, as well as the further implementation of the stabilisation and association agreement. the ohrid framework agreement is also worth mentioning in this context. the full and continued implementation of this framework agreement is an essential element of the political criteria and will remain crucial for the further integration process into the european union. in this respect it is important that full confidence in the application of the badinter principle be maintained. we also hope that further progress will be made in areas such as decentralisation and equal representation. the reforms in the ohrid context should be pursued on the basis of as broad an agreement as possible and in full compliance with the spirit and letter of the framework agreement.
member of the commission. mr president, honourable members, let me first thank the portuguese presidency and mr lobo antunes for the priority the portuguese presidency is giving to the eu perspective on the western balkans as we have seen again today. that is very important for europe’s future. mr meijer’s report addresses many of the issues where the former yugoslav republic of macedonia will face challenges on its path to the european union, particularly in the political sphere. i am confident that the adoption of a resolution by parliament will be an important contribution to the eu integration process of the country. i welcome the strong emphasis placed in the motion for a resolution on the importance of the implementation of the 2001 ohrid framework agreement. this agreement has not only been a promise of reconciliation and stability: it has also been a noticeable example of the difficult but rewarding art of making compromises for the common good. the former yugoslav republic of macedonia became a model in the region for promoting a multiethnic society in a unitary democratic state and for defining mechanisms allowing for a consensual approach to interethnic issues. this approach was indeed crucial for the eu in deciding to grant candidate status to the country in december 2005. continuing in the same way will remain essential all along the accession process. in that context, the commission welcomes the progress achieved in the political dialogue between the government and part of the opposition. dialogue is indeed essential to ensure the stability and functioning of the institutions, as well as to ensure that the process of implementing the reforms is as inclusive as possible. we now look forward to seeing this dialogue continue. we also look forward to seeing it deepen, especially in the parliament, and lead to practical, real results. it is essential that the political agreement reached between the vmro and the dui will now be duly implemented. other key challenges include the effective implementation of the police and judiciary reforms as well as the fight against corruption and organised crime. your report rightly recognises the positive role which the former yugoslav republic of macedonia has played and is playing in the region. nevertheless, we see the need to continue encouraging the country in its efforts towards regional cooperation and good neighbourly relations. the commission will assess the progress made by the country in our annual progress report which is due to be adopted on 7 november this year. we shall also present a proposal for a new partnership, drawing up a roadmap for reforms that are needed in the coming years in the country. all in all, the country still has plenty of work ahead and progress on its european road will be determined by its ability to meet the political criteria. to conclude, there are many challenges to be met. progress in the eu integration process is indeed in the hands of the leaders of the country. i trust that the government and parliament of the former yugoslav republic of macedonia will take into account the sound and solid suggestions and recommendations contained in your motion for a resolution.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. when we discuss a country and its relationship with the eu, it often happens that the discussions turn into a list of things that we think are good or things that we want to criticize about that country. often we forget a deeper background and a broader perspective. when we talk about the eu and enlargement, we talk too often about economic cooperation, money and the cost of enlargement. but we too seldom look at enlargement from the security point of view and at european cooperation from the peace point of view. macedonia and its approach to the eu are typical examples of this lesson, which we sometimes forget, but which the history of the balkans reminds us of from time to time. the geopolitical situation of macedonia is a valuable resource that can make a strong contribution to the security of the region. but the same geopolitical situation also brings different challenges and sometimes great strains to the country. it is therefore extremely important that we give macedonia a clear eu perspective, as this report does. an eu perspective not only for macedonia but for the whole region can mean the difference between a way back to the conflicts of the past or a way forward and a future of freedom and democracy. but an eu perspective also implies a number of obligations. the fight against corruption must continue. the cooperation between the majority and the various minorities must be strengthened and a number of reforms initiated. however, i believe that macedonia is already aware of these obligations and the work that remains, as it has already implemented a number of the reforms necessary to reintegrate the country into the european family as soon as possible. i say reunified because macedonia is and always has been part of europe. without a safe and secure macedonia, we will not have a safe and secure balkans, and without peace and security in the balkans, we will not have a peaceful and secure europe.
writer. imagine going to the doctor and having to pay your entire family's annual salary to afford treatment. imagine going to the doctor and being forced to choose between getting medicine for yourself and no food for the children or food for the children and no medicine for yourself. this choice faces millions of people today. two years ago, i helped start the drugs for neglected diseases initiative. many of us in this parliament took the initiative in producing this report and we have had a very good cooperation. we are very much in agreement on these issues. i would like to stress to the commission that parliament takes very seriously the need for rules to ensure access to medicines. today, 12 million people die each year from lack of medicine, especially for tropical diseases. patients are poor, there's no money to be made in research for them, and then we can't get new drugs. we must therefore address the question of the availability of existing medicines and the question of research and development of new medicines. because these countries will never be able to get out of their poverty if their population is staying home sick. the trips+ agreements must not be incorporated into the economic partnership agreements and undermine the ability of these countries to supply their populations with medicines. in my speaking time i do not even have time to repeat all the abbreviations on the various patent-related issues which the commission is currently pursuing in the economic partnership negotiations. we're asking you to stop. as far as research is concerned, we need an eu-wide fund or a global tax on the profits of pharmaceutical companies. otherwise, we will never be able to develop the medicines that will lift these people out of poverty. the right to survival is above all other rights.
a lot has happened since the orange revolution, both in ukraine and in the eu. what has happened in ukraine, the problems and challenges that ukraine has faced and is facing, and the work and reforms that remain for the country to implement are described in the report that we are discussing today. as in most of our debates on eu relations with other countries, we describe the situation in the country concerned. we make demands and issue summonses. but too seldom do we reflect on how europe has changed over time. in the less than three years that i have been a member of the european parliament, europe has become less open, less solidarity and less warmth, and more closed, more introverted and more cold. we think too often about what things cost and too seldom about what they mean for ourselves and for the europe of our children. this is particularly true when we start talking about a country's eu perspective or the possibility of future membership. i am therefore pleased that this report supports ukraine's desire and ambition to move closer to the eu and one day also to rejoin the rest of europe. i deliberately use the word 'reunification' rather than 'enlargement' because ukraine, like the rest of eastern europe, is and has always been part of europe. it's just that for decades these countries were hijacked by communism. it is time to correct this mistake and the best way to do so in the case of ukraine is to support this report, to support the further development of relations between ukraine and the eu and to give ukraine a clear eu perspective which, of course, must not exclude membership when all the conditions for membership are met.
in writing. policies to combat climate change are obviously important. however, as is usual, the european parliament's majority takes the lead on some of the measures proposed in this resolution, outside the legislative procedure. the proposals increase bureaucracy and reinforce the image of the european parliament as an institution with elephant's foot. this report goes too far in some of its proposals and i have therefore voted against this 'thinking report' because i believe that it could have been formulated more narrowly and more clearly. the future of the eu's maritime policy is now too grim.
in writing. i am voting against amendment no 34 because marine biodiversity is not a strictly national issue. the resources of the sea are shifting and overfishing in one area affects the whole sea and can destroy areas and ecosystems that are much larger than national ones. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted.
in writing. i am generally positive about the development of the railways. it's important for the environment. but i cannot support the report's proposal in paragraph 4 that the european parliament should oppose extra-long lorries (so-called gigaliners). in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, member states must be allowed to decide whether or not these lorries fit into the traffic picture. i hope that the commission and the council will leave it to the member states to decide this question.
in writing. the cramer report states correctly that, in the transport of goods, environmental issues have been neglected. that is the consequence of the expansion of freight transport on the highways and by air, and at the same time the continuing reduction of transport by rail. the report recalls the promotion of road and air transport by means of financial instruments, which are also used to reduce rail transport. we would point out that many railway links to factories and harbours have recently been closed. however, we differ with the rapporteur on the capacities of the free market to solve this problem. the rapporteur, together with the rightist groups in this parliament, is of the opinion that free competition in trans-border transport is the best solution. the advocates of this view refer to the situation on the road and in the air, and expect that competition automatically attracts a new wave of companies interested. in practice we have seen that, until now, there have been no such positive results. despite this lack of reality, the rest of the report deserves our full support.
in writing. the report gives a good picture of how emu goes hand in hand with the creation of a european state. the report called for closer coordination of the economic policies of the member states. the external representation of the euro area is considered to need to be strengthened and internal coordination on the world stage improved. at the same time, it is noted that, inter alia, the evolution of competitiveness is divergent within the euro area and that the appreciation of the euro against, inter alia, the us dollar has created different effects in different member states depending on their economic structures and the elasticity of manufacturing industry. the report also recalls that the ecb's monetary policy can never be fully in line with the situation in a given member state. these were the factors that were used by the 'no' side in the 2003 swedish referendum on emu and won over a large majority of swedish voters. i can only say that the 'no' side in the referendum was quite correct in its argument that emu is a major step towards a united states of europe.
