vice-president of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. better regulation is a highly political project. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the first is to strengthen citizens' confidence in the overall system of european integration, and the second is to provide a comprehensible and reliable framework for the european economy to generate growth and jobs. we've made a lot of progress. the european integration polls show a level of support that has not been seen for some time, our economic situation has improved significantly and is promising, and there is a broad consensus to continue on the path we have taken on the basis of our partnership for growth and jobs. better regulation is a key pillar of this policy for growth and jobs. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the mood can change just as quickly, and we would be fooling ourselves if we thought that the image of the european union had already changed fundamentally. the idea is still deeply rooted in people's minds that everything that comes from europe is not appropriate, is oversized, is centralistic. this is undoubtedly an exaggeration, but it is important that this is the perception of the european public. so we have to work hard to counteract this reflex. we want a europe of results. we want a europe that brings security, prosperity and environmental protection to all areas of life. this must be reflected in all our concrete decisions in all policy areas, whether in chemicals legislation, pharmaceuticals, information technology or agriculture. and don't get me wrong, better regulation is not an image campaign. we have completely changed our style of politics and the way we prepare and review laws. i would like to stress once again that better regulation cannot be seen in isolation but is part of an overall strategy, a fundamental reorientation of european policy. there have been attempts in the past, but this project we are talking about today has two very special features: scope and sustainability. we used to deal with one-off adjustments. but now we have extended the scope of the concept of 'better regulation' to the whole process of european legislation. it ranges from examining the need for legislation under strict subsidiarity control, to broad consultation of the groups concerned, to impact assessment of the economic, social and environmental consequences, to the question of the best possible application and implementation of community law. but better regulation is not just about new initiatives, we are reviewing the whole existing legal framework to see where and how it can be simplified and modernised. the second innovation is the sustainability with which we are pursuing this approach. it is not a one-off action but, as some of the rapporteurs have already said, a process which is profound and far-reaching and which is being pursued consistently. all existing initiatives which have not been adopted by the legislator are reviewed regularly and, where appropriate, withdrawn. a review is currently underway with a view to the 2008 work programme. our simplification programme will continue in 2007 and 2008 and we will present a number of new initiatives to this end. the result is the progressive coverage of all areas of european union legislation. we are working with all the available methods, as mentioned and supported in mr gargani's report. half of the initiatives in the simplification programme have already been successfully completed and we will continue this work intensively until 2009. this means direct and tangible benefits for businesses, public authorities and private individuals. our aim is to ensure that our legislation is clear, understandable, user-friendly and takes account of technological progress. to check the quality and to assess the impact, we have set up the impact assessment board, a body which is independent of the commission services concerned and is already making a very positive contribution. i am very grateful to mrs lévai for her full support of this institution in her report. but i want to make it very clear that we have committed ourselves to reviewing this system next year and that we will draw the appropriate conclusions from the results if necessary. i would say this particularly in the light of the important suggestions made by mr doorn. one of the major focuses this year is on reducing the administrative burden of eu rules on businesses. we have already presented ten fast-track actions, two of which are already being successfully implemented thanks to the rapid support of the european parliament. there will be more suggestions. we are now in the process of carrying out a comprehensive measurement of the pressures that exist today in ten major sectors of the european economy, covering practically 85% of the total european economy, so that we can determine the reduction potential. the objective is indeed, as i have said, to reduce 25% of these costs by 2012. i agree with mr doorn completely. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are not used to the detriment of the environment. this is a very important point, and i am very grateful to parliament for having drawn attention to it. i hope that member states will set themselves similarly ambitious targets later this year, because this project can only succeed if it is jointly undertaken at european and national level. the project will of course have an important role to play for the groups concerned. we need backup. a high-level group of independent representatives in the field of administrative classes is therefore being set up. these independent experts, representing small and large businesses, consumers and environmental organisations, advise the commission on potential reduction measures. the commission - and i personally - is extremely grateful to the european parliament for the broad support we have received from the reports we are discussing today. the commission is particularly pleased that mrs lévai's report calls on the council and parliament to carry out a systematic impact assessment of substantial amendments. we had already agreed this in november 2005 in the joint interinstitutional approaches. the commission would also welcome the council and parliament to follow mr doorn's proposal and to further adapt their working methods in order to be able to carry out certain tasks in the context of legislative simplification quickly. it's not helping us if the projects stay in the pipeline. the commission would like to stress, with regard to the various instruments summarized in the report by mr medina ortega under the term 'soft law', that, on the one hand, conventional legislation is not the appropriate solution in all cases. i would like to stress once again the principle that laws should be made only where they are absolutely necessary. we have also instruments whose application is clearly regulated by the community treaties and also instruments which are exclusively intended to prepare future legislation. i can assure you, on behalf of the whole commission, that we will fully respect the rights of parliament in all the instruments we use and will keep parliament fully informed in the spirit of mutual respect between the institutions. in conclusion, let me stress once again what president barroso said here last april as a basic principle. european legislation is indeed the uniqueness of the european union, because we are a community of law. european integration is based on the rule of law. only the law can guarantee the freedoms enjoyed by europeans today. and only the law can provide a reliable and at the same time fair framework for the economy. the citizens of europe are entitled not only to better legislation but to the best legislation that is humanly possible.
draftsman of the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. – madam president, first of all i should like to thank the rapporteur on better regulation in the european union, mrs lévai, and the committee on legal affairs, for accepting a number of the proposals made by my committee, which is very much appreciated. this is an area in which it is very easy to agree on our goal. however, that is not what the debate is about: it is about how to achieve results. the role of the commission in this must be underlined. the committee on economic and monetary affairs has proposed that the commission conduct an annual follow-up on the results of reducing the administrative burden by 25% by 2012. the role of the commission is also important in enabling choices to be made when discussing legislation or, for example, self-regulation or mutual recognition, and in ensuring that we have some such legislation and – something which the commission has also done – in withdrawing legislation that is not consistent with the lisbon process. we would like the commission to see the lamfalussy process as a good experience that has opened up financial markets in harmony with reality. however, we would also like to underline the commission’s own responsibilities, in its direct relations with citizens, to secure simplification and better regulation regarding research funding, grants, state aid, subsidies of various kinds and procurements. the commission does have a role to play on its own in this area. we would also hope that the commission will come back to the idea of giving citizens the right of action when member states do go in for gold-plating. it is important to have a counterbalance to the ever-increasing bureaucracy of all our local and national authorities and, one might also say, of the institutions of the european union. one way of doing this would be to give citizens the right of action.
madam president, i would like to thank you. to promote cooperation within the eu, the member states have signed several treaties which lay down the basic rules of what the eu is and what it can do. this is called primary law, and the eu institutions are prohibited from taking decisions without the support of the eu. i would like to draw the house's attention to the resolution by the rapporteur, mr manuel medina ortega, in which he calls on the commission to avoid using soft law. as we all know, soft law is a non-legally binding document drawn up by the commission to clarify the guidelines in various policy areas. the fact that the member states have a say in areas where total harmonisation of the law is only the impossible dream of the federalists is welcome, not regrettable as ortega puts it. the community is bound by community law only in those areas in which the member states have delegated the right to take decisions to the community.
writer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we have previously spoken about the importance of mobility for growth and employment in europe. we are talking about the mobility of employees who are continuing their training, seeking new occupations in their own country, changing occupations through continuing training, and about geographical mobility within countries but also about cross-border geographical mobility. if we look around europe, we see that it has an impact on growth. some of the countries with the highest mobility also have the highest growth and mobility in europe. it's very important. what about europe? the situation is such that it varies quite a lot from one country to another, but at the same time we see improvements. we've seen improvements in recent years. i myself come from a border region between two nordic countries where mobility was not so extensive at first but has increased most markedly in recent years. it is important for growth and employment that people on one side of the border can move across it to the other side of the border. they may not have gotten a job at first, but they get a job on the other side of the border. it's boosting growth across the region and it's good for the long term. we have several obstacles as well. i've seen that in this work. the obstacles are in the different social security systems, taxes and other things, and lack of information. as regards information on this, we have our cooperation on employment services, the eures cooperation. eures cooperation has developed in recent years. partly at the specific borders, but perhaps above all through their portal, which individual citizens can consult to obtain information on the location of jobs but also information on the situation regarding social security. what about taxes? what do i need to do if i am to move across a border? in the last budget we in the european parliament pushed through an increase of eur 2 million in eures cooperation. that is why we are putting the oral question and the resolution. we see a number of needs for further mobility. in our oral question we highlight the third country nationals. it's for the future. we know that europe needs additional labour and, not least, third country nationals. they already exist today and we should expand cooperation - not least with the neighbouring countries with which we have neighbourly cooperation - and expand the possibilities for them to also receive corresponding information on the employment situation, on social security systems and taxes. the second part is about seasonal workers. there has been seasonal work in the union for a long time and seasonal workers also need to be better informed about the situation, social benefits, what is the matter. it can be lifted out of the portal into eures. finally, i would also like to raise the question of whether the commission has any ideas on how to improve mobility in general. we had a great deal of discussion on the transitional rules and the mobility between the new member states and the old member states. a number of eu countries still apply the transitional rules. i hope that this period will be as short as possible and that we will soon be able to apply free movement in full, so that all transitional provisions will disappear. but it is also important to try to look around and see what has happened to the increased mobility of those countries which did not apply any transitional rules, because the mobility is not very extensive. it is not comprehensive and there are sufficient grounds for improving the information at all. if the transitional provisions are to be removed, we must also improve the information given to individual employees about the availability of jobs and what is required, what measures are required in terms of social security, taxes and the like. these are the questions we have put to the commission. i know that the commission shares the ambitions of the european parliament, but what does the commission intend to do?
mr president, i think there is widespread understanding of the importance of increased mobility and all the experiences are extremely good regarding workers, researchers – you can look everywhere. the success is very much in the mobility and european cooperation and we know it is important for the quality of employment, for the efficiency of labour markets, we know it is truly a european idea and we also know it is of utmost importance for the opportunities of the individual. but, having said that, we are still facing a number of obstacles. it is important to support eures and to ensure that it can do a good job. but at the same time we must ensure that the obstacles, for example the transition rules, are removed as soon as possible, because it is a paradox that one side tries to increase mobility and the other side accepts that mobility is hindered. the second thing that i think is important to say in this respect is that even when we remove these obstacles we still have too little mobility. that goes for our domestic labour market as well as across borders. i think that eures could be used not only in order to inform and improve the security for those who would like to move to another country and another labour market, as mr andersson pointed out here earlier today, but it could also be used in order to inform all the decision-making institutions of the remaining obstacles we have in the european labour markets because the gap between vision and theory on the one hand and the reality is quite big. and i think eures could have an information role in two ways: to those who are trying to move to other labour markets but also to all of us and the member states in order to make it much easier in reality to move. that is the challenge that we could discuss further here tonight.

in writing. the reports on dairy products by the committee on agriculture are mixed. junilista supports the commission in allowing the production and marketing of milk for human consumption which does not fall into any of the three categories currently in force. such liberalisation is good and makes it easier for sweden, which has had problems since 1995 adapting its swedish traditions to the rigid eu rules on dairy products. the other two milk quotas are based on the eu's common agricultural policy and are rejected by junilista. milk production should be a matter for the free market, subject to the usual food controls for public health reasons. the amendment tabled by the committee on agriculture to the report (a6-0283/2007) on the common organisation of the market in milk and milk products is utterly absurd. they propose that budgetary savings be redistributed to the milk sector and that a milk fund restructuring programme be set up, that grants be given to those in the milk sector who are exposed to increased market liberalisation, that promotion and advertising measures for milk be strengthened, and that support be given to mountain milk production and to the promotion of the school milk system. we are strongly opposed to these proposals. the junilista group considers it fortunate that the european parliament does not have a vote on eu agricultural policy, as this would lead to a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies for all the different groups in the agricultural sector.
in writing. there are good reasons for working together to achieve the smooth functioning of transport between member states, with a view to promoting the internal market. we do, however, question several individual points in the report in question. we do not share the view that the common transport policy is underfunded and that the eu should devote additional resources to this area. we question the need for common training standards for transport and logistics personnel, as we have full confidence in the ability of each member state to manage this area satisfactorily. finally, we believe that the member states should decide independently whether or not to allow vehicles with a load capacity of 60 tonnes. in view of the criticisms mentioned above, we have decided to vote against the report in question.

in writing. the junilist has voted against this report and several of the amendments. alcohol policy must be national in nature and therefore not be formulated at eu level. each member state has its own specific drinking culture which its citizens must take as a starting point in their efforts to reduce alcohol harm. international progress in this area, as in almost all others, is being made by countries moving along different paths. by learning from the experience of other countries, each country can then develop its own alcohol strategy in the best possible way. such institutional competition between countries is the explanation for the historical successes of european countries in almost all fields. parliament should not impede this competition by promoting uniformity at eu level.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i thank the rapporteur for the ambitious work he has done. the question we have to ask ourselves is: what is more important in a consumer programme consumer rights or business rights? for me, the choice is obvious. it is to strengthen consumer rights and to make companies responsible for their products. the green paper discusses how a better regulatory framework should increase trade between countries, but the first priority in a consumer regulatory framework must be to strengthen consumer rights, must it not? the main problem facing consumers today is the difficulty of adjudicating a dispute concerning consumer rights between different countries, since there is no body to settle crossborder disputes. but this problem is not addressed at all in the green paper. the eu should primarily develop bodies to resolve such disputes, not create more eu legislation. this approach is also supported by the european consumer organisation buc. the rules in force today are minimum harmonization, i.e. agreeing on a common minimum level that no country can be below. at the same time, it is possible for countries to have more stringent national legislation. to change this to full harmonisation at eu level is contrary to the principle of subsidiarity which so many people, including the commission, claim to defend. the gue/ngl group is opposed to the proposal in the report which seeks to combine minimum legislation with the principle of mutual recognition. mutual recognition is similar to the principle of country of origin. there is a risk that we will see a race to the bottom for consumer rights. companies may be tempted to set up in countries with weak consumer protection. the aim of the green paper is to strengthen the position of consumers on the eu market. but if we are serious about strengthening consumer protection, we must vote for the gue/ngl amendment, which means that the rules of the host country, i.e. the rules of the country where the product is bought, will apply. i regret to say that the report largely puts the interests of industry first and reinforces the liberalisation of the internal market. this is a threat to the standard and level that already exists in many member states. the report introduces the principle of mutual recognition in consumer law. it is against all attempts to improve consumer protection. it is for this reason that i and the gue/ngl group are voting against the report, out of concern for consumer protection.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the review of the consumer legislation and would like to thank my colleague, beatrice patrie, for her very good cooperation. i would also like to thank commissioner kuneva for her work on the green paper. the consumer rules are really involved in all political decisions. eu laws and regulations affect european citizens, and these citizens are consumers. we social democrats in europe are working hard to put consumer protection at the centre of our concerns. a well-functioning internal market is not possible without satisfied and secure consumers. the community legislation is based today on the principle of minimum harmonisation, which is quite natural, since the member states have come a long way in terms of consumer protection, as we have heard today. i think it is important that we simplify and improve the regulatory system, both for consumers and for traders, because it would facilitate cross-border trade and strengthen consumer confidence. however, it is important that the harmonisations that need to be made are carried out with a high level of consumer protection and with caution. we should also decide on a consumer protection guarantee, which means that no consumer should be subject to less protection than before harmonisation. i therefore urge you to vote in favour of amendments nos 4 and 10 by the socialist group. finally, i believe that the purpose of reviewing consumer legislation in the eu must be to strengthen consumer protection, not to strengthen trade between member states. although they go hand in hand, there is a big difference between having consumer protection as the guiding principle and focusing on commercial considerations. we have to put the human before the market.
in writing. we have chosen not to support the report because it seeks to undermine some of the good intentions of the commission to create a more sustainable fishery. eu fisheries must become more ecologically sustainable to protect both the eu's marine environments and the long-term survival of the fishing industry.
in writing. some coordination of consumer policies, such as minimum levels of consumer protection, can stimulate consumers' inclination to buy goods and services from another eu country and thus promote the development of a well-functioning internal market. however, we would like to emphasise that consumers also buy goods from countries and regions outside the eu, such as the usa and asia. the role of the eu institutions in this context should not be exaggerated. it is essential that the union measures taken are proportionate and that the principle of subsidiarity is fully respected in all circumstances. we therefore reject several of the proposals that have been put forward in the past in the european parliament, such as eu-initiated consumer information campaigns, consumer information at eu level (a matter that can be dealt with nationally or through voluntary cooperation between existing authorities in the member states) and eu-funded authorities and institutions. the current european parliament report and the commission's green paper on the review of the consumer acquis are, however, essentially well balanced and create a simpler and more predictable regulatory framework. we have therefore voted in favour of the final vote.
in writing. we welcome the review of the consumer legislation and welcome the fact that it is now intended to address any abuses, gaps or overlapping directives. we see no overall problems with harmonisation which simplifies and benefits europe's consumers and traders as long as it does not mean that we have to lower our own consumer protection levels to a greater extent, which is the result of a long swedish consumer protection tradition. we therefore wanted a consumer protection guarantee and regret that parliament did not share our position.
writer. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i think bangladesh has been a country of great hope. religious freedom was eroded, rural women had access to microcredit, and we could see a local economy that was starting to develop. but these good trends have been quickly reversed in a country where terror is rampant. in order, as the government says, to fight corruption, journalists, lawyers, political activists and student activists are imprisoned. i'll tell you what. putting everyone in jail is a bad way to fight corruption, to say the least. we promise to help bangladesh on its way to democracy and there has to be a will from the government. before i close, i would like to mention something that is in the resolution but that many people may not talk about, but that i personally think is important, as i have been very much involved with people in slums. there have been people in the old regime, in the ruling classes, who have been digging up land, but when the government tries to take back this land, millions of poor people, poor farmers and slum people, are being pushed out of their homes, away from the last little shred of hope for survival that they had with a little land. we must also think of these people when we fight for democracy in bangladesh, because they have the fewest votes of all. they have the least power of all.
mr president, churchill once said that the best way to make a task seem difficult is to postpone it, and i think that is one way of describing the energy debate we are having in europe today. in answering mr chichester, who says he thinks ‘green’ and votes ‘blue’ – well, what is left, maybe, is to act ‘green’ as well. the proposal from the commission and the decisions we have in place on 20% of the total energy consumption in europe by 2020 being derived from renewable sources is, indeed, very good. it is also nice for everyone – for all voters, industry and citizens in europe – to see that parliament, thanks to ms thomsen’s work on this report, is also very decided that this is a good way to work and that this is the way we should do it. is it possible? there are question marks, of course, when people ask questions. i think it is perfectly possible. if we look at what can be done in industry by trigeneration and by using renewables, it is perfectly possible. in households, on heating and cooling, it is also perfectly possible, and in the transport sector also. i am very happy that, in combination with what we have here on renewables, other regulations in place in europe are changed. it is about time we put more ethanol in petrol, and with the new decisions we will at least have half the rate that brazil has. i think it is about time that we discussed whether we put biogas and natural gas as well, which is the best way of boosting the volumes we need. when we do that, we also have the market in place and then the development goes on by itself, step by step. there are some responsibilities, of course. the renewable action plan – both at european level and national levels – has to be good and come into force quickly. the second area i want to point out is taxes. i cannot understand why we tax people out of behaviour that we want them to have when it comes to fuels of different kinds. the third thing is regulation, where we can still do a lot and where we have to do some cleaning also in the european system. finally, i think we are in the midst of an engineering revolution that has the same consequences as when we went from steam to electricity, and now we are going from electricity to smart energy, and this is a very good step on the way.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the european sugar industry relies on artificial respiration. the respirator, today's eu, provides millions of euros each year in direct aid and intervention aid to european sugar growers. the eu taxpayers are paying for an age-old system that has outlived itself. the committee's proposals, unfortunately, involve higher costs and slower reforms, which are the exact opposite of the market adjustment that this uncompetitive sector so badly needs. sugar-producing countries outside the eu must have unhindered access to the single market. international trade based on fairer conditions is a way for less developed countries to compete in the global market. the liberalisation of the sugar industry would also benefit european consumers. they do not have to finance an artificial system of support for producers through taxation, and they face lower prices in shops. this would benefit all.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. in the debate on the recall of the toys in question, it has often been argued that the responsibility for the presence of dangerous toys on the market lies with the manufacturer, in this case china. i regret that the discussion has not been more about the responsibility of companies which outsource production to, for example, china, in this case mattel. i do not want to blame this particular company, because there are many companies that outsource production. it's because they want to keep prices as low as possible. you want to increase profits. however, we need to discuss much more about the responsibilities of companies. the aim is to keep costs as low as possible, while companies do not take responsibility for the safety of consumers or the workers who work with these harmful products. we need to put pressure on companies that we as consumers demand safe and secure products. we also demand that products be manufactured in decent work environments without putting workers' health and safety at risk. of course, it is especially tragic when children are the victims of harmful products. we must of course work together to put an end to this.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as a parent of young children myself, i feel that this question is of the utmost importance. to be honest, i don't always think about the chemicals in a toy or its safety when i buy toys for my children. for me it is so obvious that the toys i buy in sweden, brussels or strasbourg are safe. you may think i'm gullible, but i'm not alone. in connection with a raid on my daughter's barbie box three weeks ago, and taking out both the dog and the cat and the bag, i asked some neighbors back home on my street how they viewed this when they were buying toys. all of them felt that as long as the items were sold in toy shops and were ce marked, they were safe. so our consumers are being lulled into a false sense of security. now it's our responsibility to make sure that security doesn't continue to be a sham. i therefore appeal to the commission to review the toy directive as soon as possible. what could possibly be a higher priority than our children? one of the things we have to do is to make sure that the toys do not contain carcinogens. we must ban all carcinogens and other toxic substances. many of these substances accumulate in the body. children exposed to such substances will carry them in their bodies for the rest of their lives. this responsibility cannot be placed on the parents. we don't have the knowledge. who would buy a toy if they knew it contained carcinogens? we must change and strengthen the ce marking. consumers today believe that ce means that the product is safe, but that requires a well-developed market surveillance and a uniform testing system. we must also ensure that sellers and suppliers of unsafe products are forced to take full responsibility. i also welcome a review of the rapex system. after talking to swedish consumer advisors, i understand that it is a very important tool, and that it is used on a daily basis. however, it is not complete until all eu countries make full use of and contribute to the system. finally, i would like to stress once again that our consumers must come first, because without safe and secure consumers we cannot have a thriving internal market.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we buy toys for our children to have fun and stimulate their development. without exception, parents expect society to protect their children, but current legislation allows us to put poison bombs in the hands of our children. verheugen says lead is banned from toys, but the truth is, the law allows 0.7 micrograms of lead intake per day. but the law allows industry to set the standards for how safety is achieved, and then the protection of our children is over. the standard says that the 1985 toxicity data is sufficient the commission's own experts require an update. the standard says that the entire daily intake can be counted from toys the experts say at most 10 percent, which would mean ten times the stringent requirements. the standard assumes that no child swallows more than 8 milligrams of a toy. experts think that's absurd. the standard only tests one toy per category, not every product the experts find this absurd. the commission's own experts consider that protection is a disaster, and this is just the beginning. 34 flavours prohibited in cosmetics are allowed in toys. carcinogens, mutagens, reproductive toxicants that are banned in cosmetics are allowed in toys. in addition, allergens are allowed in toys. for ten years the commission has been aware of the problems. now we want the legislation, a law that protects our children both on paper and in reality. why do the toy industry and governments get the documents but not parliament?
in writing. i voted against the report by gerardo galeote (a6-0318/2007) today. france shall not favour rum producers in its overseas departments by lower taxes than those permitted. the best way to benefit agriculture in less developed countries is to remove tariffs on agricultural products from all countries of the world and to abolish the common agricultural policy (cap). furthermore, excise duties on alcohol are intended to curb alcohol consumption. the harmful effects of alcohol are just as great if the alcohol is produced in guadeloupe or martinique as if it were produced elsewhere.
in writing. the junilist accepts the commission's proposal and the european parliament's amendment, provided that the eu institutions respect the principle that education policy is a national concern. however, the european parliament's committee on culture proposes in amendment no 7 that a reference to the 'charter of fundamental rights of the european union' should also be included. there is no such thing. the eu countries have not established such a statute. such a feature was included in the draft constitutional treaty rejected in the democratic process in the summer of 2005. however, all eu member states have signed the council of europe convention on human rights. this means that all eu citizens can assert their fundamental rights and freedoms in the european court of justice. the eu charter is the opposite of the 'narrower but sharper' eu often spoken of in the subsidarity rhetoric. the eu should deal with crossborder issues, but not with those which are decided by individual member states or which are already regulated by other international treaties. it follows that the draft eu charter of fundamental rights should be rejected, as, of course, should the draft 'new' eu treaty.
in writing. i welcome the possibility of crossborder rail services, but for a market to work, competition must be on equal terms. it is therefore unfortunate that the eu is pushing through legislation which gives countries which have not yet liberalised rail transport the right to compete with operators in a liberalised market for international travel. in practice, this proposal allows state-supported railway undertakings to compete in a market where free competition exists, which in the long term risks putting the market mechanisms at risk. i have therefore voted against this report. state-aided railway undertakings should not be allowed to compete in a market with free competition.
in writing. although the latest proposal does not impose the same stringent requirements on train operators as previous proposals, it still includes far-reaching provisions on everything from how to buy tickets (by telephone, internet and in ticket machines) to the liability of train operators in respect of luggage. it also details the different compensation for delays (reimbursement levels, meal rights, etc.) and the minimum information requirements on the causes of possible delays. i voted against the proposal because operators themselves must be able to develop these services on the basis of the protection that travellers demand (and are prepared to pay for in the form of higher fares). furthermore, the far-reaching regulations are not proportionate to the added value created by replacing existing rules at member state level in those countries which have opted for national legislation.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted against the parliament's report on the common organisation of the market in sugar (a6-0310/2007). in essence, we are in favour of a reform of the sugar sector in the community. we do not, however, consider that parliament's proposal adds anything positive to the commission's proposal. we believe that the proposal risks leading to cost increases in an already too large agricultural sector, something that we swedish social democrats can never support. we also believe that in the long term export subsidies for sugar must be abolished completely. the aid still paid to agriculture shall be paid in the form of direct payments totally decoupled from production.
in writing. the junilists support a comprehensive reform of the eu sugar market with a view to its early liberalisation and to allowing non-eu sugar-producing countries to compete on fair terms. the amendments proposed by the committee on agriculture in these reports can only be described as a further attempt to prevent the reform process as far as possible and thus to continue to pursue a protectionist trade policy. the proposals also imply that the costs of industrial conversion of existing refineries resulting from the reform would increase, while proposing more generous direct compensation to operators in the sugar industry. the junilist can never support a report which is contrary to the objectives of reforming the sugar market in the eu and which would inevitably water down the sugar market and jeopardise its completion. we oppose any attempt to circumvent the commitments made to the acp countries. for the reasons mentioned above, we therefore vote against this report.

mr president, we need to support innovations and research all over europe. that is a crucial task for business life, universities and research institutes, member states and the union. it will require much more funding for research and innovations. that is why i would like to underline what the commissioner stated earlier – that, regarding the eit, the key issue now is the financing and funding. this is because we need to ensure that, not only is the eit funded, but also that it is not funded and getting its finances via money that otherwise would have financed research and innovation in other programmes and projects. i think this cannot be underlined too much, as the funding for the eit must come from other items in the budget than those which today contribute to the efforts of innovations and research in the seventh framework programme, in the cip or other items under the 1a heading. if we can manage to do that, then we can manage to bring additional resources to innovations and research in europe and can contribute to the innovations. from the perspective of my group, i would like to underline that that is the crucial test that the commission has to live up to and we need to contribute to because, if so, we can all contribute to europe with more and better innovations. if we succeed with this, we will succeed with innovations.
draftsman of the opinion of the committee on industry, research and energy. – madam president, the cornerstones of the energy policy for the european union are transparency, reciprocity and the rule of law. and, as we have all experienced in the last two years, this is also a vitally important issue when it comes to energy and foreign relations. we already have problems in europe when it comes to security of supply, consumer rights and environmental concerns, and we have to do something about the greenhouse effect. we also have a disturbance on the market that is severe, which neelie kroes’s report showed us very clearly and which puts on the table the need to unbundle, which is also a foreign relations concern. this is a very important aspect of this issue. so we have to rebuild our energy system in europe with regard to production, distribution and consumption. i would like to thank the rapporteur mr saryusz-wolski for very good cooperation. he has taken account of all the texts from the committee on industry, research and energy. what i am not so happy about is what he added with regard to the new solana-like function, to be able to talk with one voice for the european union. this new institution would, as i see it, cause uncertainty of roles, a divide-and-conquer situation, and it takes completely the opposite approach to our cornerstones in our energy policy. we cannot check on reciprocity with an institution like this, transparency is ruled out and the powers of parliament are diminished, which is also completely the opposite of what we want in all other areas. we have therefore asked for a split vote on this topic tomorrow. finally, i want to agree with the commissioner that it is vitally important that we add the energy issue to the new treaty.
madam president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on a report which is not only very good but also timely. saryusz-wolski has done extremely important work in shifting the focus from energy as a matter of our convenience to energy as a matter of geopolitical importance. the geopolitical and security dimension is something that we who were born behind the iron curtain are always aware of, but that western europe neglected for a long time. this is perhaps the most important contribution the rapporteur has made to this debate. we need to develop a common policy to better protect our common interests in this extremely important strategic area. i am grateful to mrs saryusz-wolski for having the courage to propose this report, which contains a number of important and innovative ideas. we should bear in mind that a secure energy supply is of vital interest to hundreds of millions of europe's citizens. finally, saryusz-wolski is absolutely right when he says that we need to speak with one voice on this issue, precisely because the energy issue is also clearly a security issue. all our experience of foreign and security policy shows that every time we have not spoken with one voice we have lost and our citizens have paid the price. i can therefore only warmly recommend that we support the saryusz-wolski report in its entirety.
mr president, i would like to thank you. migration has been taking place throughout human history. it has been one of the most fundamental drivers of this story. when we take a stand on migration between countries, we are therefore taking a stand on fundamental questions, on existential questions of human freedom. don't people have the right to choose where in the world they want to live? none of this is really up for discussion here. the modern welfare state is incompatible with free immigration and difficult to reconcile with more extensive immigration at all because of the standard differences. immigration to rejuvenate our own population is almost stillborn according to the research. family immigration has very little effect on the demographic structure. it would take 50 to 75 percent of the people in a country to be immigrants if we were to rejuvenate the population of a country like japan. brain drain is a big problem for many developing countries. in fact, it is proposed that other countries be deprived of their educated population altogether. we require a new foundation.

for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. first of all, i would like to thank my colleague mr lehtinen for a very good job. the internal market for services, following the adoption of the services directive, has clearer rules on how to exercise the freedom to provide services. however, there are no clear rules on the obligations of service providers. this means that even after the services directive has been transposed in all member states in 2009, we can still face an open market in which consumers have no confidence. only 6% of consumers made crossborder online purchases in 2006. the rules for goods are still much more well established. companies, especially smes, also need clearer rules, as they often choose not to provide a service or sell across borders, leading to reduced competition and higher prices for consumers. clear rules give businesses and consumers the courage to cross borders, and to provide and buy services from other countries without fear. we must ensure that consumers can rightly feel safe in the internal market and that, even in cases of dispute, they can easily get help and redress. this requires a combination of many different measures. some examples mentioned in the report are codes of conduct to which service providers can commit themselves, the possibility of bringing crossborder class actions against fraudsters or companies which do not honour their commitments to consumers. it is not reasonable that a consumer is less protected when buying a crossborder service than when buying a product from another member state. the report therefore proposes a broad, horizontal instrument as another important element in the consumer protection we want to build for the provision of crossborder services. as a consumer, you need to know your rights when a service is provided too late or in the wrong way. if these freedoms are complemented by clear obligations for the supplier and strong consumer protection, we can strengthen consumer confidence. once again, our consumers must be put first. without safe and secure consumers, we cannot have a thriving internal market.

in writing. the majority in this parliament needs to be reminded regularly that education policy in the european union is a national issue. this report from the european parliament's committee on culture raises a lot of issues for the eu institutions to work with that do not concern them at all. for example, paragraph 12 of the draft report proposes that the european parliament should take the position that more research is needed at community level on pre-school education, particularly in the area of early and targeted measures, in order to find out which practices have the expected effect. the draft paragraph 48 reads as follows: the european parliament considers that a procedure for evaluating the eu education system should be developed in order to make eu action more targeted. the evaluation should be based on regular reports from member states as well as independent verification, with particular attention to the acquisition of basic skills by pupils and the achievement of justice objectives. all this is an attempt to invade the national spheres of competence of the member states. the subsidiarity principle is being flouted as usual and institutional competition in this important area is being undermined. we therefore vote against this report.

in writing. the junilist believes that a un mandate is what is required for a country or an intergovernmental organisation to send peacekeeping forces to conflict zones. we have no objection in principle to member states joining forces and sending troops on peacekeeping missions, provided that the operation is under the un flag and not under the eu flag. it is regrettable, however, that the current situation in chad and the central african republic is being used as a pretext for the eu's ambitions to create a military power at union level. foreign and security policy is and must remain a strictly national matter.

in writing. we voted in favour of the report by ms buitenweg (a6-0278/2007) on the application of the directive on the principle of equal treatment between persons irrespective of racial or ethnic origin. we believe that it is important to combat all forms of discrimination in society and therefore welcome the commission's review of the implementation of the directive in question. the review is a valuable tool for evaluating how to improve the fight against discrimination. we are committed to equal treatment and therefore oppose the rapporteur's demand for positive discrimination and strongly oppose the registration and collection of data which are intended to categorize people by racial or ethnic origin. as defenders of the fundamental principles of law in society, we also have doubts about the report's call for reversing the burden of proof.
in writing. sometimes a member of parliament is faced with the difficult choice between the plague and cholera. the vote on this report was a typical example of this. on the one hand, a report on the transposition of legislation which i find to be largely highly doubtful. on the other hand, amendments by the its, which in most cases did not address the weaknesses of the report but were intended to introduce new, rather than worse, arguments. voting for its is hardly doable. that's why i chose to abstain. there is no doubt that discrimination remains a widespread, difficult problem in europe. i do not exclude the possibility that there may be a need to consider how this could be tackled jointly at supranational level. in this particular case, however, we are dealing with legislation which goes very far, based on a legal principle quite foreign to our own. positive discrimination is itself a questionable method, the registration of race a prerequisite for the latter to function unacceptable, both from a moral and privacy perspective. there are also strong grounds for reservations about the reversal of the burden of proof. i am convinced that our case law, which is based on the principle of innocence until conviction, must be defended, however praiseworthy the initial intention may have been.
in writing. the junilist believes that equality between women and men is a fundamental democratic issue that must be given priority in all countries worldwide. the report contains many proposals on how to achieve this equality, among them emphasis on labour market policy measures, women's participation in political life and various forms of education schemes. it is clear, however, that as usual, the aim is to increase the financial resources of the union, its power and even to carry out costly campaigns in the name of the union. the eu institutions are not the right bodies for these kinds of issues. harmonisation often results in regression for the most advanced countries, as the starting point for different countries is very different. gender equality must be an objective for all member states, but the issue must be dealt with at national level. we have voted against this report, because it seeks to extend the powers of the european union at the expense of the sovereign states of the european union.
madam president, i would like to thank you. by supporting the lamassoure report, the european union can take a historic step. this report is a departure from the days when the number of seats in the european parliament was allocated on the basis of the categorisation of different countries into different groups, the negotiating skills of different heads of government and the assumption that the interests of the eu member states were in conflict. if parliament chooses to support the lamassoure report, we will abandon this system and instead move to one based on a principle of proportionality where the size of countries affects the representation in parliament. the system we have had so far has been very difficult to explain, but the system we can have with the lamassoure report can be explained. it is based on a maximum and minimum level and on taking account of the number of people in the different countries. it is a principle that is also sustainable for the future. this means that it is not the interests of one country or another that are favoured, but the number of citizens that counts. it is a step forward in the democratic process of the european union. i want to make it perfectly clear what options we face. it is a question of supporting the lamassoure report's proposals or of going back to the nice agreement with its arbitrariness and negotiations characterised by conflicting national interests. when i hear the latest speakers from the benches up here and their agitation i also hear the national interests that are dealing with conflicts. the lamassoure report deserves support because it is based on a principle.
in writing. the allocation of seats in the european parliament between the member states must not be arbitrary. in the late nights of the nice summit, member states were basically dependent on the alertness of a single man, the head of government or state, during the night hours. for sweden, the result was extremely bad. although we are only about a million behind hungary and the czech republic in population, we got five fewer seats in the european parliament, 19 instead of 24. it is important that we have a principle of allocation of seats that is fixed over time in view of the continuing enlargement of the union. it is also important to establish the principle of an overrepresentation of smaller states according to a degressive distribution. this report proposes improvements compared with the current distribution of seats in the european parliament. that's why we support it. but we are firmly opposed to the idea of creating a constituency across the eu. this would mean that the size of the national delegations would have to be reduced even further. the creation of a special eu constituency is an artificial way of trying to create a european demos. there is no common political arena in europe. the attempt to break through the barriers of language and tradition by creating the european electorate is doomed to failure.
for the gue/ngl group. thank you, madam president! i would like to thank the rapporteur, who has done a very good job, and also to thank him for his commitment and knowledge in this area, and i would like to say directly that our group fully supports this report. this kind of femicide that we are discussing now has increased, especially in those areas where we have companies investing in factories with lego manufacturing and where women are totally dependent on men, socially, economically, in all areas. the young women employed in these assembly plants are not only at risk of the increased violence but also work under very degrading conditions which reinforce the image of women as inferior beings who can be murdered, tortured, deprived of liberty, etc. i note what the commission has indicated for action, but i would also like to say that i would like to see even tougher and sharper pressure from the eu on these countries. we must show that it is completely unacceptable to sign agreements on respect for human rights while these murders continue. we could freeze trade agreements until we see that the demands we have the right to make are met, until the governments of the countries concerned, for example, put in sufficient resources to find and prosecute the perpetrators. this widespread brutality against women has actually been going on for almost ten years now and instead of decreasing it has actually increased, so it is high time for action. thank you, thank you!
for the ind/dem group. thank you, mr president! i, like my constituents, think that the eu is too closed-minded and bureaucratic. i therefore welcome the commission's proposal, which seeks to increase the transparency of the institutions' expenditure. the agricultural subsidies are questionable. it is therefore important that citizens are allowed to see where their tax money goes and how much. it is regrettable that the committee's amendment goes against the eu's role as a transparent institution. the committee's requirement for registration to access information is a serious invasion of the personal privacy of all citizens, whether farmers, journalists or pre-school teachers. allowing the registration of citizens for the purpose of examining public documents is a development we do not want to see in the eu. i therefore call on all members to vote against the committee's amendments nos 4 and 23. thank you, sir.
the report on airport capacity and ground handling is an own-initiative report with high flying plans for central planning by the eu. so the question is: what kind of society do we want? a market economy or a planned economy? the report notes that europe is facing a sharp increase in airport use and that there is a need to optimise existing resources. notes that the commission has not yet fully explored the possibility of an overall approach to the need for additional capacity; it is about planning future investments and defining strategic hubs. it is believed that it is possible to anticipate the risks of capacity shortages. calls on the commission to start applying a top-down approach; if member states have not taken any significant action in this area before 2008, action should be taken and so on. i do not believe in this overall strategy. i believe that such centrally planned projects are doomed to failure. in the vast majority of cases, there is national competition between countries to meet the needs of aviation. some countries will do well, some countries will fail. the countries that succeed will attract tourism and business. let the countries compare themselves. i also know from my own experience that in a number of countries there are private investors who are building airport capacity and they are guided by investment decisions which are quite different from those of the public authorities. in some cases, it is also a matter of regional decisions. it is impossible to coordinate these three different types of decision, private, public and regional. we should therefore allow the developments to take their course and regulate only the absolutely necessary elements, namely the air traffic control needed to get the systems in order for the air transport within the eu. in my experience, the cost of expanded airport capacity is almost never right. the costs are often three to four times higher than estimated and the projects take twice as long as originally intended. technological progress is also a factor of uncertainty. demand is changing rapidly. suddenly, there are whole new regions that want transport. suddenly, we want new technology with high-speed railways that might make it less interesting to have air connections. let us not therefore believe in a grand strategy. i do not think we should go back to the kind of central planning that existed in the former socialist countries. that is why i will not support this proposal.
in writing. the report by mr chatzimarkaki further strengthens the commission's initiative to increase the legitimacy of community policies by publishing expenditure figures, improving accounting and budgetary control in the agricultural sector, including through a series of amendments. i supported the parts of the report which aimed at increasing the transparency of citizens in the use of funds and the publication of information on beneficiaries. i also supported the requirement that member states should ensure that every year, ex post and via the internet, they publish who received funds from the european agricultural guarantee fund and the european agricultural fund for rural development and the amounts received by each beneficiary from each of these funds. however, i opposed the idea that those who use or view the data should either log in or register, as this would be contrary to the swedish principle of the right to anonymity when accessing such public data. my intention to vote against amendment no 20 in the light of the strong swedish principle of publicity, following a vote wrongly cast on my behalf, has been entered in the minutes.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted for the report. we believe that the principle of degressive proportionality is reasonable and has great potential for support from the eu heads of state or government. the other proposals presented have no realistic prospect of being adopted at the summit. the report gives sweden 20 mandates instead of the 18 we would have under the nice treaty. it is therefore important that the treaty be adopted. otherwise, the treaty of nice will apply, which means that all member states except germany will lose their mandate. the question of how to define the concept of citizen and calculate the demographic base governing the distribution of mandates must be examined. we are very sceptical about national lists for the european parliament elections. if you want to investigate the matter, we can accept that.
in writing. we are firmly opposed to the idea of a european-wide constituency with perhaps 10% of the seats in the european parliament. the creation of a special eu constituency is an artificial way of trying to create a european demos. there is no common political arena in europe with media or debate spanning all member states. each country has its own political agenda. attempts to break through linguistic and traditional barriers by creating eu parties, which are entirely dependent on eu contributions, are doomed to failure. it should be recalled that 54.4% of voters stayed at home in the 2004 european parliament elections. the socialist party is the largest party in the european parliament elections. there is no enthusiasm for elections to the european parliament in the new member states either. in slovakia, 16.96% took part in 2004. with the possible enlargement of the eu to include countries such as ukraine and turkey, the current member states will have their delegations reduced. this affected the smaller groups and reduced political diversity. regional and minority parties will have no place in the european parliament. both the christian democrats/conservatives (ppe-de) and the socialists (pse) seem to have a hidden agenda of trying to create a two-party system in the european parliament. we are voting in favour of the report, not because it is excellent, but because it is an improvement.
in writing. the importance of the question should not be overestimated. a new distribution of members among the member states will not change the current development of the eu. the majority of market liberals and federalists in parliament will be retained. but the report expresses clear principles for a distribution which, by its very nature, will nevertheless be approximate in the end. the distribution will also be better than the present situation, where the largest country gets fewer members while the smallest get more. that is why i voted in favour of the report, even though there are some paragraphs which i do not support, not least the text on the national lists and the european political parties.
in writing. sweden should hold a referendum on the new constitution to get the same exemption and an opt-in clause (like the uk). with such an opt-in clause, sweden, for example, has the right to decide for itself whether and, if so, when to adopt the euro and join the social charter. this does not mean, however, that i do not want a clear document which is the basis of the eu and which clearly defines the scope of the eu's powers. such a document must include rules on the distribution of seats in parliament. i am therefore prepared to support the part of the proposal for a new eu constitution which relates to the distribution of seats. in fact, if the proposal for the distribution of mandates does not conform to the proposal for a new eu foundation, there is a great risk that we will have to go back to the treaty of nice. the treaty of nice means that sweden only has 18 seats, two fewer than under the proposed new eu constitution and two fewer than sweden is entitled to in view of the size of the eu member states.
in writing. the junilists believe that human rights must be upheld in all societies around the world, both in eu member states and in other countries. the report is a proposal for a resolution calling on the member states and the states concerned to work towards the upholding of women's rights. this is a heartfelt question, but the forum in which it is being discussed is not the right one. as usual, paragraphs are included where the eu wishes to act with a united foreign policy voice. with the report's proposals for new budget lines, campaigns and increased powers, the eu is seeking to usurp more power and is using issues such as these as a means in this quest. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. however, the eu is not supposed to pursue a united foreign policy and that is why we have voted against this report.
in writing. the report by anne jenssen deals with a problem which is very much transnational. in other words, it was not the lack of european relevance that finally decided me to vote against most of the amendments and to finally abstain on the report as a whole. the main problem with the report was that it was too detailed in its final version. i find it difficult to see the need for a 'master plan' at eu level on how ground services at airports are to be organised. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. without getting into the substance of the location of, say, bromma airport, i concluded that this must reasonably be a position taken by stockholm and sweden. not for the european parliament.
in writing. the own-initiative report on airport capacity contains high-flying plans of what can be achieved by central planning on the part of the eu. the report notes that europe is facing a sharp increase in airport use and that there is a need to 'optimize' existing resources. it is claimed that future developments show the need for intervention at eu level to help 'eu citizens and take responsibility for the eu economy as a whole'. the infrastructure relating to the airport is even being examined. in the vast majority of cases, there is national competition and between countries and airports and strategic decisions have to be taken at national level. in some countries, airports are privately owned and investment decisions are guided by profitability assessments, while in others they are publicly owned. the idea of european planning in this area is therefore totally unrealistic. we do not know the future demand and it is constantly changing and constantly shifting between different regions and countries. nor can we say how technological developments are likely to affect development. we don't know what the cost of that is either. moreover, all cost estimates tend to be 2-3 times higher than estimated for infrastructure. similarly, the issue of groundhandling services should be a matter for each specific airport and not be regulated at eu level. the report is based on a planned economy. i cannot support this report which is most reminiscent of the systems we could find in the old socialist countries and have therefore voted against.
thank you, mr president. i would like to raise the issue of the decision of the turkish parliament to allow the turkish military to enter the only reasonably calm region of iraq. it now looks as if we will be able to include these events in our discussion on wednesday of the general report. i hope that everyone will have the chance to participate in that process and to table amendments, because this is a major threat to peace in the region. this shows the problems of the military pushing and getting decisions through. now we have to make sure that the government resists, that there are no troops and that no one is provoked into bringing military forces into iraq. if there's one thing they have too much of, it's military. if there's anything they're lacking, it's normal human discussion, and that's what we're going to support. thank you, sir.
madam president, i would like to thank you. time is short, and i am speaking mainly to express my support for the co-rapporteur of the breyer report in the ppe-de group, hennicot-schoepges. i find it very unfortunate that the majority of my group has chosen to depart from the hennicot-schoepge advice on several important points, and thus not to support important proposals in the report. i agree, of course, that the 48-hour information requirement before spraying is impractical and would not work, but to conclude from this that we do not need any information requirement at all is not reasonable. the hennicot-schoepge proposal, as a compromise, is well balanced here. another important issue concerns the exclusion criteria for particularly sensitive substances. first of all, of course, we must be consistent in our decision with the decisions taken by parliament in relation to the new reach legislation. one of the starting points was the phasing out of substances where it is difficult to establish thresholds for the risks of this type of high level concern substances. of course, we have to follow that line here too, especially as we deal with substances that come in close proximity or risk coming in close proximity to things that we are going to eat. another issue on which i support the rapporteur, mr breyer, is the zoning proposal. i was surprised to see that the commission's proposal was to divide europe into zones which are relatively similar in terms of vegetation, climate, etc. but the proposed division is unfortunately too arbitrary. i cannot support this approach and believe that the member states must retain the right to say no to various preparations even if neighbouring countries give the green light. i'd like to take up some more questions, but time is running out.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this is a report which is very much characterized by the compromise between the ppe-de and alde. i think that, on the whole, it is a balanced report. the responsibility of member states for tax policy is established, while there are a number of taxes which would benefit from more eu coordination. the rapporteur's original proposal envisaged a comprehensive coordination with a strong emphasis on higher levels of redistribution - that cannot be the way to make europe grow and create new jobs. in the globalised world we live in, europe does not need higher taxes. rather, i think that those of us who live in high-tax countries should study other models, for example, a flatter tax. in general, the tax should be reduced on labour and increased on harmful consumption. for example, targeted environmental taxes should be introduced, as well as reasonable excise duties on, for example, alcohol and tobacco, as the commissioner points out. the eu must be able to act more coherently in this area. this is a politically sensitive issue - we have heard it here and i know it - but equally in my view necessary. if we are to solve the major climate and energy challenges we face, it would be irresponsible for us to exclude governing fiscal instruments. it is indeed possible to achieve high growth, with high environmental and energy costs, and at the same time to reduce the overall tax burden. those of you who doubt this may study the policy of the bourgeois government in my home country of sweden, if it is allowed to be a little patriotic at the end of the debate.
