for the ind/dem group. thank you, mr president! the budgetary questions have been handled very competently by mr virrankoski and mr itälä. i have great respect for their work, within the framework. but that's the problem, they have to work within the framework. the eu budget process is deeply outdated and therefore subject to criticism. the starting point is always this: more money is coming in, what are we going to put it on? this is a 50-year-old organization, which is why it should be zero-budget. we have to ask ourselves: if we started today (and not 50 years ago), what would we put our budgetary resources into? should we invest them in the creation of the economic and social committee or the committee of the regions? would we spend the other half on a protectionist agricultural policy, on publicity and marketing for products that would otherwise not sell? would we be betting on subsidies for tobacco production, production of tobacco which cannot be sold in the normal way, while we are basically banning smoking in the eu? would we put money into structural funds, which take up almost the entire remainder of the budget, and thereby decide that countries can get money centrally from the eu budget, provided they go to a certain type of regional policy? the answer is -- and i'm sure that everyone in this room, not many, would say, no, we wouldn't do that. so the question is, how do we approach this? we cannot do that unless we are prepared to tackle the fundamental budgetary issues from the start and then work on them. we're in the wrong place. if you do a standard economic analysis and think about what should be done with budget money at eu level that is spent via brussels, it is allocating money that the national states would otherwise not be able to invest, for example in basic research. we all know from economic theory that basic research is totally under-supplied in the market. you get free passengers all the time. it is assumed that someone else is paying for the basic research and that once it is done, it is available to all of us. there are many such areas, and incredibly little money goes there. we talk about it, but we do nothing. the money goes to things that were usually decided 30 or 50 years ago. this is the closest thing to a scandal. we also have an eu that buys up buildings, all over europe, thinking that this is cheaper. it's an irresponsible gamble, speculating with taxpayers' money. this shouldn't continue. we need to get into a debate about what the eu should do with the money we take in. the right thing to do is take maybe 10 to 15 percent of all the money. the rest goes to unreasonable things, and to various kinds of public relations activities, such as money for adaptation to globalization. it is the competition between nation states for good institutional solutions that has created europe and its success. let's keep this. thank you so much!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. many people are now wondering why parliament is calling for an audit of the longterm budget. can we really not cope with new needs such as galileo, the european institute of technology, the eu's actions in kosovo and palestine and at the same time make cuts in the budget? the problem is that the budgetary ceilings apply to the whole budget, but they also apply to each budget category. in order to avoid, for example, galileo leading to cuts in other research programmes, we are calling for this so-called small revision. but note: within the overall ceilings. we would rather have the surpluses in the agricultural budget, that is the point. as regards palestine and the costs of eu action in kosovo, we want to use the so-called flexibility instrument. otherwise we'd have to take money from africa or latin america, and that would be unreasonable. the eu's rigid budget model needs to change. but i want to be clear, i don't want to crack the overall framework, just achieve greater flexibility within the framework to deal with new needs. how else will we be able to respond in the future to the requirements of climate and energy policy, for example? i would like to mention two other things. firstly, the debate on the treaty shows the importance of increased resources and long-term thinking in the eu's efforts to promote communication, dialogue and democratic development. i have subsequently tried to make some adjustments to the committee on budgets' quota in this area. i hope we've all gotten on board with a more offensive approach. secondly, i would like to mention the pilot project which i myself initiated. this concerns support for the rebuilding of cultural and religious institutions and memorials in conflict-affected areas. the proposal is primarily aimed at the western balkans, but in the long term the lessons learnt there should also be applied to other areas where destroyed museums, demolished churches and mosques may constitute a source of conflict management which the eu can help to eliminate. it could be, for example, areas such as palestine, afghanistan, iraq and perhaps even tibet. in that area i appeal to the members for their understanding and support. thank you, thank you!
thank you, mr president. the adoption of the budget each year is one of the most important tasks for us as members of the european parliament. it is one of the few occasions on which we have the opportunity to send a really clear and coherent signal about the kind of europe we want to see here in parliament. in general, i am pleased that more and more of my colleagues in this house seem to see the importance of keeping the eu funds in check, but much remains to be done. i think it is important that we prioritise the budget correctly, but the work of monitoring the money and its use is just as important as budgeting it for the right purpose. if we do that today, i think unfortunately in many areas we see that some of our money is doing almost more harm than good. in addition, the institutions must develop eu accounting systems to make it easier to understand and follow the money, so that it can be seen both before and after it has actually been spent. it's a matter of transparency. i myself would like the eu budget for 2008 to signal more clearly an eu that puts priorities, an eu that focuses on its core task - to create free movement of goods, services, people and capital. but unfortunately, in this year's draft budget there is much more to be desired in that respect. let me give just a few brief examples: subsidies for silkworm farms, beekeeping, cotton farms, energy crops, figs, hemp, nuts, olive groves, rice farms and tobacco. export refunds on cereals, sugar and wine products. the total budgetary lines are over eur 1 858 436 000. apart from the fact that the budget lines are, in my view, an incredible waste of european taxpayers' money, they do more harm than good. they affect people outside the union. the most serious example, i believe, is the eu's support for agricultural exports, whereby the eu subsidises its own agricultural sector and thereby dumps goods and prices on the markets of poor countries. it is a serious obstacle to the natural competitive advantages of poor countries and a serious obstacle to their path to prosperity. we have to do something about this, not in 2013 but next year. thank you, thank you!
for the gue/ngl group. thank you very much! 650,000 people die from smoking every year, and 80,000 die from secondhand smoke every year, in the eu alone. these black figures alone give us a clear picture of the need to do everything we can to combat smoking. karl-heinz florenz has put forward a series of good measures, such as tightening up existing legislation, deterrent labelling on cigarette packs, measures to prevent young people from starting to smoke, measures to help smokers who want to quit their addiction and, in general, measures to support the member states in the fight against smoking. that's good! but i am opposed to shifting more power to the eu in the area of public health, especially when the member states are already doing good work. the smoking ban in the pub is a good example. at least ten eu countries already have some form of smoking ban in restaurants and pubs. it started with ireland in 2004 and quickly spread to sweden, italy, finland, malta, belgium, etc. and many more are on the way. should we now stop this process of good examples and wait for the introduction of central eu legislation? no, i do not think so, but let the good examples continue to spread. i note that florenz says in his opinion that he wants to call on all member states to introduce general smoking bans. that is good, because i interpret that as meaning that we do not have to go around the european commission and, moreover, run the risk of this being lobbied off by the tobacco industry lobbyists. in conclusion, by the way, is there not a risk that we bring in one hand and give in the other? on the one hand, we are calling on people to stop smoking, and on the other hand, the eu continues to subsidise tobacco farming by over eur 1 billion a year. that is 1 000 million euros a year. these subsidies must of course be abolished. hurry up!

thank you so much! i would like to begin by saying that i am fundamentally sceptical about the idea of the eu banning smoking in public places throughout the union. even those who are in favour of this type of smoking ban must see the problem with us doing so at eu level. after all, decisions like these are controversial. if the citizens feel that they are coming from brussels, that someone other than those directly concerned is making these decisions, i think that resistance will increase. however, there are things we can do here in the eu to reduce the harm caused by tobacco smoke throughout europe. we can, for example, abolish the eu's incomprehensible ban on swedish snus. i will again, with the stubbornness of a fool, seize the opportunity and point to the swedish experience. we have the lowest smoking rate in all of europe, we have the lowest rate of all tobacco-related diseases in all of europe, and you know what? we are, however, about the same as the rest of europe in tobacco consumption, but we are using snuff instead of cigarettes. in fact, if the rest of europe replaced cigarettes with snus to the same extent as we have done in sweden, 200,000 europeans would be spared from oral cancer every year. that's why i think it's immoral to continue to turn a blind eye. unfortunately, the commission seems to be reasoning exactly the opposite. today the commission ordered åland to pay almost sek 20 million in fines for not having prohibited snus effectively enough. this is despite the fact that snus is a traditional product in åland, where it has also replaced cigarettes and saved lives. the commission does this and has the audacity to claim that it is well-intentioned and that it is concerned about the health of the people of the islands. but i've talked to them, and they're paying their dues. the commission therefore has no doubt that smoking will increase in åland. i know, however, that there are still colleagues in this house who are sceptical and who do not really believe that it can be that simple, that the statistics are not really as clear as i say. i would urge you to come to my meeting, at least on one point, and vote in favour of jules maatens' proposal to evaluate the health effects of snus. let's put the facts on the table, a scientific basis for further debate. i am convinced that this will be the first step towards lifting the indefensible eu ban on snus. thank you very much, mr president.

mr president, the basis for agreement with the council on this report was a statement made by the commission which was supposed to be read out yesterday during the debate. however, that statement was not read out. if the council would agree to attach it to the minutes or give it some kind of legal status and transparency, then that would help further the process on this issue.
in writing. we firmly reject the report's proposal that oral questions should be read only in the original language of the full text of the outcome of the negotiations. in the european parliament, they try to make a big deal of the speakers speaking to the whole of europe as spokesmen for their european groups, but at the same time they are therefore to deny access to what they are saying afterwards and limit accessibility by cutting down on translations into different languages. if we want a democratically functioning eu, we must be prepared to pay for it. an organisation that spends over £100 billion on protectionist agricultural policies must be able to pay £100 million to show respect for the citizens of the eu. however, if translation costs are deemed insurmountable in the future, the minimum requirement is that the full minutes of the hearing should contain the speaker's original language and a translation into english.
in writing. we do not oppose the suggested temporary solution in order to guarantee the existence of a network for sis 1+ for the period from 13 november to 17 december 2008. however, we cannot support the use of the passerelle in article 67(2), indent 2, of the ec treaty as suggested by mr coelho. that is why we have chosen to vote against the report.
in writing. we have voted against the report because we do not think it is balanced and, in particular, avoids addressing important problems with nuclear energy. we also do not believe that the union's energy research funds should be used to develop new generations of nuclear fission reactors. we are questioning the environmental value of synthetic fuels produced from fossil sources or hydrogen produced from energy of the same origin or nuclear fuel, as none of these energy sources are sustainable in the long term from an environmental or supply point of view. we also believe that fossil fuels should be actively phased out in the long term, which is not covered in the report. we believe that carbon capture can be an important part of reducing carbon emissions, but other energy-saving, efficiency measures and the development of renewable energy are more sustainable in the long term and should be the ultimate goal.
in writing. i have chosen to support the report because the eu has discussed in an unusually balanced way the need to include nuclear energy in the future energy mix in europe. the report states, inter alia, that 'in order to meet basic needs, nuclear energy cannot be abandoned in europe in the medium term' and 'that nuclear power is currently the largest low-carbon energy source in europe and emphasises its potential role in climate protection'. nuclear power currently accounts for one third of the eu's electricity supply and will continue to be one of the most important sources of energy in many eu member states. when the issue of co2 emissions is brought up, it is unfortunate in my view that nuclear energy is not given greater attention. if we are to meet the energy needs of the future without increasing dependence on fossil fuels and rising co2 emissions, the development of new and safe nuclear power will be crucial. unfortunately, nuclear power is not included as part of the measures considered realistic to reach the target of between 20 and 30 per cent by 2020.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the davies report on a strategy to reduce co2 emissions from cars (a6-0343/07). to reduce co2 emissions, the priority is to reduce the share of co2 from road transport. finding more environmentally friendly alternatives to the use of cars is important. but we believe that car manufacturers should be given the opportunity to choose how they want to meet the environmental targets that we politicians set. legislation should not regulate in detail how this should be done. we also oppose the rapporteur's regulation of advertising which is unjustified and restricts freedom of expression.
in writing. we swedish social democrats believe that tax policy should be a national concern. the report also stresses the fiscal sovereignty of the member states. we chose to vote in favour of this report because it emphasises in many ways the role of tax policy in the member states for employment, welfare and the environment and the proper functioning of the internal market.
in writing. the junilists take a strong stand against this report, which aims to bring about a common tax and customs policy for the eu. it is surprising that the european parliament is taking decisions on this day on matters where there is no common policy. deciding on tax matters and promptly appealing to the member states for the harmonization of their national taxes is not for the eu. furthermore, attempts to approximate the introduction of a community tax are completely unreasonable. the report also shows how the lisbon strategy opens the door to the eu to tackle new policy areas, with supranationality, new projects and increased costs in its wake. the junilist group votes against this report, since taxation is a matter for national sovereignty.
in writing. i have today voted in favour of the report on the contribution of tax policy to the lisbon process. i am in favour of sound tax competition and a common consolidated corporate tax base for international companies, without harmonisation of tax levels, and with the possibility for each member state to opt out if it so wishes. it is also important to establish the sovereignty of the member states in the field of taxation. i am very much opposed to any attempt to go against an eu tax.
thank you, madam president. in december, the first summit between the african union and the eu in several years will take place. it is an important meeting for the eu, which has a great responsibility to support the economic and democratic development of africa. the portuguese presidency deserves praise for this initiative. the eu must become more active on the african continent. what concerns me, unlike brian crowley, is that zimbabwe's dictator robert mugabe is likely to be present at this summit. sitting at the same table as mugabe is not worthy of the european union. mugabe's misrule and corruption are paralyzing the entire country. the political opposition is harassed and jailed, free speech is non-existent, food shortages are rife in this country, once the breadbasket of africa, and hundreds of thousands of people are homeless. the economy is falling apart, inflation is over 7,000 percent, the median age is the lowest in the world, and 25 percent of the population is hiv-positive. madam president, commissioner, manuel lobo antunes, one way of showing our disgust with mugabe's dictatorial ways is that the eu does not accept mugabe's presence at the summit. mugabe's terrible rule must be stopped. dictatorships, my friends, are about clarity. thank you, thank you!

madam president, i would like to thank you. i should like to begin by thanking mr marco cappato for an interesting report. i'm on the libe committee, responsible for the long-term budget for the drug program. we are trying to be concrete in our work on how we in the eu are to reduce harmful use and supply. that is why i think the debate on afghanistan is central, because that is where the majority of the heroin (93%) that kills our young people in the streets comes from. if opium production for medicinal purposes can be used to bring about a change in the control and production of some farmers under the supervision of the eu and the un, then let us say yes to a project. it comes from me, who comes from sweden with a very powerful anti-drug and drug policy. unfortunately, i do not think that is enough, but we must of course also look at other possibilities, such as energy production. but farmers need jobs and livelihoods, and that is why we in the european parliament must take responsibility and demand more action. 40% of gdp, we're going to replace it, take responsibility for it! as regards paragraph 1 (a) to the council not to use disinfection to eradicate the pollinators, i have a different view. i think we also have to look into it, to break the deadlock we are in now where nothing is happening. finally, a word on overproduction. that does not mean that there is no more need for painkillers for the world's poorest, for women and children. they use very little painkillers compared to us in the eu. so let us look globally and find constructive solutions in the eu and the un with afghanistan, for peace and democracy, against terrorism and drugs. thank you, thank you!
thank you, madam president. the ombudsman's criticism of the lack of transparency is a necessary and much needed criticism. i also welcome the parliament's proposal that the ombudsman should investigate the practical impact of the critical remarks made against the commission, among others. it is true that parliament is often unanimous in its criticism of other institutions, but there are indeed good reasons for parliament to be self-critical. the ombudsman has recently requested parliament to disclose information on the remuneration of members for travel and staff, but parliament has refused to disclose the information, claiming that this would be in breach of data protection law. i think that this is just putting out the blinds, and i have the support of the european data protection supervisor in saying that. staff remuneration is, of course, a public document. it is time for parliament to clear the slate on the issue of compensation. only the person who hires staff with slave contracts and other strange contracts has anything to fear from more publicity. it is not possible that any member here is afraid of not being re-elected in 2009 if it comes out in the media how employees are treated in certain cases, let me say in certain cases. thank you, thank you!
in writing. we are opposing a continued expansion of the eu budget, which today is already too extensive, with a large part of the funds going to the wrong ends. today there seem to be a reciprocal action between new objectives for the union and demands on financing by the eu. this means new demands on payments from the member states, but also that new funds are being transferred to a system which is bureaucratic, inefficient and complex. today there is also too large a focus on industrial agriculture and foreign policy and military projects, and not enough on the environment and social projects. however, there are many desirable initiatives being financed via the budget, and we support reasonable changes to existing means. we did choose to support the specific amendment 945, since it covers, among other things, aid to palestine, even though there were other aspects of the amendment of which we do not approve.
in writing. i have chosen to vote as much as possible in favour of the council's draft budget for 2008 and therefore against all the increases in expenditure proposed by the european parliament. it is extremely unfortunate that parliament wants to finance galileo from the community budget. if it turns out that private operators are leaving because of profitability problems, the eu should take the consequences and shut down the project or let interested member states in, as it did with boeing. i have also been involved in the question of the abolition of subsidies for tobacco growers and support for wine production, but i can only regret that parliament is not ready to support these proposals.
in writing. today we swedish social democrats voted on the eu budget for 2008. we consider the proposal as a good compromise and, although we lost on some points, we support the whole proposal. we voted for a reduction in agricultural export subsidies, but we think it is unreasonable to vote for a total and immediate abolition of subsidies. we also voted against continuing to support tobacco growers and against limiting the number of parliamentary sessions in strasbourg. in the case of daphne (protecting children, women and young people from violence), we voted for an increase in the appropriation up to the commission's proposal. we were prepared to vote for more money, but we were not given the chance to do so because of that amendment which never came to a vote. in the resolution to virrankoski (a6-0397/2007) we swedish social democrats voted against an amendment which, among other things, called on the commission to ensure that no farmer receives more than eur 50 000 in direct support from the eu per year. this is an ongoing discussion, so we agree with the spirit of the proposal, but do not think it appropriate to fix either an exact amount or specific conditions at this stage.
in writing. we swedish social democrats in the european parliament voted in favour of the report. the deal is a step in the right direction. the impact of the agreement on fish stocks and the livelihoods of the local population is being monitored more closely. it is now important to monitor the effects of the agreement closely. we voted against amendment no 7. we fully support the intention to promote the sustainable development of fisheries, which is also the aim of the new fisheries agreement. the agreement shall also not have adverse effects on the fisheries and the population of madagascar. however, we cannot support the immediate termination of the agreement if it has negative consequences. we believe that the joint committee to be set up to monitor the agreement is a better solution to ensure that the agreement is respected and that it benefits both parties and the environment. the agreements shall be renegotiated if it is shown that this has adverse consequences. we are in favour of a greater share of the costs being financed by the european vessels benefiting from the agreements. however, we believe that the objective must be that the whole agreement should be self-financing.

for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. this report on the role of sport in education sets out in detail how member states should organise the subject of sport in schools. it is yet another example of how eu politicians and officials go into all areas and all levels of detail in their eagerness to centralize political power here. subsidiarity is celebrated in the party talk but never respected in the political practice. this is a record breaker in ridiculous detail. let me give you a few examples. paragraph 10 states that member states are to be encouraged to make sport compulsory in primary and secondary schools and that at least three sports lessons per week are to be guaranteed. paragraph 24 refers to the importance of offering gender-blended sports lessons for pre-school and primary school children. this is how it's done. it is a shameless and ridiculous attempt to advance the eu's positions in an area which is for the european people to decide.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. climate change is a transboundary environmental problem and the eu is a key player in global climate action. emissions are rising globally and time is running out. the swedish junilist therefore supports the main points of the proposal which is being debated tonight. the integration of aviation into the eu emissions trading system is a self-evident and cost-effective step in climate action. but it is not trade itself that leads to emissions reductions, but the overall emission ceiling that we choose to set. the system must be broadened to include additional climate-impacted sectors to achieve the climate goals we have committed to. we are in favour of the committee's proposal for a uniform date for the directive to enter into force. it is unacceptable that the commission has been influenced by international political pressure to delay climate action and distort competition. moreover, the directive applies to everyone. we cannot allow exceptions for particular persons or events. our heads of state must pay their expenses, as do the rest of us. anything else would be unreasonable. finally, i would point out that common environmental objectives should not be used as a means of introducing a common tax policy. the revenue from the action will not be transferred to the eu as proposed in amendment no 14. each member state must be free to decide how it wants to use the proceeds from the sale of its emission allowances.
the airline has to pay for its environmental costs, and that's why this proposal is good. air transport cannot be exempted from environmental costs paid by other modes of transport. but i have a hard time with the air-hunting hysteria that is so much in the air, as if it were the biggest environmental problem and the best thing for us to do is to stop flying altogether. because it's simply not true. air travel accounts for five percent of carbon dioxide emissions. a full plane uses less fuel per passenger-kilometer than a car. it can and must be even better, but this is by putting tough demands on the aviation industry, which is driving the development forward. not by stopping flying. traveling and meeting people from other parts of the world is important. i am convinced that ryanair has done more for the understanding between the peoples of europe than all the eu cultural projects put together. reduced travel must therefore not become an intrinsic value. isolating people takes us back in time, to a dark time. the commission's proposal is in the right direction, but its perspective is cynical. that someone else should fly less. that attempts have been made to exclude the air travel of heads of government and state, while demanding that citizens take responsibility. proposals like these create a well-deserved contempt for politicians, and i am glad that we in the committee on the environment have removed those very words. equally cynical is the demand by some of our colleagues on the left that we cut air travel by more than half in just a few years. those who make such proposals are hardly taking the train from stockholm to brussels, for example. like your own travels are priceless but other people's travels are unimportant. with this kind of frivolous proposal, it's the ordinary people who are forced to pay, not politicians and business leaders, where someone else takes the credit. there will be students who can no longer afford to study abroad to go out and see the world. there will be grandmothers in norrland who won't be allowed to see their grandchildren in the big city as often. it takes us back to a time when an sas cabin, in the '80s, was just business executives, politicians and union thugs. instead, tomorrow we will vote for tough but realistic demands on the airline industry, which will force them to pay for their environmental costs. it is a question of forcing forward better and more environmentally friendly aviation technology, but above all of making it possible to prioritise. to take the plane against the real carbon sinks, like coal-fired power plants. because the important thing is that emissions are reduced not flight.
in writing. with all due respect to the substance of the matter, we would point out that the european union could have done without this report. this european parliament should be concerned with matters of a narrower scope and not with everything between heaven and earth. the european parliament's committee on culture and education should be abolished as it deals with matters outside the scope of eu cooperation. the committee mainly produces reports proposing that the union should intervene and regulate the areas of detail for which the eu has no competence. in the current eu cooperation, the member states are exclusively responsible for the organisation of education. cultural matters are also a matter for the member states and the union currently has very limited cooperation on cultural programmes. for these reasons, the european parliament's committee on culture and education has no reason to exist.
in writing. we do not consider that the amendments tabled by the committee on fisheries add anything positive to the commission's resolution, but rather a step backwards. we cannot accept any reduction in transparency, which is what the report de facto implies.
in writing. we, the swedish social democrats, decided to abstain in the final vote (a6-415/2007). the reason for this is that the report addresses a number of important aspects of sport in education but at the same time issues which are and should remain a national concern. we voted against the texts in paragraphs 10, 24 and 47. we share the view that school sport is important and that volunteering is an important part of the sport movement in europe. whether you should have 2, 3 or 4 hours of exercise a week is not something the eu should interfere with. of course, it is also important that gender equality is an integral part of the curriculum and that sports teachers are trained in the subject. we also share the view that mixed-gender sports lessons are a good idea, but this is not an eu issue either. we would like to stress, however, that we share the report's view that sport is important for several reasons. on the one hand, it can make an important contribution to countering adverse health trends such as obesity. furthermore, by its educational nature, it can contribute to social added value for society at large.
in writing. many positive objectives in the report such as work on public health, anti-discrimination and accessibility for disabled people, but despite this there are too many calls and demands for detailed regulations which should not be made at eu level, and i therefore abstain in the final vote.
in writing. few question the importance of school-age children having sports instruction in school. the negative trend of unhealthy lifestyles among youths seems to be intensifying from year to year. the schmitt report therefore deals with an urgent and important subject. the problem with the report, particularly with regard to the specific points which were voted on, is that mr schmitt goes too far. it is difficult to see why the eu should be the one to regulate in detail the number of hours of sport in schools, not only for primary school pupils but also for secondary school pupils. i also see no reason for brussels to regulate sports education in detail, with requirements for mixed-gender groups and special training for the gendered. the last paragraph, which seeks to establish a 'special reward system' for different types of voluntary activity, is particularly remarkable. it is not that the spirits of europe do not deserve appreciation. it is because it is difficult to see how such a system would be designed, administered and financed. the report is undoubtedly well-intentioned. the current design, however, was very doubtful. that's why i voted no.
in writing. we swedish social democrats have chosen to support parliament's report as a first step towards reducing the climate impact of aviation. we regret, however, that a majority of this parliament is in favour of both high emission ceilings and the auctioning of a small proportion of the emission allowances. the entry into force of the directive has also been brought forward by one year from the date decided by parliament's committee on the environment. in the long term, we would prefer to see aviation have its own emissions trading system.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. i thank the rapporteur for a well-balanced report on a very important subject. few things are more important to our union than being able to act with force when war breaks out or when natural disasters strike. the eu has the will and the means, but more is needed. let me touch on two sensitive points. firstly, in my view, it is reasonable in some cases to use military action to protect humanitarian operations. in darfur, we need immediate arms protection to guarantee the humanitarian efforts. here, the first thing to do is to look at the outcome - saving lives. secondly, aid can be used to promote democratic development. here too we must look at the result. let me give you a concrete example that the commissioner is familiar with. in eritrea, a swedish journalist has been imprisoned for more than six years, simply for exercising his freedom of expression. today, thirty swedish editors-in-chief are demanding that a link be made between swedish aid and the imprisonment of david isaak, as his name is. i'm inclined to agree with them. eritrea should know that the will to provide aid can also be lost if democratic principles are so flagrantly abused as in eritrea today.
madam president, commissioner, the portuguese presidency! i prefer to look at the opportunities of globalisation rather than the problems, but that depends on how we act in europe. i am in favour of investing in research and development, of making more long-term investments in good environmental conditions and of investing in people and lifelong learning, but what the commission forgets - the mistake in the commission document - is that it forgets the social dimension. the trend in europe today is one of good growth and more jobs, but also greater exclusion, greater disparities and more precarious jobs, more jobs - not least in germany - that are not viable but require social benefits to supplement their wages. we must link growth and employment with a social dimension that reduces the gaps in europe between people and regions. this was the subject of the debate in guimarães, which i attended during the meeting of the ministers for employment. the portuguese presidency is trying to push the issue of the integrated guidelines and change them and make the social link much clearer and much more integrated. but the commission does not want to. the commission does not intend to change the guidelines. we need to change the policy. in our resolution we agreed that we wanted new guidelines which integrated the social dimension and of course also addressed the issues of security in change and 'flexicurity'. the commission should also take up this matter, so that we can establish a stronger link between growth issues and the social dimension. we must also anchor the lisbon strategy. it is not anchored today at the member state, regional and local level. many people are not familiar with the lisbon strategy, and we must integrate it and get the social partners and civil society to work together to ensure that these issues - the social dimension, growth and employment - are important and are integrated.
in writing. we have chosen to vote in favour of the report even though some eu countries already have fully functioning legislation in the field of soil protection. we supported amendments nos 106, 107, 108 and 110, which read as follows: member states which have already introduced specific national legislation to protect their soil shall be exempted from the obligations laid down in this article, provided that their legislation guarantees at least an equivalent level of protection. although the directive may be seen as excessive in some member states, we are hopeful that it can bring about an improvement in the large number of member states which currently lack a functioning legislation on soil protection. we hope that the member states which have functioning legislation in this area will also work with parliament in the further negotiations to ensure that the directive does not create unnecessary administrative duplication for them once it has entered into force.
in writing. a framework directive on soil protection is not needed. the market situation in the member states is different. the problems addressed by the directive are national in nature and are therefore best dealt with at national level. the necessary soil protection is already regulated by existing eu and national legislation. the proposal only leads to more bureaucracy and more cumbersome rules for the parties involved. detailed rules and calls are typical eu inventions that lead to increased costs and irritation throughout the eu apparatus. in sweden, some of us question the compatibility of the directive with the eu's regulatory work and want the swedish parliament to examine the proposal under the subsidiarity principle. we have therefore chosen, on principle, to reject the proposal as a whole.
in writing. we have voted in favour of the motion as a whole. it is important to introduce and respect international standards in order to protect the financial system against crime and money laundering in general, while ensuring that member states' own measures are not incompatible with the rules of the internal market. however, we find it repugnant that the eu, by frequently using the term 'terrorism', is seeking to extend its power at the expense of its member states. funding terrorism is a modest part of money laundering. the large sums go to other types of well-organized crime.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report because we believe that there is a need to increase knowledge of bzp, which is a new synthetic drug, and to subject the substance to control measures and criminal sanctions. however, we voted against all the proposals aimed at preventing the introduction of control measures and criminal sanctions, which are contrary to the precautionary principle.
in writing. we believe that freedom of the press is an important principle. officials should be able to provide information, obtain information in order to exercise the right to freedom of expression and information, the right to anonymity and a prohibition of investigations, which means that authorities and other public bodies cannot investigate who has disclosed information under the freedom of information, without risk of reprisals.

of course of the utmost importance. parliament is preparing itself by this debate and a resolution, and the council has also prepared itself by a resolution. what is still lacking, in my view, is a clearer cooperation between the various eu institutions. the portuguese presidency has a strong responsibility here. the european union must speak with one voice in bali. the commission will be able to make a contribution to the development of the european union's energy policy. they need our support. not just in fine words, but they need money, methodological development and access to new technologies. we need to focus our efforts on different policy areas and aid needs to be changed to be climate-friendly as well. part of the solution also relates to the second point i have been intending to mention in the debate on forests. large areas are being cleared today and we all know what that means for the climate. but it is also a disaster for the people who live in these areas when their livelihood is lost. we need to develop a working method where we pay developing countries and ordinary families to take care of and maintain forest areas. the very idea of sustainable start of the negotiations is a good one. this debate is an excellent tool, but we must also prepare it together with our friends in a strong transatlantic dialogue as well as dialogue with china and india. we know that 25 states emit 83 per cent of the gases we need to stop and, commissioner and minister, a true friend is a person who asks how you are and also stops to listen to the answer.
we're about to decide how to fight global warming after 2012, when the kyoto protocol expires. we have a very good base. we are calling for reductions of up to 80 percent by 2050, support for developing countries to reduce their emissions, action against the meat industry which accounts for almost a fifth of global greenhouse gas emissions, more flexible patent laws to make it easier to spread green technology, certification of biofuels to avoid them coming into conflict with the food supply and the preservation of the world's forests. all of this is, as i said, great. what is missing and what we must do in the future is to address the ever-growing traffic flows within the eu, the eu subsidies and the fact that the market tends to be supreme when the eu legislates and implements. there are eleven amendments, and most of them i think are good and reinforce the focus of the resolution, namely that it is the rich world that is responsible for climate change and therefore must take the lead in radical reductions. i am concerned about amendment no 7, which calls for the use of nuclear energy to combat the greenhouse effect. we're not going to replace one environmental problem with another.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is almost 15 years since the rio conference signed the climate convention, and yet it is a fact that climate emissions are increasing faster today than ever before. this shows how insufficient international cooperation has been so far. the eu must continue to take a leading role through domestic action, from tougher requirements for the cars of tomorrow to increased support for alternative energy sources. but 'domestic action' is not enough. karl heinz florenz asked what we can offer the rest of the world. that's a good question. i think we can offer three things. firstly, clean, environmentally friendly technologies to all the developing countries in the process of modernization, not least china and india. they have every right to develop, but they don't have to repeat our mistakes. support for technology and knowledge must be a priority in the eu budget. it's a win-win for them, but it's a win-win for us, too. equally important is to take our historic responsibility and provide support for adaptation measures to all the low-income countries that will be hit hard by storms, floods and prolonged droughts. the money allocated to various adaptation funds and the recent initiative of the commission, the 'climate alliance', are not enough. they're ridiculously small. the needs are a hundred times greater. thirdly, as mrs ek stressed, it is important to take account of the role of forests, especially tropical forests. we need to give forest owners an incentive not to cut down the forest but to preserve the forest. climate policy is not going to stand or fall with bali, but a successful outcome in bali will of course increase the chances of reaching a final agreement in 2009. to facilitate this, i assume that the commission and the council will listen to parliament, not least on the need to do much more in terms of technological cooperation, adaptation measures and forestry issues.
for the gue/ngl group. i am not sure that this is the case. this is a good indication of the lack of social security in which too many citizens of the member states are forced to live. the survey also shows the links between poverty, overcrowding, social exclusion and increased ill health and shorter life expectancy. the mapping addresses the importance of the right to housing, work, social security and women's and disability rights. it also shows that gambling, alcohol consumption, drug use and smoking increase social exclusion. it is therefore clear that the member states must address these problems. so far, all is well, but reality is very often different. these are often just words, because the eu, with its various directives and regulations, often prevents the member states from solving the problems by referring to the rules of the internal market and competition. in addition, wine producers and tobacco growers are subsidized. the free movement of goods, for example, prevents sweden from maintaining a restrictive, solidarity-based alcohol policy, which is very important. sweden has a gambling monopoly to restrict gambling, but then the eu threatens with the court. the same double standard applies to the text on legislation on misleading advertising and advertising messages to children, whereas the eu decides on television directives with product placement and advertising to children. it is possible to change the social reality, but then public health and environmental concerns must take precedence over the competition rules of the internal market, and social welfare and security must take precedence over the deregulation of privatisations. gue/ngl votes in favour of the report and we will continue to do everything we can to improve social security for citizens.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would also like to begin by thanking mrs lind for her excellent work and excellent report. i myself was invited to the azores conference where we, the commission and parliament, held a dialogue with civil society on these issues. i would also like to commend the commission for taking these issues seriously in its new communication on social exclusion and for putting forward a recommendation next year which raises these issues. because it's exactly as mr. spidla says: the situation in europe is generally quite good, with increased growth and more jobs. but when we see this, the gaps are also widening. the gaps are widening and poverty is increasing. there are major differences between member states. in some countries the differences are enormous, and i am not talking about new and old member states - there are new member states with small gaps and there are old member states with large gaps. this is something we have to deal with. i think the commission's three-pillar strategy is quite good. in the case of work, work is not enough, because there are also bad jobs that do not provide a livelihood and do not give influence. it must be good jobs that pay good wages. we in the committee looked at the situation with regard to minimum incomes and saw great differences between member states. we need to exchange experience to find best practice. then there are the public services, which must be available to everyone: social services, housing, health care and so on. the methods are the open method of coordination and we shall strengthen it. i would also like to mention before i end my speech that we are voting today on parliament's position on, among other things, the forthcoming integrated guidelines. we must also include the social dimension in the integrated guidelines, so that we link up growth and employment issues with a social dimension, so that we do not see these issues in isolation from each other but as a whole.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the european parliament has now voted to update and amend the integrated guidelines. the social dimension is one example. so far the commission has not wanted to hear it. i would like to hear mr spidla's comments on the decision now taken by parliament and whether he will pursue the issue of updated and amended integrated guidelines within the commission.
in writing. schengen cooperation must be open to all member states wishing to join and participate in it. however, it is of the utmost importance that all countries participating in cooperation meet all the requirements already at the time of entry, since cooperation involves a common external border. if there are shortcomings in a country, sweden has no possibility to control border protection. the audits carried out show that there are problems that need to be addressed and it would be reasonable to wait for entry until all requirements have been met.
in writing. the undersigned swedish social democrats in the european parliament wish to make clear in this explanation of vote how we voted on the braghetto report (a6-0408/2007). we think it is very important to establish a recovery plan for tuna. the tuna fishery has long been threatened by overfishing. in the long term, this risks damaging the entire ecosystem of the fisheries concerned. we do not think the commission's resolution is fully sufficient, however, and we voted against it. we voted in favour of committee amendments nos 4 and 6. two amendments which remove two exceptions for tuna fishing which we consider to be questionable. we also supported amendment no 13, which would have meant that we would have adopted a recovery plan worthy of the name. the proposal went much further than the original proposal, which we fully support. the state of tuna stocks remains seriously low and the european union must take greater responsibility for the recovery of tuna stocks.
in writing. the current political climate in pakistan is certainly critical, following the declaration of a state of emergency by the president and the clear violations of human rights committed through imprisonment, violence against peaceful demonstrators and the silencing of the media. we are firmly opposed to this, but we are also opposed to the european parliament making another attempt, through this resolution, to allow the eu to take over the foreign policy of the independent member states. the un is the only body with the competence and authority to apply pressure on behalf of its members in the global community. it is very urgent to restore stability and peace and to restore respect for human rights in pakistan, but this is not a task which the eu should take away from the member states.
in writing. we support the resolution dealing with the upcoming bali conference. the resolution expresses several important points. among other things it clarifies the significant amount of emissions which are emitted from the livestock sector. it also emphasises the importance of relevant support to developing countries in their prevention and mitigation work to limit the negative effects of climate change. however, we do not support the proposal that nuclear energy should be seen as a tool which is necessary to prevent climate change.
in writing. environmental issues are transboundary and therefore the un climate conferences are an important diplomatic arena for change at the global level. the resolution calls for the eu to bring the member states together under a common banner to push the negotiations of the conference in what the eu considers to be the right direction. the fight against climate change is a good goal for something, but it is regrettable that global environmental policy in the hands of the eu is crossing the border into foreign policy, while dictating to third countries as well as to member states what their national climate policy should look like. the junilista believes that each country should have its own voice at the climate conference and we have therefore chosen to vote against this resolution.
in writing. the junilists are in favour of increased trade and deepening economic relations with ukraine. such a development would benefit both actors in the short and long term. however, it is clear that the underlying aim of this report is not only to develop economic relations but also to pursue a foreign policy on the part of the eu. the tone of the report is clearly commanding and the proposals for cooperation are made almost exclusively on eu terms and with the eu's own interests at the forefront. that is why the junilista has voted against this report.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted for the report. it is important to intensify the fight against poverty and social exclusion in europe. we would like to make our position clear. paragraph 32 speaks of the establishment of a decent minimum wage at member state level. the report clearly states that this should be done with the social partners where appropriate. we therefore believe that the collective agreement model we have chosen in the nordic countries is covered by the text.
in writing. the report also calls for the eu to take action to combat poverty and social exclusion. we have been very active in national policy on many of the proposals, such as labour immigration and increased opportunities to combine work and family life. the report also calls for the eu to take action to ensure that the eu's citizens are not left behind. for example, we do not think that the european parliament should be involved in treatment programmes in prisons or in the treatment of gambling addiction. the european parliament should not have any say in the way member states choose to run their health care systems, and we reject the claim that deregulation would reduce the quality of care. labour market policy measures such as the introduction of minimum wages should also be left to national decision-making.
in writing. there are many praiseworthy writings on anti-discrimination and the importance of equal treatment in this report which deserve attention. unfortunately, it also contains extremely far-reaching proposals on the introduction of minimum wages, among other things. sweden is against politically determined minimum wages. whatever one thinks of as a reasonable social policy, the eu must accept that member states choose different solutions. this must not be another example of the eu going in and making detailed rules in an area which must be entirely at the level of the member states. moreover, the eu countries are at vastly different levels of development, and the introduction of minimum wages is preventing the poorest countries from catching up.
mr president, vice-president of the commission, council, members and, above all, the citizens of europe! today is a day of celebration, a great day, a day of joy, a very important day, far more important than many people realize right now. this applies both to those who believe in fundamental rights as a principle and to those who believe in the development of europe and the community. it should have been a matter of course for a long, long time that the eu institutions should be bound by the values we all stand for, but that has not been the case. in fact, even the british believe in the rule of law, no matter how it is established. few members of parliament would say that it is fun to help abolish human rights, when the vast majority think just the opposite. it has been a pleasure and an honor to be involved in developing these values, which i am convinced mean so much to us. now everyone knows what this union stands for, even if one does not have the courage to read the whole treaty. these are fine values, these are good values, these are values to which we should all contribute and ensure that this whole union also helps to implement them properly. a warm thanks to jo leinen, to all those involved and, not least, a warm congratulations to the citizens!
the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur for a good job. the changes brought about by globalisation bring new and better opportunities for all the countries of the world, but they also bring challenges. europe is at a crossroads. we can choose to welcome the new and flexible economy and its opportunities or to embrace various forms of protectionism. 'flexicurity' is therefore one of the most important tools for creating a labour market which, as the rapporteur says, fully exploits the strength and power of the workforce. education, mobility and employability are keywords. no model is one-size-fits-all, of course, but we can and should learn from each other. we in the committee on economic and monetary affairs point out in our opinion that too rigid protection systems, while protecting those who are in, can make it difficult for others to enter the labour market. the demographic trends in europe are also a problem, as the rapporteur also pointed out. more people need to get to work. 'flexicurity' is therefore a good model for europe to continue to develop positively in a global economy. the example of denmark, which the rapporteur did not mention, illustrates this in my view. mr president, there is one thing we should all be able to agree on in this house, and that is that too many people are out of work today. europe must grow sustainably so that new jobs are created.
mr president, mr president of the council, mr commissioner! i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for a well-done job and an excellent report. like josé albino silva peneda, i prefer to use the expression 'confidence in change', the change we are facing in the form of globalisation, in the form of demographic change. i think that's a better description. there is a difference between the commission proposal and the parliament's proposal, namely where the focus is on the changes. we have a different focus in parliament's proposal. the commission's focus is on job security versus job security. there is no such contradiction. it is possible to combine a sense of security with a new job and still have a strong sense of job security. we in parliament are trying to focus on participation in the process, strong trade unions and strong social dialogue. we are focusing on active labour market policies, we are focusing on more education and training measures and we are focusing on strong social security systems. many have said that it is not a model but that everyone must start from his starting points. that is where the lisbon process comes in. now that we're getting down to the basics, i want to highlight what stephen hughes said: look at paragraph 15, see what principles should be guiding. finally, i would like to say to mrs schroedter that it is not that the rapporteur has not had any contact with the european trade union movement. there have been close contacts throughout, and they fully support us trying to change the focus. to refrain from having views before the ministers for employment have them would be to give them the final say. parliament must have a line... member of the commission. mr president, it is always a pleasure for a mother to see her child grow up to be a bright and lively teenager. that is how i feel about our directive on audiovisual media services without frontiers tonight and it is a feeling of satisfaction and pride that i share with the godmother of the child, our excellent rapporteur, ruth hieronymi. the evidence of the child's intelligence is manifold. mrs hieronymi has just mentioned them: a scope adapted to the audiovisual of the future, because it has been extended to audiovisual media on demand such as vod; the reaffirmation of the principle of the country of establishment, and therefore the anchoring of the freedom of movement of programmes, while adding a procedure for dialogue and intelligent cooperation to prevent or resolve possible conflicts; a balance between respect for consumers and additional freedom for our businesses; strengthening the right to information with the new mechanism on access to short extracts of important events. all these and other developments, in my view, are evidence of an intelligent balance between renewal and respect for values. as proof of the vitality of the child, i would mention the promotion of cultural diversity in the digital environment, the recognition given to new advertising techniques, a legal framework finally secured for product placement, the attention finally paid to access to audiovisual media services for our citizens with visual or acoustic disabilities, the trust placed in industry by providing for the implementation of the directive through self-regulation or co-regulation. i would like to thank parliament for its very positive role in helping me to reach adolescence. this is yet another example of excellent cooperation between the three institutions, which has succeeded in making successful legislation a basic element for the industry and culture of tomorrow. now the child has to leave his parents, take off, become an adult. for a community directive, this means that transposition by the member states takes over. in line with european policy, i hope that this passage will not make the child fatter, which would be
