the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. first of all, i would like to congratulate and thank our rapporteur for the work he has done on this matter, as well as the commissioner. i would like to say that this is a subject on which there are many different and very strong opinions, but we have nevertheless produced a proposal which we are discussing here tonight which will take european television forward. there are a few things i think are important to stress, amongst others that we are establishing the principle of the law of the country of origin, which means a better and stronger basis for diversity but also for a common european television and - which is important - better conditions for a european film industry, because they are very closely linked. it also means more space for free media to exist across europe's borders. of course, some things could have been even better. i myself would have thought it would have been good if we had more openness about advertising time, but this creates more flexibility. i think that the proposal which has now come back on product placement is also an improvement. we have therefore reason to be satisfied with the steps being taken. let me just say one thing for the future, because this legislation is based very much on the fact that there is a difference between what is called linear and non-linear broadcasting. i think that distinction will play less and less of a role in the future. we can already see today that this difference is not so great and relevant. i think it will be important to monitor developments in this area so that we do not get into a situation where our traditional television media in europe are in a worse situation than those broadcast non-linearly, via the internet and in other contexts, because it can fundamentally damage our long-term prospects in the global perspective. i would like to thank the rapporteur once again and to say that we have made a step forward.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the proposed amendments to the eu's arms directive aim to improve the control of weapons. there will be better labelling and stricter requirements on the trade and manufacture of arms. that is good, and it is particularly good that the committee wants to make this even more clear. this is particularly important in the light of the tragic shooting deaths at jokela school in finland a few weeks ago. the combination of young people on the move, the spread of a culture of violence on the internet and the availability of weapons is unfortunately life-threatening. that is why the important adjustments we are about to make are very important. it is also good that this is a minimum directive. the member states can therefore take the lead and have more progressive legislation. i wish all eu legislation were like that. that would solve a lot of problems. it looks like we can agree on a first treatment. that's good, because it saves us time and resources that we can use for other things, like promoting a more peaceful and child-friendly society.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. hunting is an ancient custom. each member country has its own unique hunting tradition to preserve. today's debate is therefore the culmination of a long process. strong wills have been opposed, discussions have been numerous and shadow rapporteurs have been barred from participating in the tripartite meetings. several of the original proposals could have threatened hunting cultures in the member states. unfortunately, the european firearms pass has not been sufficient as the only necessary document for the temporary use of hunting and competition weapons in another member state. the free movement is impeded by the fact that some member states are allowed to request additional documents. community hunters and marksmen are exposed to more bureaucracy than third country nationals. fortunately, the fees for these permits have been banned. during the process, as shadow rapporteur, i have tried to influence the final report on the occasions when i have been allowed to participate in the meetings. i have worked on two issues in particular, namely against a ban on ordering weapons on the internet and against changes in the possibilities for exemptions for courses for agricultural and sport shooting schools. in a long-distance region, a ban on the purchase of guns online would prevent the availability of guns for hunting purposes. today, we already have a good regulation for internet purchases in sweden, which is accepted by both hunters and authorities. the second question was about the revision of the criteria for the old-age pension, which would affect different types of secondary school programmes. in sweden, hunting education plays an important role in teaching future generations about hunting and wildlife management. now our hunting traditions can live on. from being a cumbersome bureaucratic proposal, the compromise, while not ideal, has now become acceptable.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. madam president, i would like to thank you. it is high time that world trade also took its climate responsibility. since 1990, world trade has exploded. what does that lead to from a climate perspective? yes, of course, to increased transport and increased emissions. for example, is it reasonable for eu livestock farmers to import millions of tonnes of soya from brazil for the european meat industry, or for fish to be caught in norway, shipped on to china for filleting and purification, and then back to europe for preservation? no, of course not! our excellent report gives us the opportunity to take concrete action against this. we demand that transport bears its environmental cost. we want to spread green technology to developing countries by fundamentally changing patent and intellectual property rights, for example. we want to eliminate subsidies for dirty energy production. we want mandatory environmental certification of biofuels and that all trade agreements be analysed from a climate perspective. these are just a few examples from this excellent report. by doing this, we can ensure that world trade becomes part of the solution, not part of the problem.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. for the 13th consecutive year, the court of auditors has thus failed to implement the eu budget. why does this happen? are eu politicians, bureaucrats and citizens notorious cheaters? of course not! the problem is that the eu wants to regulate the conditions in a region of 23 countries and half a billion people. this is what opens the door to cheating, abuse and error. the whole organization needs to be reformed from the ground up. there are two ways to go. firstly, we must move from perverse detailed regulations to systems where poor member states receive their aid without detailed rules on how it is to be used. secondly, we must ensure that the guilty are actually identified. this in turn requires maximum transparency, that whistleblowers are treated as heroes and not traitors, that journalists are welcome to investigate the eu's shenanigans. none of these three requirements is actually met. a recent example is the fate of journalist hans-martin tillack. he discovered fraud in eurostat but was instead accused of a crime by olaf. he lost in the belgian courts and in the ecj, but now he has been acquitted by the european court of justice. the role of the eu in this sad history shows that we have a long way to go if we want to break down its bureaucratic establishment. is there that will? i doubt that.
in writing. the roure report caused a lot of headaches. i am of course in full agreement with the ambition to combat racism and xenophobia on several fronts wherever they arise. there are good reasons to discuss legislation at eu level. ideas, good and bad, move quickly between borders and people. legal protection is, in its own way, deficient in europe. at the same time, there is reason to be cautious when it comes to the harmonisation of criminal law, not least in an area which interferes with the constitutional sphere. sweden has a generous attitude towards freedom of speech, freedom of the press and freedom of religion. parts of europe do not, on the basis of the facts. the council decision, which was negotiated with great difficulty by the german presidency, guarantees a minimum level of protection. the roure report wanted to go further and contained a number of very vague phrases. that is why i voted against the report in the end. of course, freedom of speech cannot be absolute. however, this does not mean that all restrictions must be particularly well justified.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report because we believe that it is important from the point of view of legal certainty and in the light of the developments in both trade and marketing that we have modern common rules for the choice of law in contractual obligations. however, we are not entirely satisfied with the compromise reached and have therefore voted against paragraph 2 of amendment no 77 concerning consumer contracts. we believe that europe needs stronger consumer protection with clear rules for both traders and consumers. the compromise does not, in our view, resolve this satisfactorily. secure, rights-aware and confident consumers buying across borders are essential for europe's economy and prosperity. however, we share the concern expressed by small businesses about the burden placed on e-commerce operators in europe in terms of acquiring knowledge of the different consumer laws in the member states. this is an important question that remains to be resolved.
in writing. we reject further subsidies to political parties at eu level. democracy requires that political parties be built from the bottom up by citizens forming opinions, formulating political programmes, starting parties and winning support in general elections. allowing the political and technocratic establishment to create eu parties from the top down using taxpayers' money is a democratically repugnant idea. the result is that parties become independent of their members but dependent on a political elite. it is therefore totally wrong to increase subsidies to eu parties by providing funds to create political foundations at european level. eu parties are already costing taxpayers over €10 million in the 2007 eu budget, and it is likely that political foundations will receive equal financial contributions. this means that in future the eu taxpayers will have to pay around sek 190 million per year to the eu parties and their affiliated organisations. the funds go to eu parties which are mere brussels desk products, parties which the people have never asked for either as citizens or as taxpayers. it is also morally questionable that the politicians in this house should be so reckless in allocating tax funds to their own organisations while cutting public expenditure in the member states.
in writing. all eu institutions are already bound by international law guaranteeing the protection of human rights. this is clearly shown by the judgment of the court of justice in racke. in addition, all eu countries have ratified the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. the convention is binding on all states and is fulfilling its function well. on the contrary, a new text would risk legal uncertainty and the adoption by the court of justice of the european communities in luxembourg of decisions contrary to those already adopted by the european court of human rights in strasbourg. we do not agree with the fact that the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, as it is set out in the annex to this report, is given a legally binding character. we believe that it is wrong to give the president the mandate to solemnly proclaim the charter before the new treaty is signed, and we oppose any attempt to publish it in the official journal of the european union before the ratification procedure is successfully completed, if it is ever completed. we call on the member states to open negotiations for the eu's accession to the convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, so that the eu institutions are also covered by these common human rights.
in writing. we swedish and danish social democrats voted in favour of amendment no 41 in the report. there are problems in the labour market with precarious employment and wages that are not sustainable. this has to be corrected. in some countries this is done through the legal minimum wage. in other countries, these issues are resolved through negotiations between the social partners. in the nordic countries, we have chosen the latter model. we swedish and danish social democrats are in favour of continuing to use our collective agreement model to ensure that employees receive decent wages. as the amendment refers to the objective being implemented through member states' national systems, we consider this to be consistent with the collective agreement model we have chosen to use.
in writing. the respective labour market policies of the member states shall be determined at national level through a broad and democratically-based debate based on research and national experience. the respective labour market policies of the eu countries are to be developed in the context of institutional competition. the fact that we are now fortunately able to discuss the danish flexicurity model is due to the fact that the eu did not introduce a common labour market policy, say, 25 years ago. if that had been the case, some states on the continent, led by germany, would have implemented eu legislation that would have had devastating consequences for employment and growth in europe and would have been harsh if not impossible to change. the new member states would have been forced to adopt this policy as part of the acquis communautaire and thus be forced to start under unreasonable conditions. it is because there is no common policy in this area that we can now compare flexicurity, the nordic model, the anglo-saxon model and the continental model. it's thanks to this institutional competition to find good solutions that we have in the minds of existing solutions to compare. on the basis of the above arguments, we voted against the final vote and against all the amendments which contain comments on the form of labour market policy to be pursued by the member states.
in writing. unfortunately, the concept of flexicurity has now become so firmly entrenched in the debate that it is impossible to avoid it. instead, the battle is over whose definition should prevail. simplified, this means that the conservatives want to emphasise flexibility and the socialists want to emphasise security. fortunately, the report on flexicurity which we voted on today in the european parliament uses the former definition. that is why i was able to support the report, despite a number of less good papers. the removal of the rigidity which prevents young and older workers in particular from entering the labour market must be given top priority if we are to overcome exclusion!
in writing. the first paragraph (a) itself states that there is no legal basis for a european tourism policy. the report then goes on to speak of the important role of tourism in social integration and the importance of increased cooperation to facilitate tourists. it is very much about collecting statistics and joint actions for quality assurance and consumer protection. the importance of preserving, protecting and restoring europe's cultural heritage is also emphasised. calls on the commission to draw up guidelines for the member states to achieve better policy coordination in the development of tourism at national, regional and local level; the european parliament also wants to 'culturally mark' europe's cultural heritage in order to increase support for those parts it considers it wants to preserve (european cultural routes and monuments are mentioned as examples). tourism policy is, in my view and under existing legislation, a national concern. the tourist industry must be developed in competition with the tourist activities of other countries, and each country must be given the opportunity to formulate its own policy. i have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. we moderates have today succeeded in persuading the european parliament to vote against the introduction of special duties on goods from countries which have not signed the kyoto protocol. we also convinced the european parliament not to support the description of the market economy as the cause of the world's environmental problems. we have therefore voted in favour of the report on trade and climate change. trade is not in itself a threat to the environment - quite the contrary. trade is creating the wealth we need to meet our future environmental challenges. it allows us to use scarce resources more efficiently and to lift people out of poverty, which is the root of most of the world's environmental problems. openness and trade are also the prerequisites for technology transfer, which means that developing countries can today get a better environment faster than we got. transport that does not bear its environmental costs is a major problem. we moderates, however, believe that the position adopted by parliament in today's vote on several proposals to combat and restrict trade is wrong. parliament should instead focus on forcing transport to bear its environmental costs. the concern we all have for the environment must not be used as an excuse for protectionism, because protectionism is a threat to development and therefore a threat to a healthy environment.
in writing. the junilist believes that the eu has an important role to play in the fight to reduce the impact of humanity on the earth's climate. as one of the largest players in world trade, the eu is thus able to influence the environmental impact of production and international transport. however, there are major risks associated with this report. it is clear from the text and from the context that a policy based on these considerations will open the door to the protectionist tendencies that many interest groups and countries in the eu still support. the special interests always try to act as the representatives of the general interest. supporting this report is therefore very risky. in the long run, we could have increased protectionism and thus high costs for the world's poor and for eu consumers. instead, the eu should continue on its path as an example and ally for all countries that want to take responsibility for climate change in the world. the junilista has therefore voted against this report.
in writing. lipietz report on trade and climate change touches on two of the areas where the eu has the most power to influence internationally. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we in the european parliament take a stand on this matter. all the countries of the world need to participate in the international economy through trade. only with trade and economic development can there be a real opportunity to lift people out of poverty and to affect the environment. europe's commitment to the environment and development must continue to be a catalyst for a sustainable economy based on trade, not a barrier to it. i therefore regret that the report voted on today took a line which is not entirely in favour of trade and development. i voted yes, however, with great hesitation, as the most problematic phrases were removed.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking marie-noëlle lienemann for a good framework directive. as a swede living in stockholm on the baltic sea, one of the most polluted seas in the world, i really welcome this directive. forty per cent of sweden's population lives less than five kilometres from the coastline and tourism provides 71,000 jobs in our country, including on our islands. in addition, the fisheries sector provides 4 000 jobs. thanks to the fishing industry, we have the opportunity to eat the fish that is so important to us swedes. a damaged marine environment leads to a reduced quality of life for many people and is not a desirable development. there is a risk that commercial and recreational fishing by boat and by sea and recreational fishing could be lost if we in the eu do not act together, here and now. a common maritime policy is designed to protect and strengthen marine ecosystems and to establish databases of control and knowledge. we in the european parliament have higher ambitions than the commission and i am glad that we have set the target of achieving a good environment by 2017 and not by 2021. i am also pleased that member states will be presenting action programmes for marine environments as early as 2015. thank you commissioner franco frattini for joining us now! as a concerned citizen, i would like to move faster, but if all 27 countries are moving, we can do something and start now. this is one of the best examples of the need for crossborder cooperation. i hope that the baltic sea can become a pilot area, because eight of the nine countries around the baltic sea are members of the eu. we own both the question and the responsibility. it is also important that we who are involved in external environmental policy cooperate with russia on the baltic sea and the marine environment. we have to make decisions on both environmental and energy policy. marine environment in the center now!
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it is, of course, totally unacceptable that children should be exposed to toys that pose a risk to their health. in fact, almost everyone thinks that buying ce marked toys in the eu is safe. anything else is unthinkable. but in reality, we buy toys that contain carcinogens and lead against our will. the very idea of these substances being used in toys that children often put in their mouths is unlikely! the safety of children requires immediate action by the commission. the profit interests of manufacturers, suppliers and importers must never take precedence over the health of children. one dangerous toy is one too many!
wine is a drink with ancient traditions, a drink that belongs to european culture. in vino veritas, as the ancient romans noted. but wine is also an alcoholic beverage that is harmful to public health, especially if consumed in abundance. wine is an important source of income in several eu countries and provides jobs and vitality to rural areas. i think wine is a drink that should largely sell itself if it is of good quality. the report's proposals for large and increased marketing efforts are therefore difficult to understand. it cannot be the task of the european taxpayer to bear the cost of allowing european wine growers to sell products that have been out-competed on market terms. on the one hand, the eu is talking about drinking in moderation, on the other hand, the eu wants to sell more wine. this doesn't make sense. the eu wine market certainly needs reform, but let us do it wisely. wine markets look and function differently by tradition, and eu citizens have different requirements and preferences.
in writing. i voted against the report because it concerns the provisions of the common agricultural policy which we want to abolish. the cap needs to be fundamentally reformed and we have been saying for a couple of years now that this debate must start as soon as possible. we believe that the eu should start cutting agricultural aid as early as 2010, after the so-called health check of the long-term budget 2007-2013. the commission has also proposed that the council should be able to adopt a decision on the matter by the end of the year.
in writing. we voted in favour of the commission's proposal for a directive. we believe that there is a need for common rules for direct aid schemes and that it is positive that we are moving away from a system where aid is linked to production and instead moving towards a system where, for example, rural development, open landscapes and quality are at the centre. we support the commission's proposal for simplifications. a simplified procedure is one way of finding acceptance by the farmers themselves. unlike the committee, we believe that controls are important to legitimize the system in the eyes of the eu taxpayer. however, the checks must be proportionate to their intended purpose. the rules should be designed in such a way that they do not have to lead to misunderstandings.
in writing. the amendments tabled by the committee on agriculture to the commission's proposal are both good and very bad. junilista agrees that it is too difficult for individual farmers to understand the content of the various eu directives and regulations in the agricultural field today. simplification is absolutely necessary. but i am opposed to the committee on agriculture's proposal for a directive that unannounced checks are not justified in the system because they would contribute to 'a disproportionate but not unjustified fear'. there must be effective control systems in place when public funds are paid to individuals. we cannot ignore this, which the committee on agriculture of the european parliament for some reason wishes to do. i have therefore voted against the european parliament's resolution on this matter.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to express my appreciation and gratitude to the honourable members and rapporteurs kyösti virrankoski and ville itälä. they have done a very competent and responsible job and have thus also been able to keep the budget down to a level lower than i thought possible from the beginning. but of course they have been forced to work within the limits that were set for them and i would remind you that those limits are unreasonable. first, the revenue side is largely given. about 1% of the gross national income is to go to the eu. but that's not how it's supposed to work under normal circumstances! we should first think about what the eu is going to do, then see how much it will cost and then finally take in that money. you don't take money in first and wonder what to throw it on. it's a total false start and everyone knows it. so let's change that! secondly, we know that at least 75 per cent of the money that the eu spends is spent on completely useless and harmful purposes. so we have an enormous amount of money to spend on useful things. in practice, more than half of this money still goes to agricultural policy and another quarter to a third to what are known as structural funds on the structural side. the eu should not be doing any of this. the eu should develop and monitor regulatory systems for the internal market and for environmental cooperation. this is surprisingly inexpensive. if we move to this in the future, we will be able to reduce the eu fee and still have money for other things that the eu should be doing. in my view, galileo is now being funded. it's a success. we should invest heavily in basic research because then we have an opportunity to develop fusion energy. this is the great task of the eu, not the means to fight forest fires, to adapt structures to globalisation and other propaganda money.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. this report brings to mind alice in wonderland, and madam president, it is irresistible to challenge the interpreters by exclaiming 'curiouser and curiouser!' cried alice. what are we doing? is it really reasonable for the eu to use european taxpayers' money in advertising campaigns to convince the same citizens to buy the goods they have already subsidised? of course not. i'm not going to. one of the eu's objectives in the common agricultural policy is to guarantee fair prices for consumers. but when this is done through taxation rather than through productivity growth, consumers pay the same amount for food anyway, just in a different way. but in addition, the incentives to increase productivity will be diminished. so food is going to be more expensive in the long run because of this agricultural policy. the committee is in favour of restrictions on competition. the report also calls for the introduction of a european system of taxation of goods and services, which would be more effective and more efficient. there is a stench of hidden protectionism about the whole proposal! we have many examples of eu advertising campaigns in the past: in 1997, advertising campaigns for eu-produced cut flowers were launched as imports became cheaper thanks to the abolition of customs duties. the prominent swedish columnist johan hakelius wrote the following in a leading swedish newspaper: when eu citizens all over europe buy the same kind of flowers, of course, cohesion and european harmony grow. tulips are important for peace. in this chamber it may be necessary to point out that this was intended as irony... in 2000, the eu had 450 000 tonnes of olive oil in stock and the eu decided to invest in advertising campaigns in the nordic countries to inform us about the goodness and usefulness of olive oil. in 2007, the european parliament voted on advertising money for the dairy sector to protect this industry from further market liberalisation. the cap is in fact being reformed. it's slow, but it's making some progress. but the rapporteur's proposal takes several steps backwards. they want to replace the reforms that have finally been implemented with increased promotional measures and information campaigns. the rapporteur blames the wto and wants to increase the number of products to receive this support. this is nothing but unfair competition with agricultural producers in non-eu countries. the cap must be abolished in the long term and prices and production must be determined by supply and demand, as in all other areas. through unfortunate decisions in the past, we are trapped in an unreasonable system. all the more important that we're heading in the right direction. unfortunately, the recommendation here is that we go the wrong way. the money that can now be released for the people of europe through tariff reductions, export refunds and national aid cuts must not be wasted by increasing the budget for promotional measures and information campaigns. promotions and advertising of goods and services should be paid for by producers, not by taxpayers. europe is on the wrong track in this area, as in so many others. the eu must be a europe of freedom, where people and businesses fight for the favour of consumers, not protectionists. for the sake of europe, i wish i could feel optimistic, but unfortunately i cannot.
mr president, i shall speak first about kosovo. in the balkans and the caucasus, we see a conflict between european values and nationalist tendencies, between european integration and movements and ideas that are supported by russia. if, in the future, we look back, i am quite sure that we will never regret the efforts we can make today in order to support european integration with all the different means we have, but we might very well, in the future, regret the efforts we have not made. i think it is important to discuss that at the european council at the end of this week. i wish, secondly, to speak about globalisation. i think it is important to remember that it is globalisation that has made europe into a leading world economy and that globalisation is a necessity if we are to be the world’s leading knowledge economy, because we will never be world leaders if we are only the best in europe. we can never achieve results by protectionist measures. in the long term that will undermine the opportunities for jobs and prosperity, but it will not give us the opportunity to be a world-leading economy as well as defending european values. that leads us to the conclusion that it is important that the european council underlines all the efforts that are needed regarding energy legislation, telecom legislation, the internal market, trade and competition. competition is not a fight between different personalities or identities. it is an opportunity for all to participate and to move europe ahead. that is the way we should choose, and that is how we can use the treaty in the absolute best way to respond to the global challenge. that opportunity has been given to us this week and we should use it.
in writing. we cannot legitimise the system of using taxpayers' money to market european agricultural products in third countries. we believe that this should be done with restraint, particularly in developing countries, because it risks harming domestic industry. we believe that the eu must encourage local agriculture in these countries and not break the legs of the farmers. a functioning local production system can provide an opportunity for these countries to develop economically and a path to democratic development.
in writing. we are voting against the report and the commission's proposal for the following reasons: - we had hoped that the reform would save the union's taxpayers money and that the wine industry would be freed from funding under the common agricultural policy. in the long term, we believe that the common tax-financed subsidy of european wine production must end. we believe that it is reprehensible to use taxpayers' money to promote european wines. it is contrary to the parliament's alcohol policy which advocates a restrictive attitude towards the marketing of alcoholic beverages. in this situation, the increase in the use of market measures is the result of an unfortunate double standard. we are also against proposals to use european taxpayers' money to promote wines in third countries. we believe that the marketing of european wines should be done with caution, especially in developing countries, as it risks crowding out domestic industry. the union should not be cutting off the legs of local producers in developing countries, but instead supporting them.
in writing. the amendments to the report on which we have had to take a position in today's vote have been quite absurd. for example, the amount of sugar in different wines must be left to the consumers who buy the goods to decide for themselves. this should not be established in decision-making processes between eu institutions. producers in other parts of the world have succeeded in producing wines that appeal to european consumers while being cheaper than european wines. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. there is no doubt that excellent wines are produced in europe. the question of principle is whether we should allow poorer countries to be excluded in favour of european wine production. it is important to have a global perspective, and therefore also a public health perspective, when discussing wine production. that is not in the report. for these reasons i voted in favour of rejecting the commission's proposal and against the european parliament's report on the subject. wine producers should operate in a free market and not, as is the case today, receive substantial subsidies from the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. everyone has noted that extremism is growing within the eu. i think we should ask ourselves why. why are xenophobia and other extreme views on the rise? i am convinced that exclusion and lack of participation in society is a breeding ground for extremism and xenophobia. the equal value of all human beings is a foundation of a civilized society, and therefore we all need help in countering xenophobic forces that discriminate against people of different ethnic background, sexual orientation, gender or disability. these groups use violence, they use threats. in my own country we have even seen the murder of people who have defended human rights. we are witnessing the murder of young people of different ethnic backgrounds, simply because of their background. this will never have to happen again. we who work for the equality of all men must never be silenced. but we know that's not enough. xenophobic and extremist groups exploit young people in economic and social exclusion to create further fear, anxiety and hatred towards other groups. the fight against extremism must therefore be combined with the building of a just and united society.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking all the parties for the resolution. today, no eu country is free of right-wing extremism, not even my country, sweden. in the last election in 2006, the sweden democrats won two-thirds of the municipalities. one can suspect that their next target is the 2009 european parliament elections and the 2010 swedish parliamentary elections. we need help to make this difficult, just as other countries need help in their own countries to stop the spread of right-wing extremism spreading throughout europe. europe needs democratic parties which invite everyone, not just a few, to their party programmes. in the 2006 elections, the slogan of the swedish social democrats was 'everyone must vote', and this is even more evident in this debate, because the parties and groups we are talking about in their party programmes do not respect the fundamental values of the eu and the equal value of everyone. for me, as a member of the libe committee, the view on asylum and refugee policy is absolutely crucial. the far-right parties are opposed both to a more open europe and to developing the eu. instead, they advocate for nations with closed borders. it is a threat that i feel in sweden and collectively against all of us in the eu. i would like to make one further comment on the resolution. the propaganda that extremist groups spread to children and young people is in the form of white power music. media and communication are the tools that we use, and we do it outside of school, family, civic education and our political values. it is important that we, as the elected representatives of the people, now take the debate. we will do so from today until the european parliament elections in 2009. i welcome the resolution. member of the commission. madam president, i would first like to warmly thank the economic and monetary affairs committee and, in particular, the rapporteur, mr christian ehler, for supporting the policy set out in our communication. i fully endorse your view that legislative proposals are not appropriate at this stage. some issues can be improved through set regulation without major cost impacts and by working together with the european forum of deposit insurers (efdi). the recent financial turmoil provides evidence that maintaining deposits or confidence is crucial during a financial crisis. as to deposit guarantee schemes, two elements seem to be key – an appropriate coverage level and a short payout delay. if depositors know that their deposit will be covered and if they feel confident that the insured deposits will be reimbursed rapidly, there is no need for them to join any queue outside a bank. the existing directive has proven to be flexible and allows member states to increase coverage according to their own economic situation. member states may take immediate action if their coverage level proves to be inappropriate. the timely payout of insurer deposits can indeed be improved. according to the directive, reimbursements should normally not exceed three months, but this reflects the technology available back in 1994. this is why we have asked the efdi to identify obstacles to a rapid payout. depositors must also be made aware of the protection available to them. the existing information obligations in the directive are applied differently across europe. therefore, we have asked the efdi to identify best practices to improve the dissemination of such information to depositors. with regard to cross-border crisis, i share parliament’s view on the need to have clarity about burden-sharing and the interactions between all involved parties before such a crisis occurs. the ecofin conclusions of 9 october are clear on this point. i have noted the suggestion that the efdi participate in general burden-sharing discussions. let me underline that only very few schemes have powers which go beyond the mere reimbursement of depositors. their funds will also only cover a fraction of the amounts involved in a larger cross-border crisis. therefore, i am unable to support the suggestion to include the efdi in general burden-sharing discussions. the report also stresses the importance of eliminating possible market distortions. as requested, we will examine this matter. however, currently we do not think that the high cost of fully harmonising the present regulatory framework, estimated at between eur 2.5 billion and eur 4.5 billion, would be justified. some level-playing-field issues are already being dealt with. for instance, we need to facilitate topping up, where a branch should be able to offer a level of protection in a host country that is higher than in its home country. however, sometimes, arrangements between schemes in different member states have not worked in practice, and we support the efdi’s efforts to achieve a voluntary model agreement. some member states already adjust the contributions through their schemes according to the individual risk of banks. we would like to assist interested member states, as this would contribute towards a level playing field for banks with similar risk profiles. in conclusion, europe needs deposit guarantee schemes that ensure depositor confidence in a financial crisis. with the envisaged improvements, i am confident that we will come closer to this objective.
madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for underlining the need for studies and analyses, but also concluding that, if there are distortions on the market, something has to be done in order to achieve a level playing field. i think it is important to conclude this from this debate. if there are distortions, we can have different opinions – if they are there. but if they are there, something needs to be done. we need to do that because i think we all agree that we want more cross-border competition, and we also wish to secure the interests of the consumers. it is relevant to discuss the difference between different systems, because if you have in some member states ex-ante systems and in some member states different ex-post systems, which in reality assumes that the state might bale out banks which cannot pay their customers, then you have a distortion. it is my opinion that, due to the differences in opinion we have, there is a distortion already. the distortion is even more serious if it is also based on the assumption that the state should help banks which are not able to pay their customers. i think a good achievement with this report by mr ehler is that we have achieved that conclusion. i think it is important that the commission respond to that in future actions. we might have differences in opinion about how the state of play is today. but we do agree on the need for action to be taken if the studies to be done show that there are distortions. i would like to thank the rapporteur for that and also ask the commissioner to respond to that with action.
in writing. we support the basic principles of the eu budget for 2008 and want to emphasise that it should give citizens good value for money. the framework set by the financial perspective should be respected and we therefore welcome the fact that the budget will remain within it with a good margin. agricultural aid is one of the areas where there is scope for cuts in favour of objectives more in line with the lisbon ambitions, i.e. that the eu should develop into the world's most economically successful area.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report because it is important to remove the obstacles which currently exist to the recovery of maintenance from a citizen who is resident in a member state other than the one to which he is entitled. in this context, it is particularly important to protect the weaker party in the recovery procedure. however, we oppose the wording of amendments nos 9 and 26, which require the parties to conclude, after independent legal advice, written agreements on jurisdiction and the law of the country to be applied for this to have effect in court. such demands are incompatible with the swedish legal tradition.
writer. madam president, i would like to thank you. i have to say, it feels incredibly sad that the abuse and violence against women never seems to end. this applies both to this resolution and to the following one. i would like to thank all those who have worked to bring this resolution to fruition. it is almost inconceivable that even in 2007, women in saudi arabia are denied all human rights. it is not enough, therefore, that women should be subjected to degrading and abusive acts of gang rape. and then you're supposed to be punished by the authorities, while the perpetrators go free. i think that all of us, both women and men, in the name of solidarity, must do everything we can to ensure that human rights apply to women in saudi arabia as well. we call on the commission and the council, and of course all the member states, to raise these issues in all their contacts with saudi arabia. we have a lot of international conventions and we have statements from the eu in many contexts. sometimes it's frustrating that we talk and talk and this violence goes on and on. thank you to all those who have taken the initiative and worked on this resolution!
writer. madam president, i would like to thank you. the resolution calls for justice and reparation for the hundreds of thousands of women, known as comfort women, who were forced into sexual servitude before and during world war ii. all these humiliated women who have been held in sexual slavery are still waiting for justice and redress. the japanese authorities have certainly done a lot, but as the resolution shows, there is still a great deal for the japanese government and authorities to do to bring justice and redress to the women. the majority of the women forced into this terrible situation were very young. it means that their entire lives have been ruined by these terrible experiences. they have been forced to live their lives with physical and mental illness, isolation, shame, and often in extreme poverty. the failure to bring these women to full justice and redress also means that those who committed these crimes have been granted immunity for their actions. once again, the victims, the women, are being punished, while the perpetrators go free. this resolution is also important because we see what always happens in war and conflict zones, that it is always women who suffer the most. that is why it is important that we raise this issue. we have to try and stop this.
mr president, the negotiations in bali on climate change were a success, but we have to think further. it was a successful first step on the road to copenhagen. the participants from the european union’s different branches did a very good job and the portuguese presidency held it together in a very good way. but i would like to focus on the question marks and the problems we face, and this mainly concerns a couple of topics. firstly, the roadmap. we have to keep track of every meeting on the road to copenhagen. we have to do it in cooperation and fully realising the measures or the tools of the new treaty, and do it because there will be codecision on these issues in the future. that means other working methods and more cooperation between the commission, the council and parliament, and i look forward to this. let me now turn to the problems. we have to engage with the g77 group on forestry, the combination of technology transfer and the fight against poverty and climate change. this could be better prepared than in bali, and it is absolutely necessary to do this. i have here in my hands the full commission lisbon strategic package. it is not only the lisbon treaty but also the lisbon process that we have to merge together and with the work of climate change. i think there is still much to prove if we are to talk the talk and walk the walk and fulfil the promises europe made in bali.
